# Chapter Summaries

Compact, one-to-three-line summaries for the next batch. Written in the order chapters are read. Files: `chapters/volume-01/chapter-0001.md` … `chapter-0020.md`.

# Volume 01 — The Street That Wasn't There

## Volume 01, Batch 0001 (Chapters 1–10)

### Chapter 1 — The Extra Stop

Civic Atlas Office route room, Alder Quay, late afternoon of the 4th of Sazur. Milo Rook signs the routine Line 7 detour correction and finds the arithmetic impossible: the Bellwether Lane entry at 4:17 carries a forty-one-second dwell on a segment with no slack, so the tram must run late or overspeed. The route reader shows no stop; the district index has no name; the tram authority's platform register lists *Bellwether Lane, platform withdrawn* six years back. Supervisor Dilley Farr wants the entry struck as a printing error and offers a compromise: file it disputed, not deleted. Milo keeps a private copy and finds in its margin a bracket-and-dot survey mark that was not drawn on this sheet at all: a damp halo shows the ink arrived already wet, and because it bridges the printer's fold instead of sitting in it, the mark was pressed onto a shut, damped sheet. The press is Tuesday or later and dates nothing; the ink is the clue, and the ink is older than the paper because the mark was never made for this print run — it came off something else. Jo Lask, Line 7 conductor, comes up two flights to ask whether the stop is still physically there, and reports that the nameboard's screws are still in the coping. At 4:17 the platform speaker announces "Bellwether Lane" on a circuit cut six years, from a platform with no lamps. **Ends:** Milo leaves for the embankment with chain and coat; the platform stays dark and silent. **New:** the recurring annotating hand (first instance, unresolved).

### Chapter 2 — The Road After the Bell

Old embankment, disused Alder Quay platform, edge of Cedar Court, dusk. Milo finds the hum is not the tram rail but a live line under the ground, pulsing at a period of sixty-nine that sometimes drops to sixty-two. Courier Pell Harrow appears with a satchel and chalk, and offers a bypass he will not use himself when his own warning mark ("a light's out past the second one") is showing. Mara Quill, Continuity Records, intercepts Milo with a Registry badge: the disputed stop reactivated a six-year-old housing case inside a project designation. She reads the closure notice aloud — *one loss recorded, persons to be withdrawn from the interval affected between the second and the third calls* — and Cedar Court's reading of it (a count) is not stupidity. Pell says the bell is "not what the notice calls it" and admits he does not know what it is. **Ends:** an empty maintenance tram slows at the disused platform and passes without opening a door; Pell says the route is not missing a destination, it is missing a corner. Something sounds once under the rails.

### Chapter 3 — The Mark on the Empty Platform

Night. A controlled test with three agreed conditions: no crossing, Pell may stop it at any time, and the field book stays shut unless there is a reason. Ordinary instruments fail without drama: the chalk crosses read two ways, the compass is useless, and the eye keeps finding the drain, then a section of works hoarding, then four inches of dark at the hoarding foot. Milo writes what the chain says versus what the eye says. The Field Book leaves its first Atlas residue: **"A return mark is missing."** He records its limits — it names no person, no bell, no owner. Pell chalks a return mark with a bar inside a circle; Milo tests it and it satisfies nothing, because the line did not ask for a mark made tonight. Milo's brass Lark token yields only his mother's remembered tram instruction, not a route, and he writes down that resemblance is not evidence. **Ends:** the third bell sounds beneath the tracks and eight feet of solid floodgate wall is no longer solid — a lit narrow lane, and a woman in a blue work coat striking a door three times, waiting to be told which way to move.

### Chapter 4 — The Lane Between Stations

Late night. The first cost-bearing crossing. The lane repeats a set of doors numbered 1 to 9 and back; turning early does not send Milo backwards but fails to move him at all. The lane opens a service court with a pump shed, a washing line, and three doors — and a position in front of the third that the route wants occupied. Milo goes into the court and sits down instead of taking it, and the lane holds. He calls out to Pell, who answers with a bicycle bell and a spoken count, and Milo can get out only by yielding rather than filling and following the outside voice. He returns with a brittle scrap of printed route paper showing a stop marked with a small unexplained square. **Ends:** Ada Fenn, at the edge of Cedar Court at 1:20 a.m., says he came through on the wrong bell and asks whether he is here to take the lane away again. **Cost:** the orientation blank begins — he cannot find the gate, the road, or a corridor he has used for six years until Pell chalks it. The written route home is intact in the Field Book.

### Chapter 5 — The Turn Nobody Fills

Cedar Court kitchen, after midnight. Mara produces the notice that has governed the block for six years. Ada Fenn refuses to speak for the Registry or become proof, and puts the real question back: what route do you need, not what route was there. Three incompatible readings of the third turn are voiced by people with real memories — Ada (a pause to check the lane is clear), Jo Lask (a handover: the count passes door-to-front and the tram will not move until it has been handed, so it belongs to whoever is at the door), Lena Marr (a count of who is still on the street) — and the notice supplies a fourth, a count of the dead. Tomas holds a works shift interval and the foreman walking the reach. Milo cannot separate them and says so rather than offering a convenient sentence. He asks Ada to show the door-check: standing at the bottom, counting your own floors, called and called back, and if they cannot answer you go up. Ada then says the current version is a substitute — "the door-check with the doors gone," because the doors are four storeys up now — and refuses to pretend the two are the same thing in front of the woman with the list. He catches himself thinking of Ada as a *witness* — a condition his office could use — and writes *do not use a person as a condition* on the back of his hand. Ada interrupts him before he can name the hand, and warns that she was asked a question by a polite young man from Crown Heights nine years ago, and that eleven months later a sentence from her own kitchen turned up in a document in her own kitchen; she has already been a document once. **No name is spoken on the page.** **Ends:** Mara announces that Orrin Sable has got a **variation listed for first light** — not the hearing, which is the 14th — and a floodgate crew will throttle the western culvert to a maintenance slit at sunrise. Milo must decide whether to put his measurements in a legal record.

### Chapter 6 — A Name on the Return

**POV: Mara Quill.** Hall of Wards and the records alcove, Crown Heights, pre-dawn of the 5th. The dawn proceeding is an **application to vary an order in force at the Directions Bench** — not the hearing, which sits on the 14th — and that distinction is the scene's spine; Orrin leans on the 14th and Mara makes him separate the two facts in front of the clerk. Mara's own block capitals are on the intake routing slip from six years ago; she ticked the annex box, which meant she saw that an address was affected and passed it on, and she has spent the night learning what that cost. Orrin Sable states his position without asking to be excused for it: a standing instruction from the Prefect's office, a contractor's bond lapsing on the 11th of Sazur, and a hazard that is real. He offers a supervised survey on condition that residents sign a waiver and Milo surrender his notes. Mara refuses and gets, instead, a finding authorised *for the protection of persons in temporary accommodation* — the reason, not the authority — and a one-shift emergency inspection at the old service court on the ninth, with five named residents and the surveyor of record. In the alcove she finds the file is nineteen pages when the report is eleven: a replacement page, filed eleven days late, that removes the line "*no evidence was found of any hazard to persons at the service court*". The compensation ledger holds one line — *Venn, J. — pump crew, 2nd shift — relocation due, pending* — with no cause and no next of kin. She compares the works-perimeter annex notation to Milo's enlargement of the timetable mark and writes *consistent with*, underlined, and refuses to call it an identification. In the corridor Milo cannot find the hearing room; she makes him read his whole book aloud from half past five until six and correct her four times. His sworn disclosure puts the crossing **about sixty metres from the disused platform end, on the west face of the hoarding** — the same point as his Chapter 3 Field Book note — and his statement that he came out at a different point on the same alignment is what the record will turn on. **Ends:** Orrin confirms the gate goes to eighteen inches of sill at sunrise, not a seal, and the service court — where Lena Marr and Owen Fitch have been standing since half past four — is about to be pointed at the old alignment.

### Chapter 7 — The Closure Crew

Sunrise of the 6th, Floodgate Four, western culvert, Line 7 embankment. The order throttles the gate to eighteen inches and forbids a seal, because a seal would back water into the works zone and the low streets — so a lawful, careful compromise points the district's water at the old alignment. Tomas Rusk identifies the blow-off sound and warns that a dead main with a valve on it is a plug in a plug, and that the water will find the lowest thing left. Mara invokes at ten to seven the same protective finding the Directions Bench wrote into the 5th's variation, against its date, which is what puts her under review. Milo halts a tram with a lantern. **Pell chalks the walk line** and holds the road with Rue and Idar. Milo writes the threshold, turn and return marks into the book himself, specifying two knots tied by two different men and *held by Jo Lask* chalked beside them, and then **calls the interval out loud himself from the arch foot for nine minutes**, from seven o'clock to nine minutes past seven, until his voice gives out on Danner's come-along. Six people in three trades pace themselves off his count and it holds. When Jo is trapped, **Milo hands the call to Pell** on the tram side because Pell is the only one who can count, and refuses to frame it as a reward. Danner winds the gate down by hand to eighteen inches. The diverted head of water crosses the old alignment and separates the service court from the works side; Jo Lask walks backwards into the rails, the alignment folds, and the water in the lane goes round in a loop with him in it. **Ends:** Milo stops Pell from going in, sends him up to call the interval, and stands on the arch stone with a rope in both hands, having no other part to play.

### Chapter 8 — The Narrow Return

Late morning of the 6th, then the clinic. Inside the loop, Milo refuses to give Jo a route and asks what he wants. Jo wants to be the man who counts — the one thing he has ever been good at — and Milo refuses to strip it from him. Outside, **Pell Harrow calls the interval from the tram side with the lamp and a bicycle bell** and Rue reports that Pell went up with the lamp at seven and is most of the hour through on the count he promised — **which is the chapter's clock, and puts the rescue at ten past eight, the hour and the crossing coinciding**; Pell stops the moment Jo is out and refuses any credit for it; Jo hears his own service call coming down through the embankment. Ada's door-check does *not* give the rule — Ada explicitly refuses to interpret the third bell, and the "no new turn" insight is Milo's alone, discovered inside the circle and said aloud for the first time to a man thirty feet away. Milo enters, walks the circle, refuses the third doorway *while it is still open*, and shouts Jo's name. Jo stops the loop on his own, and counts himself out over 219 paces. Lena Marr reads her mother's list aloud from the landing; Owen Fitch refuses to be counted and is not asked twice. Dr Iona Sen treats the hand and states three things: it is not a sprain or a headache or tiredness; the cost arrives as the inability to find rooms; and when a man is holding the pattern, the thing that ends well is when he is allowed to put it down. She also corrects his own arithmetic: he has been **across twice** — the lane at half past twelve and again at ten past eight on the 6th — and the embankment at eleven and the arch at twenty to eight were sightings, not crossings, however much he wanted them to be. **Ends:** Tomas Rusk offers his pump-house ledger — the bell schedule matches the works bell and the third call is a works shift interval, at ten to two on the night the lane closed — but withholds the page until someone explains why the road shut in the middle of a run. The amended Line 7 sheet goes to the wire tray at 11:40 with the Bellwether entry removed.

### Chapter 9 — The Public Line

The ninth of Sazur, late afternoon, at the inspection point above the service court — a yard of roughly eighty people. Milo refuses the carbon copy's unplaceable destination when Mara offers it as the demonstration's route: a name on a public sheet is a claim, and the claim would be his. The one-shift passage is deliberately small — a chalk line, a trestle table, a real service door, Jo Lask at the tram calling the calls, Pell and four Streetkeepers moving the return mark on a timer, Ada describing only the door-check she is willing to share, and the marshal keeping the tally. When Idar points out that five Streetkeeper cuts — the ways out of the court, including his own, the only one a woman with a pram can use before seven — have been chalked into the fourth column of the official inspection sheet, Milo strikes all five out on his own motion and heads the sheet *no household routes are recorded in this document, and no person has consented to one*, because a man with no authority has no business publishing a route that belongs to somebody else. Lena Marr chooses to be walked out and named in the report. Owen Fitch refuses to be entered anywhere and walks home the ordinary road in front of eighty witnesses, costing the report its address; Mara tells him the price honestly and he accepts it. **The beat that changed:** Mara's reassurance that there is no list is not true as written. She has been carrying his initials on the Registry's 5th-of-Sazur inspection instrument since dawn on the 5th — she named him herself, because the protective finding required persons in temporary accommodation and there was no other wording that got him into that yard. She produces the folded page in the open, tells him she cannot take him off it, and says the refusal line will now cost him the address *and* the initials. Owen's answer is that his refusal must be written against the initials he is already on, so a reader in twenty years can see he knew and said no anyway. He walks home up the embankment ramp. **At five minutes to six** the ground pushes and the service door sounds like a stop being announced. Milo opens the Field Book and it is silent, and he takes more from the silence than from the pressure. He has the chalk in his hand, does not draw a new turn, and the danger is not his own temptation but that somebody in the yard will *answer* — he shouts at Danner and the contractors, two of whom already have a hand up; Jo closes the doors; the pressure lets go. The demonstration occupies the site all shift, so Danner's seal crew has no window and writes *no window* on the form twice. **Ends:** Milo signs a report stating that the order's account is incomplete, records that he has crossed **twice, on the fourth of Sazur and again on the sixth**, and disclaims any authority to reopen anything. While the yard empties, Mara turns the carbon copy that has to go into the file and stops dead: *there's writing on this.* The two unsigned margin questions are **not read in this chapter** — the reading is Chapter 10's.

### Chapter 10 — The Temporary Stay

The tenth, the eleventh to the thirteenth, then the fourteenth. Iona Sen makes Milo recite the route to the office he has just described and states the rule: this week you ask somebody to take you where you're going, every time. The Registry demands the Field Book on the ground that it was created in the course of official duties; Milo refuses in writing and offers an extracted copy of the relevant entries instead, watched by Dilley Farr. **Orrin Sable intercepts him in the corridor and puts the antagonist's case to him — the map did not make the seam coherent, it made it legible, and a believed route is a used route that will be standing in the way of the river on the twenty-second — and Milo writes the argument down in the other man's words without editing it.** Mara, under review and barred from the route room, tells him the price of the filing before she makes it — a temporary stay until the timetable review on the twenty-eighth, which dies if the hearing on the fourteenth goes against her. It is four days to that hearing, not a fortnight. Milo drafts the application himself. Cedar Court reads the eleven inches of paper that is a plan of a handrail: **four consented names, each with a line saying what they agreed to and when they may stop**, then Lena Marr's tenancy named in full with *asking to be written down, not consenting to a restoration*, and a fifth entry that is not a name at all — *one household has declined to be entered*, no name, address or initials. A deliberately empty right-hand third is headed *for people who are not here yet*. Owen works the fifth line out for himself from a fourth-floor landing — it is the only place in the sheet where he can be — and then the scene turns on what the plan does not have in it. **Milo has left the door-check off, and Ada is the door-check, and she is ill, and she will not ask.** He asks Owen to take the third floor off her; Owen agrees once a night, going up, not the ground floor, stopping when he likes, unwritten, on condition that Milo tell Ada herself and not the yard because a rota that goes round the yard would stop her doing it altogether. Owen also refuses the plan being called clean: his initials have been on the Registry's fifth-of-Sazur inspection instrument since Mara named him, and Milo's own hand says so. Nothing in the scene costs Milo his mother, his token, or his district, and nothing is disclosed to a man met three days earlier. His line about the empty third — *it's the only part of the sheet I could put a straight line under* — is the closest the batch comes to saying what he is for. Pell chalks the bollard on Ferry Road, says he has been Milo's chalk twice and will not be a third time, and leaves for the south with a lead about his brother Tern. The three days between are dramatised in the Ferry Road pie shop, where a woman Milo cannot name walks him round the corner and tells him not to answer her polite. On the thirteenth, the day before the hearing, Milo and Mara examine the carbon copy in the alcove: the destination can be dated out of use but not placed on any current map, and the two margin questions are read aloud for the first time and are in different leads, so at least one author is not the annotating hand. **Ends:** the floodgate test is set for the 22nd of Sazur and will drive the eighteen-inch gate out to full flow; Milo's written route home is complete, correct, and unable to tell him which way to turn, so the next batch starts with a request for help.

## Volume 01, Batch 0002 (Chapters 11–20)

### Chapter 11 — What the Hearing Kept

The hearing of the fourteenth of Sazur sits from eleven and finishes at twenty to two, and it goes against Mara. The continuity order is upheld in full, the culvert finding is upheld, and the twenty-second's test proceeds. Four things come out of it. **The stay survives** to the timetable review on the twenty-eighth and is read out clause by clause so its size is exact. **The flood levy is amended** — the contractor's bond lapsed on the eleventh, so eleven per cent on the rateable and a per-head schedule on temporary accommodation, calculated on two hundred and thirty-one non-rateable people; Mara objects in one sentence, is told the Bench cannot hear a rates schedule, and says she will take it to a rates authority. **The Registry is directed to produce the complete file within seven days**, on Orrin's own offer, invoked against him, with the Bench minuting that the fifth was a variation, the ninth an inspection, and this the hearing. And **the inspection sheet's refusal line is read into the record** with O. Fitch's initials already against it, because a clerk asked how to index a household with no name. **Ends:** Milo walked to the works office by Ada Fenn's judgement and not her favour, and a Recovery clerk tells him the block has no representative because the name the register holds for it is asleep on the third floor.

### Chapter 12 — Three Doors

The appeal needs the water clinic, the Board school and the works-office counter, and Lena Marr organises the walk on the back of a Recovery form. At the clinic, Hettie Brann reads the old address and explains the postal queue: a letter to a closed street goes to Crown Heights, comes back stamped *no registered location*, and kills the appointment. Milo times both routes and finds that the practical walk past the second hatch is the seam, and the legal one is forty minutes on the embankment. At the school, nine children cannot be enrolled on a tenancy that the closure turned into a claim, and the alternative — one Continuity referral with one household name at the top — **is a roll**, which is the instrument the order was written against, and Owen Fitch says so before Lena does. At the counter, Lena does the arithmetic and finds the trap before anyone explains it: **the relocation payment is assessed on displacement from a named place, so the name must stay on the compensation schedule and off the street index.** An address ends the money. **Ends:** Owen refuses to be entered, Ada tells the yard to stop offering her stairwell, and Mara says the register's head of household for that block is the oldest man in it and he is asleep.

### Chapter 13 — The Route Card

Milo tries to write instructions and produces a document that is flawless and unusable, because he has written it for somebody who stands at a door and knows it is the main door. Iona Sen tests it: Ola Petrossian, a nurse who has never been past the tram steps, walks it, cannot find the door, and stands in front of a fence for eleven minutes wanting a cut she is told she may not use. Lena Marr then reads the second version, which works, and corrects its worst sentence — *the lamp has been out a fortnight and you are going to use this path anyway* — because a card that tells a frightened person to walk past a dark lamp has put the Registry's hand on it. **The training is real: directions in the reader's units, not the surveyor's.** The examination is real too: three timed runs of the clinic's back stair, a signed set of conditions (a named person outside holding a count, stated minutes, walked in and walked out by someone who can stop the work, report the morning after), and a clinic card that will be needed weeks before he needs it. **Ends:** Ada refuses him a wall — nothing goes on the third-floor wall, and a sentence she said in her own kitchen turned up in a document in her own kitchen — and sends him to the courier round instead.

### Chapter 14 — The Round Without Pell

Rue shows Milo the round: nine stops, a folded oilskin sheet, no address column and no copy, because nobody has ever asked it to stop. The slot in the works hoarding is full of post — Pell has cleared it since he was fourteen and Pell is south — and a child's school pack has been in the slot nine days and is two days past the enrolment date in it. The conditions are exact: the cuts are not to be drawn or written, the dead lamp past the second hatch is not to go in a report or in a conversation with a marshaller, and he is not to say it in the yard. Then the drainage. The district record says the low streets drain west along a 1904 culvert checked sound; Rue says the drain opposite the old painted wall comes up in a dry week and has for eleven years. **Milo sounds it: four feet brick, seven feet brick, eleven feet open with grey water and a culvert smell.** The record is a lead lateral the size of a thumb and the real culvert under Bridge Street is the works' dead, valved storm main. **Ends:** in the chandler's yard, with Milo locked in the store for twenty-two minutes, Rue's round writes a door-check rota on accountancy paper — *anyone can cross their own name off, nobody else crosses it for them* — and Owen Fitch writes his own name on the third-floor line and, in his own hand beside it, *not asked to*.

### Chapter 15 — The Long Sheet

Nineteen inches by eleven, drawn over three nights, and beautiful. The witness mark is the third-floor stairwell, arrived at rather than chosen; the datum is Jo Lask's live interval, taken from a column that says *not a standing duty*. Neither man was asked. At one o'clock on the second night Milo writes that a route needs a count, works out that he cannot get one without admitting what he is doing, and draws for two more hours. **The sheet is false, because it has no count in it.** He walks it with Rue and it works; he does not tell Ilse Oller what it is and takes her up the second floor, and they come out on a fourth-floor landing that has no fourth floor, and the lane behind it is the one from the fourth of Sazur. **The brass token is cold through all of it**, which he notes and does not interpret. Ada Fenn spends an afternoon talking Ilse out by the door-check. Owen works out that his nightly labour has been turned into a fixed point, and Jo learns his interval was used as a ruler, and Milo writes it all down, on a page and a half, in his own hand, with his name at the foot, and files the sheet **as false**. **Ends:** he tells Mara Quill the token exists, that Lark Street is his mother's district and that she was its conductor, and that he needs her to be able to disqualify him — and when she offers to file the disclosure he refuses, and she writes the refusal down with the date on it and makes him sign it.

### Chapter 16 — Between the Second and the Third

Mara's disclosure, and it is not the one he made. The intake routing slip's annex box has **two sets of initials**: hers, and one above it in a different pen, dated after hers, which she has read four times at night and cannot bring herself to read aloud. She has been doing this case off her own hours in a records alcove for six years, she has not told the review panel, and she says the reason is that procedure is the armour and she is good at it. Then **Verrick Assal is asked**, for the first time in six years, in the works compound. He was off shift on the relief and came out at ten past one for his coat; he got to the door, said *clear*, and when he came back the door was shut, and he cannot produce one thing that happened between the door and the frontage, and he does not know what the third bell was for. **Ends:** Milo writes three families on a table leg — an evacuation, a count of the dead, a later reuse of an older route — and puts the five Cedar Court accounts under them, and Lena Marr tells him in front of twenty-two people that the boxes are his, made with a pencil, because a surveyor makes shapes, and that all five accounts are wrong and none of them is lying.

### Chapter 17 — Say It to Them

Milo reads **Orrin Sable's argument out of his own notebook, twice** — once in the works office at ten in the morning, once in a yard of thirty-one people at four in the afternoon — because the first is a rumour and the second is a fact, and he promised to say it before the twenty-second and not after. Danner independently arrives at the same mechanics from a stop log in a nineteen-something drawing, and Milo follows with the rod: eleven feet of open ground and a lead lateral where a 2ft 6in culvert is on the record, so the twenty-second is going to put a river through a pipe the size of his thumb under two hundred and thirty-one people who are not rateable. **Owen's objection is that Milo has been the man nobody can be angry with for three weeks and now wants the yard to vote**, which is the same move in a different coat. The drill is written on the table leg, ground floor first, with Owen's clause that anybody may change their mind out loud and everybody stops. Ada asks for the fifth floor out; Owen takes the fourth with it and tells the yard not to thank him. **Ends:** Tomas shows Milo the maintenance ledger number on the first page of his wallet-file, and it is the number the withdrawn replacement page cited, and the book has two things in it — the bell schedule and the second-shift roll — and Jonas Venn's line on the roll is not empty.

### Chapter 18 — Ground Floor First

Seven in the morning: Tomas hands over the book in his own front room with Mara writing verbatim, and does not say the name out loud on a set of steps. Two in the afternoon: Danner's rod goes in through the personnel door and finds fourteen feet of clay where the drawing says, and the wall holds, **and it changes nothing**, because the door stands eleven inches over the low point of the old alignment and the gauge has to go through it on the morning of the test, and the plate in the stores cannot go on without a variation. Eight at night: the round does not run, the count is Rue's from the second hatch, and the ground floor comes out in eleven minutes. At nine minutes past nine the service door makes the pitch of the sixth, and the Field Book — silent since the fourth — writes **one line about a count being a person rather than a number**, which settles nothing. Ilse Oller has gone back in for a cardigan; Owen enforces the rule that made the drill not the closure; **Milo goes in and gets her out on Rue's count.** Jo counts the nine seconds in which Rue was on her own and not on anybody's, and is the only person in the yard qualified to say that was nobody's fault. Ada calls the drill off and takes Ilse up. **Ends:** the marshal's minute records the success and the nine seconds together, and Milo does not correct a word of it.

### Chapter 19 — What the Roll Says

Four in the morning on the twenty-second: Lena comes back from the low streets, having told the widow at half past ten the night before. Jonas Venn's widow is fifty-four, she mends other people's clothes, she has a daughter in Wragg Street, and nobody had been to see him. She asked three questions — has anyone been, have they paid, **where is he buried** — and the third one cannot be answered, because the disposal authority names a cemetery whose register for that district was not kept after the closure. The Registry produces the file on the twenty-first as directed and it contains a **certified copy of the withdrawn page**: proof the file was extended, no statement of what was removed, and a nameable absence where the sentence about no hazard at the service court used to be. Milo's forensic note on the certification is that it is *drawn on the sheet* and the two confirmed marks are not, which is a difference in method and not in age. **Jo Lask is suspended for four weeks and half pay** and says, in a yard, that truth is cheaper when it is a person than when it is a street. And Ilse Oller crosses her own name off the rota, because she would rather be one of the people who says no, and Owen says a list with a line through it is worth more than a list with a name on it. **Ends:** at ten past five on the twenty-second, Orrin refuses the plate on the morning of the test for a reason the yard cannot answer, and Milo understands that the correct procedure was built by people who had watched a man with a lever once.

### Chapter 20 — The Copy Register

The structure comes out to full flow at ten past six and the best-operated thing Milo has ever seen is also a foreman writing a gauge reading in his own hand. The wall holds. The water goes down the old alignment and takes four hours of the low point with it, and comes out of the alignment into a service court a foot and a half deep in grey silt, with the sets standing in ridges and the handrail four feet out of true and three storage cages emptied into the river, one of them a filing box of a dead woman's letters. Nothing collapsed. The ground went where it was always going to go. Six people on the fifth floor wake at four in the morning and Owen goes up, unasked, and gives the correct answer to the only question he is asked. Iona reads out the count of four in a yard and asks who walked him. Then the Bench's own order produces the file with its provenance, and the provenance is a **copy register**: the maintenance volume Attachment 9 was copied from went out on a district works office reference and came back **eleven days after the original safety report on a Crown Heights repository reference**, so somebody in the central archive had a maintenance book on their desk in the week after a pump-house failure. The entry carries a **second pair of unsigned margin marks in two different leads**, the upper a bracket-and-dot, the lower not, and the lower one asks *who gave the order* — as it does on Attachment 9. **Two documents carry a pair, not three:** the Chapter 1 timetable margin and the Chapter 6 annex carry the mark alone, and the Chapter 10 carbon is the *consistent with, not sworn to* instance. **Ends:** Milo files a public request for the register and the two consultation references, served in open court in front of a marshal and a foreman, because *a road on a public sheet is a claim and a shelfmark is a question* — and he is walked home by a woman in a pie shop who has been doing it for nine days and who does not talk about it.

## Volume 01, Batch 0003 (Chapters 21–30)

### Chapter 21 — The Notice on the Wall

Tuesday the 23rd of Sazur. A printed Line 7 service amendment goes up on the works-office hoarding, laid for the timetable review on the 28th: the Alder Quay–Bridge Street loop withdrawn, the service court and its diversion corridor closed from the first working day after the review, the disused platform recovered and the nameboards removed, and the withdrawn alignment brought to a schedule level by the district works and **certified by the surveyor of record**. Danner prices it at eleven weeks and says out loud what it means: the low streets go back behind a lead lateral the size of a wrist and flood in November. The Registry's answer to the public request served on the 22nd arrives: the copy-register line is confirmed as a **return**, the shelfmark and the date are given, and an internal consultation reference is confirmed as **not producible** because the officer holding one is not an officer of the district. **Milo notices that the withdrawal reference on the file index and the consultation reference are the same form — four letters and two numbers, a clerk's hand — and writes it down once and says nothing for four days.** Rue's round loses two of nine stops inside the works footprint and drops to five on a Sunday. **Pell Harrow returns for one day** with a maintenance list from a village with no official address and his brother's name beside *no access, road closed, residents not notified*, says a count is not a favour, and will not be thanked. The school pack that sat nine days in the slot is resolved: the girl has a place from the 22nd. **The letterbox in the works hoarding — a corrugation, six years old, cleared by a boy since he was fourteen — is going with the hoarding, and nobody built it.** **Cost:** the sixth lost route, the way from the ferry steps up to the chandler's yard.

### Chapter 22 — Who Speaks for a Route

Wednesday the 24th. The Continuity referral is on the Board school's counter at 10.55 with nine children on it and no household name at the top. Four days a form, nine forms, one household to a form: **thirty-six days needed, four available.** Owen Fitch says the one form is a roll, and Lena Marr takes the right answer and hates it: **she files the households individually**, and the school releases **nine places until the spring intake**. She tells the yard the number twice, in the yard, at four o'clock, and does not ask to be told it was right. In the afternoon the amendment's certification box is read out at the works office: *certified by a person familiar with the route withdrawn* — a person, not a surveyor, and a description of a route is a description of what people do on it. Civic Recovery, the tram division, the foreman and the surveyor of record all decline it in nine minutes, and the fallback is the surveyor of record from survey, which means the only description of the route's use in Alderquay is the **long sheet, filed as false**, in Milo's own hand, in a district copy that says *false*. Milo signs the geometry and leaves the use blank. In the evening Ada Fenn refuses the box on the third-floor landing, gives one true thing about a door-check — *a thing that nobody decided does not have a purpose, it has a use* is Iona's later version of the same thought — and tells Milo he has called her a *third support* out loud in a yard and never asked her whether she wants to be a term. **A woman on the fourth floor has written four words on the accountancy paper: *Asked for herself. Tuesday.***

### Chapter 23 — The Interval and the Call

Thursday the 25th. In a front room over a boatyard, with the kettle going cold and Mara Quill writing verbatim with the time on it, **Tomas Rusk and Jo Lask put the old use of the bells together and do not agree, and neither pretends to.** Tomas: the three calls at ten to two are the printed shift sheet and the second-shift roll of the same book, forty-five years apart and in agreement; they are sounded from the pump-house gantry, not the frontage; and the second is not a change at all, it is the call on which **forty-one people walk down the reach into a laundromat court**. Jo: a change is a bell and a lamp and a man in a doorway, and **there is nowhere in a bell to hand a count to**, and a woman in a blue coat with her hand flat on a door is not on any shift roll, and the third call is the third because that is when a count is passed from a person to a person. Tomas: a bell is a bell and he has been hearing the bell and not the man under it for six years. **Tomas's condition is paid:** the road shut because the crew came off the frontage to the pump house and the reach emptied and a man died of a fault in a second-stage main, and a frontage with nobody on it is a road that is not being kept, and the officer wrote a hazard and an empty court into one sentence — and Tomas was the only thing left on the reach with a lamp. At four o'clock Mara reads **paragraph six of the original report**: the three calls *are not a signal to persons and indicate no entry and no refusal*, and the works consider the frontage *at all times a service access and not a public route*. It survived the replacement page. The hazard sentence did not. **Milo writes in his book that he is not going to arrange the two men, and waits four days without drawing anything at all.**

### Chapter 24 — A Line Crossed Off

Friday the 26th. Danner needs a level through the court before the plant and cannot put a fall on a form without a surveyor of record. **Milo works out that his public condition is not a restraint on his work but an instruction he gave to a foreman** — a man told that the district's surveyor has declared no passage will not send two of his own in with a tape and a torch — writes that down at 08.20, and goes. Pell Harrow holds a count of nine from the path head with a trade counter. **Owen Fitch walks him in and out, unasked, having been sent by Ada Fenn, and says on the way that if there is a mark in the route saying somebody is standing on the second landing he is stopping the work.** The return mark is the second-floor line of the accountancy paper, because a name on it has been struck through in a woman's own hand and Milo decided at ten past eight that a line crossed off is a line occupied by nobody. He wrote it in. **He went in at 13.58 and the lane believed his sheet rather than the woman.** The works shift bell went at ten to two; four seconds of the count were not said by anybody; the second landing became the foot of the alignment with silt to the boot and a handrail four feet out of true. Owen stopped the work. Milo came out on the count, out at the coal hatch side, and Pell said *nine* twice. **The fifth crossing, read out in a yard with dates and places, with Rue saying out loud, first, that the four seconds were nobody's fault.** Ilse Oller says what he did with her line, and that she did not go down to the coal hatch, and that nobody in that building is going to again. **The Field Book's one residue in the batch: *A line crossed off is not a line given.*** **Cost:** the seventh lost route — the two hundred and fourteen metres of his own route card.

### Chapter 25 — The Fourth Condition

Saturday the 27th. **Ilse Oller walks Milo to the clinic** because a note on the trestle table asked her to and not him, and corrects him four times, and tells him on the way that he has the shape of four streets out of five and it is not going to get better. Iona Sen has the crossing already — Pell told a counter clerk at seven in the morning, Hettie Brann told her at eight, and the world should know Hettie Brann is not discreet. She times him three times: fifty-eight seconds, forty-four, and one that came in wrong at the end, which is the price of one bad Friday. **She states that a condition is a floor and he has been treating it as a ceiling**, and then the finding that is not a condition and cannot be signed: **he is not to be left alone with a piece of paper about a person — not ever, not once, for as long as he is in this district.** And she tells him **the nine other people** in the treatment room since the 9th: three hurt in the drill, four the same woman on the second floor, a works-office man who has not slept, and a nineteen-year-old who went through the coal hatch side on her own and came out. The number is in a locked drawer and is not going in a report, and Milo is the first person she has told. Rue tells him the letterbox is gone as of tomorrow and asks him not to put it in a book. **Milo's book entry: no loss today, seven lost, and the ninth of Iona's number is going to be in him for the rest of the month and he does not know what a man does with a number like that except carry it wrongly.**

### Chapter 26 — The Timetable Review

Sunday the 28th. The round goes out at seven for the last time through the works footprint, five stops, and the gate-man lets Rue through. **The review is a meeting, not a hearing**, and the man from the timetable division is Barrow, nineteen years in the job, with a list of nine items. **The stay ends at 10.25 in one line**, and Barrow says he asked for it to be minuted clause by clause at his desk's request and that it is not a favour, it is so that in five years nobody can say Mara hid it. The certification box is read as *no person available*, the fallback applies, and the amendment goes through on **a description of a bend**, which Barrow says he has never done in nineteen years and is doing because the alternative is a description of a household and there is no household on the form and has not been for six years. The amendment is made at 12.20, effective the first working day. **Danner reads the drainage number into the record because Milo asks him to in a room of nine**: a 1904 lead lateral, a dead and valved storm main, and the low streets flooding in November in a dry month. Barrow writes it under Mara's name and says it will not be answered in the ordinary course, because there is no ordinary course for drainage. In the evening **Jo Lask reads out the new working timetable**: the diversion loses a minute and a half at the Ferry Road turn, the running time is eleven seconds shorter where a car comes out past the box, and **eleven seconds is the difference between handing a count over and not**. He is suspended, half pay, a hearing on a Thursday, and he says he wants it noticed that nobody asked him to read it out.

### Chapter 27 — The Second Reference

Monday the 29th and Tuesday the 30th. **Rue takes the decision:** the round goes in on foot through the works gate on the shift change, seven stops instead of five, twenty-two minutes each way, eleven weeks, **unpaid**, and she makes fourteen people say the price back to her so that she has said it in front of people and not only to a surveyor. Milo's sixth... — he writes none of it down, which is the correct thing and the hardest. In the afternoon Mara comes back with the answer to the withdrawal. **Page nine of the Bellwether file was withdrawn on a Crown Heights repository consultation reference, not a district continuity reference, and it is not in the district's book.** To get that answer she had to state a personal interest in the file in writing — **she has filed a disclosure about her own conduct**, and the review is now opened on her own paper. She also states that the withdrawal reference and the copy register's consultation reference are **the same form**, and that she has promised herself for a month not to make a structure out of a coincidence and now has two of them. **Milo stops himself two letters into a name in the corridor and Mara leaves it exactly where it is.** He then says the thing about the cost: her disclosure got filed four days late because he refused to let him file his, and he will not apologise for it, and he will from today give her anything bearing on the file in writing with a date on it whether she can use it or not — and she refuses the apology, tells him exactly what that refusal costs her every day, and will not ask him for the Lark disclosure, because a disclosure gets filed and Lark Street would be in a building in Crown Heights for the rest of her working life, put there by four hundred correct jobs. On the Tuesday the plant comes onto the alignment: the fill moves and makes a new dip, the court's drainage changes owner to a dead valved storm main, and the court is empty in a way it has never been empty. **The Field Book is silent: *Nothing. Noted, not read.*** And Milo writes that a lane which can only be answered by one person is a rota with a face on it.

### Chapter 28 — Three Accounts of One Hour

Wednesday the first of the next month, in the rain, thirty people under the porch roof. Lena Marr forbids a board: **no boxes, no table leg, and I will hold you to every word.** Jo Lask's account: three calls off the gantry, the second moves people to the court, the third is **answered**, and a count is not a thing you can say into a works. Tomas Rusk's: the printed sheet and the roll, forty-five years apart, in agreement; and the new sentence — *I have been talking about a bell and not about a lane.* Paragraph six, read by Lena from a photocopy: the three calls mean nothing, and the frontage was never a street. **Verrick Assal is asked a third time and says the same three sentences and asks, in writing, that it be recorded that he has been asked three times, said nothing three times, and is not a witness** — and adds that he will answer for the pump house instead. Then **Lena Marr reads her mother's forty-one names aloud in the rain in four minutes and eleven seconds**, and says that is what it was for, and that she has been calling it a count of the dead since she was nineteen because that was easier than saying her mother stood on a landing in the rain. **Milo writes no table and does not take a copy.** Then the arithmetic, done by a conductor: the interval between the calls is **thirty-one seconds**; the extra Bellwether entry had a platform time of **forty-one seconds** on a segment with no slack, so it was not a stop, it was a **hold**, and the difference is **ten seconds**. And a man waiting at a hold is waiting for somebody. Milo refuses the two coincidental forties and Jo says: *you don't want the two forties, you want the ten.*

### Chapter 29 — What They Agreed On

Thursday the second, four o'clock, a public meeting at the works office compound called by the district, about forty people, a trestle table under the awning, the Recovery officer turning her relief schedule face down before she speaks. Ada Fenn comes at half past four and stays at the edge, and when the school woman asks her what the bell was so that she can put a reason in a note to the education authority she says no, and gives the one true thing she will give: a door-check is a door-check, and the practice is the only part of it anybody can hand to somebody else. Four accounts are read into a public record for the first time, each read back to its author first, with Verrick's *asked three times, said nothing three times, not a witness* attached. **The school woman puts nine on the table and says the next intake is in the spring.** Danner refuses to stop the works for a meeting in a portacabin and says the statement is worth a page with four names on it instead of a gap, and that he knows what nothing looks like and it looks like a district that agrees with itself and is wrong. Mara Quill says a Bench will take four accounts or one, and that the meeting cannot be minited, so it will be sworn, and that a man who refuses still ends up in the file in her hand, dated, with his name at the foot. **And at the end Lena Marr reads the closure notice as one sentence with a semicolon in the middle** — *one loss recorded; persons to be withdrawn from the interval affected between the second and the third calls* — and says she has read it wrong for six years because everybody in the district stops at the full stop and goes and looks at the first half, and that a theory at half past nine after five hours in the rain is worth nothing, and asks for Saturday morning.

### Chapter 30 — What the Third Call Was For

Friday the third, the morning after the meeting. The yard, twenty-two people, the machine going on the other side of the wall. Milo lists what is not in dispute — three calls at ten to two off the gantry, the second moving forty-one people to the court, the third answered, the crew off the frontage at the change, the reach empty, one sentence with a hazard and an empty court in it — and then asks the false question, and **Iona Sen takes it apart**: *for* is a word about a decision, and nothing in that yard decided anything, and a thing nobody decided has a use rather than a purpose. The question is not what the three calls were for. **It is what the third one is asking for.** Then the surveyor: a service court is a place you take people to when the street is going; the door stands eleven inches above the low point of the alignment, so a man on the road is below the door and a man in the court is above it; the works wrote in paragraph six that the frontage was a service access and not a public route; put those two sentences next to each other and forty-one people were sent to the one place on that reach you send people to. **It was a move, it went to the right place, and it was interrupted in the middle.** The first two calls moved people toward the service court; the third is a check — a person saying a space is clear before the next turn; a check is not a bell. **Then Ada Fenn corrects him on the triad in a yard:** the order took the name and shut the routes, and the third support it neither took nor broke — it scattered the people who kept the routines, and one routine was not scattered, because the woman who kept it never left the river core. **Two withdrawn, one left standing and overloaded**, and she refuses the words *third support* and *broken* and will not be put on the rota. Then the round of nobody-is-wrong: Tomas is right about the bell and wrong about the word *change*; Jo is right about the tram and wrong about *only*; Lena is wrong about *counting*; Verrick offers to answer for the pump house. **Jonas Venn is separated from the lane for the first time on the page** — a real accident, a real concealment, and a sentence that put two different things in one document, and the death did not create the grammar. **The goal changes:** not reopening Bellwether, but a witness that changes hands, and a rota of five lines that is one line and a half. Ada Fenn says she is stopping, refuses to write on the sheet, and names herself: *I am a woman with a habit, and those are not the same.* Owen Fitch says one sentence about the stairwell. **Milo says out loud that a limit made in public is a bill with somebody else's name on it**, and that he is not asking this yard for a passage. And at ten past four Rue reads the accountancy paper at the trestle table with nobody watching: four names when it went out — the ground, the second struck through in her own hand, the third with a man's three words beside it, and the fourth — one floor of nothing, **and on the fifth-floor line, in a hand nobody in the yard recognises, three words that are not a note and not a refusal: *Second and Thursday.*** She folds it into the satchel and does not pin it to the wall, and Milo walks away and does not write it down.

## Volume 01, Batch 0004 (Chapters 31–40)

### Chapter 31 — The Man With the Card

Saturday the fourth, at the works before the Registry arrives. A trench on the eastern side of the old alignment, a scaffold tube, a **soft lens nine foot down** that the plate finds on every pass and that Danner predicts will take another two feet of settlement across the reach before it stops. **Tarin Kest, twenty-eight, Survey Enforcement, comes through the gate at ten past ten with his own card**, and he states the terms of his audit in advance: he is auditing **the maintenance of the order, not the order**, because he can recommend suspending an order and cannot yet do it honestly. He reads the file in filed order — he is scrupulously fair to the document and about to be wrong about one of them. **He reads the long sheet, sees *false* on the district copy, and concludes in about ninety seconds that the route has no description in this city, because the only man who wrote one had to be struck off first.** He notices the fourth finding of Ardery's forthcoming report is the sentence that decides the case in October, and he is right. Danner tells him, to his face, that he will be the man who fits the plate on the twelfth and will not enjoy it. **At four o'clock on the ramp, in front of nine people, Kest asks whether Milo's figures are contaminated by a private grievance.** Milo cannot answer aloud, because the answer is the unfiled Lark disclosure, and cannot answer in writing either. **Ends:** Kest says he will come back on Thursday, and says he is not Milo's adversary and would rather write *no* with something in the box. Milo's entry: the inspector is competent, read the file properly, understood in ninety seconds what six people in a yard took a month to understand, and is going to exclude him.

### Chapter 32 — Six Days

Monday the sixth. **The plate variation goes in at twenty past seven** on a six-day clock, and the reason takes Danner a quarter of an hour and contains three sentences a form does not require, and the works clerk reads two of them before he puts the tray down. **The *alternative considered* box is filled in a foreman's own hand, and the reason is that he will not leave a space on a district form.** **Orrin Sable comes down the ramp at half past ten**, does not go in the trench, and makes the batch's central political speech: *a believed route is a used route, and a made route is a used route*, and eleven weeks of regrading takes a route and turns it into a surface, and a surface is the only thing a person can stand on without somebody's permission. He says he is putting the plate on the form early and saying so, that his date is the sixth of December, and that he read the drainage box and would do it again. **He asks about the rota and is right about it**: no standing, no authority, no inspector, and the day the last name is crossed off it reads as empty. He leaves on the ramp. **At 15.20 the alignment comes up to the schedule level** across ninety feet, seven tenths out, with a visible join where the old court was. **The Field Book is silent and Milo writes two lines and distrusts it.** **Ends:** the notice is stamped and put up on the hoarding at ten past nine on the 7th, and Milo reads it that same evening standing up and cannot put the sentence down; Danner's line at the foot of it goes up on the Sunday morning after, which is the 12th.

### Chapter 33 — A Name on the Fifth Floor

Wednesday the eighth, the round home at four. **The rota is read out loud and counted, and Rue states the rule that makes the count single-valued: five lines, five names, one line through one of them in the woman's own hand and **not restored, and that line is counted as having a person behind it because she is the one who put it through and nobody has put it back**; four people behind four of the five names, and the fifth-floor name counted as having nobody behind it. She says she will say it the same way every time anybody asks her.** Rue refuses, in three separate places, to let anybody read the fourth-floor or fifth-floor name as a curiosity, and states why in her own terms: *it is the only power I have got and I am not spending it on curiosity.* **Idar went up to the fifth-floor landing on the Wednesday: a light on, a door, no answer, two knocks, and he came back down.** The post is addressed to that name. **Nobody goes up again and Rue does not pin the sheet to the wall.** In the same afternoon **Tomas Rusk withdraws from swearing** and does not take the account back, and gives the reason himself: a sworn account is a man saying out loud that he will stand behind it for the rest of his life, and his account says a road was not being kept, and a road cannot be put right, and the district's own mechanic would be the one who swore it, and it would be in a file for a hundred years with nobody knowing which half mattered. **The reason is a wage, four years of a pension and a knee, and nobody argues him out of it.** He also says, for the first time out loud, that somebody in Crown Heights had a mechanic's private book in front of them on the ninth of that month six years ago, and that he is not going to say it out loud again. **Ends:** Milo writes four events down in the order he can defend, and the fourth is that a document is the same in six years and a sworn account is not, and three of the four people in this district are on a piece of accountancy paper.

### Chapter 34 — A Blank Left by Accident

**Monday the tenth, the morning, and he asks before he writes — the request drafted the night before — for the first time in four months, and Jo Lask refuses and is right**: he has no time, no shift, no box, no turn, he is suspended, and *a man who is available at a time is a man who can be asked at a time* — and he sends Milo back to the one thing Jo Lask warned him about on the 28th of Sazur, which is the hole where a withdrawn person is. **Verrick Assal agrees to walk him** and explains the yes in nine words and then four minutes: he has been asked three times about a bell he was not in a room for, he asked in writing to be recorded as not a witness, and a man who can say stop is not a witness. **The sixth crossing, the tenth of the month, in and out at 14.06**, eleven seconds inside a stated nine minutes, four of them at the beginning because the ordinary part lasted four seconds this time. The lane asks the same question it asked on the twenty-sixth, and **there is nobody at a door, and that is all, and nothing supernatural happens.** The book's one line, **six words**: ***A blank left by accident closes.*** He limits it in his own hand before he does anything with it, and does not take it near the yard for a week. **The count is read in a yard of nineteen in the rain with Iona Sen's tally present, six crossings with dates and places, and Iona says she knows he went in with a piece of paper about a person in his head and will not say it in front of the nineteen and will not let it go either.** **Cost:** the eighth lost route, the two hundred metres from the works gate to the end of the disused platform, **given back the next morning in chalk on a gate post by a man who was told by Rue to say it was not for the surveyor.**

### Chapter 35 — A Minor Seam

Saturday the eleventh, ten past two. **Cass Ardery's field report, in full on the page** (and the Bench he is frightened of is named here as the twenty-first of the month, with ten days to it, which is the only October date in the batch), dated the 11th on an inspection of the 9th: two supports withdrawn; **no witness maintained**; *no rota, schedule, roster or return arrangement is maintained at the site by the district, by the works, or by any party having authority in this matter*; **persons affected: nil**; minor seam; no further public interest requiring enquiry. **Milo's reading: the report is a true statement about the supports, made in a form that cannot be corrected from inside the form, and it is the same shape of error as paragraph six of the original safety report** — a correct statement about a thing, filed in the first week, that governs the case because nothing in the form has a box for the part that is not true. Ardery is told to his face and takes it. **At six o'clock, about thirty-five people are in the yard because that is what time the shift bell has gone for six years, and the plant stops for eight or nine seconds and starts again, and a woman of about seventy on the second landing says it was eight when she was young.** **That is the witness, and it cannot be put in a box**, and nobody is asked to knock on a door. Mara Quill, who has been in the yard for an hour and eleven minutes without speaking, says the one thing about a document: the fourth finding will decide October without anybody choosing to, and the correction is not *the rota exists* but a document that says a person is there on the authority of somebody who can be cross-examined, and there is not one in this district, and the promise at the foot of the sheet is doing more civic work than anything she has filed since she was twenty-six, and the moment it is in a report it has an authority over it and stops working. **Pell's card comes through the round on the Monday:** who keeps a road that is not on a map; the list is in a tin and there is no hut; *do not tell me what to do with this.*

### Chapter 36 — Plate Fixed

Sunday the twelfth. **The plate goes on at ten past six in the morning in nine minutes, with the door shut, by two of Danner's own, and Danner writes *plate fixed* on the form at six twenty-five.** **By nine o'clock the trench is standing in river water, and Danner explains that a door is not a hole in a wall, it is a hole that goes through it, and four inches of steel on the through end makes the low point a pocket with a roof and a wall and no way out.** The soft lens is the only thing left to tell the water where to go, and the low streets are the low ground next to it. **And the plate contradicts the form Danner applied on, in the box where he put the truth because the box was the only place it would go.** **By half past one the water is four inches on the crown of Bridge Street, in a nine-foot band, in a month with nine-tenths of an inch of rain, and it stops at Nell Crale's cellar and goes round it.** She has kept a cold store and a milk-and-ice round for twenty-two years and there is no cold store within four streets. Milo Rook offers her the only tool he has and she tells him what it is worth. **Dr Sen has eleven at the dispensary counter between two and six, and the treatment-room count for the day is eleven, nine of them since the ninth of Sazur and two since, and she does not total the number out loud, and tells him to go and count the ones who are not on the schedule, on doorsteps.** **At six o'clock the third call goes and the plant stops, and a man in the trench says *nobody told us to*, and nobody answers him.** Milo's entry is the shortest since the twenty-sixth: nothing of this is his map, and the plate is four inches of steel fitted lawfully on a six-day form, and the form says the low point is to remain clear of standing water.

### Chapter 37 — The Precedent

Monday the thirteenth and Tuesday the fourteenth. The two returns on the board and the Recovery officer under the awning with her schedule face up for the first time, saying she has collected two and four a head on two hundred and thirty-one people since the middle of last month and **cannot tell you their names because she does not know them.** **Tarin Kest reads out a document he did not expect to find**: a district continuity order four years back on thirty-four dwellings at the low end of **Filewell**, varied and allowed to lapse on a certificate signed by a foreman who does not remember doing it — and **on its index, a repository consultation reference in the same form as the one against page nine of the Bellwether file.** He does not claim a match, does not open the third box, and writes *no person available* on the corner of a sheet himself. His conclusion: the Filewell district had thirty-four households and a clerk could name a person at the counter, and this district has one household on five storeys and the register's answer is a man asleep on the third floor, **and that is the whole of the difference between the two files and nobody in the order behaved differently.** Then the room with a door, and his paragraph, and the *file is not a witness* exchange: **Mara Quill brings him a drafted application to strike his record from the file on his own application, and tells him she drafted it** — not because she thinks he is contaminated, but because in seven days she has to put something in front of a Bench and she was looking for a way not to have to be the one who defends him in a room, and she says that is her own hand and she has a review about similar work. **He tells her to take the measurement out. She refuses, and gives the third reason: he is not going to be out of this file, because a file that one man can discredit is a witness, not a case.** **The other way: the level is a foreman's level, and it goes into the works' file under Danner's signature, and everything else of Milo's is struck without his consent.** He catches her designing a rescue for him and sends her back to ask Danner first, in the exact terms of the thing that has been done in this district since the sixth of six years ago. **Ends:** Kest lodges his own variation application in his own name in a corridor in front of nine people, it comes back with *no person available*, he says thank you, and says a form with an entry in it is a document and a document is what a department can be made to do something with.

### Chapter 38 — Two Hundred and Forty-Nine

Thursday the sixteenth. **Danner writes *subsidence observed, 4 ft in trench, low end* on the Wednesday's return and is stood down at ten past six on the Thursday in about nine seconds in a portacabin, by Rennick, the contractor's site manager, who says it is a company thing and not a Thursday thing and has the form ready.** The regrade stops because the daily return has to be certified and the only man certified to certify it is off the site: **two days out of eleven weeks, which nobody in the district notices.** The sixteenth's return goes up at ten past nine in Rennick's clerk's hand with **six inches, eleven properties, persons affected: nil, subsidence: none observed** — and the first return on which nobody had been in a trench, and the trench had four feet in it. **The argument about the number happens in a yard of thirty at four o'clock**, with Lena Marr laying the Bench's minute of the fourteenth of Sazur beside the board. **Tarin Kest says out loud that two divisions of his own department have said two different things about the same street in the same week and he has to write both in the same paragraph.** **The count is done over two days by six people on doorsteps without knocking**, which is Lena's rule and the whole of it, and it comes out on Friday evening as **two hundred and forty-nine people in fifty-four rooms in eleven streets, eighteen more than the schedule, and thirty-one rooms on no list at all.** **The Recovery officer does not turn the schedule face down**, and says the number is a number of people and her schedule is a number of rooms and a rate cannot be built on a door, and that she has been collecting two and four a head for six weeks and has never been told what the two and four is for. **Orrin Sable comes at half past three, stands at the back for twenty minutes, and says the number out loud, and says he read the drainage box on the sixth and let the plate go anyway and would do it again on Monday, and puts three pumps on that street that night with a man he stole from the eastern works, and says that is the only part of this he could do anything about.**

### Chapter 39 — The Interchange

Sunday the nineteenth. **The regrade restarts on the Saturday with a second gang and a second machine to make up the two lost days, and the last ninety feet, the ninety that pass under the Alder Quay interchange, go down at half past three with a spirit level across the join and the bubble in the middle**, and a man writes *level* on the site sheet in his own hand. **At six o'clock, on the third call, the west side of the interchange goes nine inches out of true over about thirty feet of street.** It does not collapse; it ratchets about the three ways on solid ground. **The footbridge stair tower — a hundred and one steps, on one masonry pier, put there in 1938 — comes down across the rails and comes down eleven feet out of place.** Eleven people on it, nine off it on their own feet, two not off it by themselves: **Petts, who was the man at the bottom of the trench on the fourth and was shored up out of it by a scaffold tube, two of the second gang, and a tram driver of thirty-one called Whelan who came off on her feet.** The overhead goes, the junction box shears off its base, the Alder Quay substation feeder follows, and the evening service is worked by hand from about seven. **Nobody is in the water, the wall holds, and the culvert is where it was on the twenty-second of Sazur and has not moved.** Milo walks the hundred and forty feet in under two minutes, does not go onto the tower, and gives the same reason twice. **Jo Lask — suspended from driving since the twenty-second of Sazur, four weeks and half pay, so that the four weeks do not run out until the twentieth, and not from the platform — takes the count by hand at the Ferry Road turn for four services and gets every one right, and the diversion gives the count eleven seconds less because a signal is not a person, and the thing he read out in a yard on the twenty-eighth of last month came onto a printed timetable.** Dr Sen has four through the treatment room and does not total. **The Field Book is silent, and Milo writes the silences down as two dated pairs, the thirtieth of Sazur with the sixth and the tenth with the nineteenth, and declines to read anything into either; the token is in his pocket and cold.** **The joint finding of the Monday is four paragraphs and its second paragraph says a surface was made level across a place which had been two surfaces for six years** — differential settlement over levelled made ground, entirely true, naming only the certifying surveyor of record, **and Orrin Sable is in the finding once, in a list of persons who were present.** **Ends:** Mara Quill, in a corridor, tells him to write nothing of his own on it, because a sentence with his handwriting on it is a sentence a man in a coat can throw away in October.

### Chapter 40 — What She Won

Tuesday the twenty-first, Directions Bench, ten past two to five past three. **The Bench in this batch is the twenty-first of the month and nothing else: ten days from the eleventh, nine from the twelfth, seven from the fourteenth.** The chair, **Perrins**, states on the record what the application is not: it does not suspend the order, does not restore a name, does not lift the regrade or the plate, and does nothing about the water. **Four things asked for, one granted.** Refused: the classification, on the ground that **evidence is not excluded in a room for being the wrong shape**; the daily return, on the ground that it is evidence of a day book and not of a street; the surveyor's account of the level, on the ground that a document speaks for itself. The count of two hundred and forty-nine is entered on the record **as a count**. The instrument: **one named person, once a week, in daylight, on the last Thursday of each of the next four weeks — the 23rd, the 30th, the 6th and the 13th — expiring at noon on Friday the fourteenth of next month, extendable by nobody including the chair, with no form for extension because there is no form for a thing that is only ever four weeks.** The named person is **Lena Marr, who has not been named by Mara Quill because she has not been asked her**, and whose written consent is read out in her own words, and who states the price herself: **two of the nine children are not coming back in the spring, and the minute says so.** **Tarin Kest lodges his variation application and it comes back with *no person available*, and he says thank you, and says he is not going to change the paragraph about Milo's interpretation because a bridge came down, because if he changes it after a bridge comes down it is a reaction and not a decision.** In the corridor Milo says the one thing he wants: not that his account be true, but that it be **true and not his.** In the yard, Lena Marr says the four Thursdays are not a route and that somebody has to build the next one, and describes it exactly — not a map, a list, a record, a petition, a hearing or a rota; more than four people; somebody can be handed it and somebody else can change it; **a space left in it on purpose; and somebody who was not there on the first crossing.** **Ends:** Milo's entry — the crossing count, the eighth lost route given back in chalk, three silences in the book, the Field Book sound, the line home legible — and the new problem, which is that he cannot build the thing this district needs because Iona Sen's fifth finding is that he is not to be left alone with a piece of paper about a person, and the first person anybody will think of is on the fifth floor of a converted pump works and has not come down, and nobody is going to go up and ask, and he has written that down so that the next person knows it has been thought about and left where it was.

## Volume 01, Batch 0005 (Chapters 41–50)

### Chapter 41 — Four Thursdays

Wednesday the 22nd and Thursday the 23rd. **The works' consent is given by the site manager in eleven minutes in a portacabin** on a four-line form reading SITE / PERSON / OCCASIONS / PERIOD, the four dates written out in full, the hours written on the form, and **site** and not **route**, and Milo Rook is outside the door listening at, and has said since that those eleven minutes were the most important thing that happened to him that week and that he was not in them. Rennick's reason, said once to a door: the order does not stop him, the daily return has said *persons affected: nil* for eleven days, and a person at a door in daylight is a person in a photograph, and he would rather have one person on his site who is a person than a return saying nobody is here. **The first of the four Thursdays at ten to two, at the ground-floor door, coal hatch side, by Lena Marr, with Rue holding a count and Verrick Assal standing in the strip with a spade he has brought and will not use.** The call asks; she says it; **nothing whatever happens**; she is home at eleven minutes past two and makes Milo write the reading down and corrects one word of it — *empty* becomes *clear*. **The project is named out loud in a yard of twenty-six and nobody agrees what it is**, and three answers are said: a street (a woman of sixty on the second landing, who says she went back to sleep the first morning the bells went after the closure and would like it on the record), a connection (Doreen Sankey, forty-one, two rooms behind a boatyard, eleven minutes to the tram steps, who gives his name in a yard for the first time), and a memorial (Tomas Rusk, sixty-one, who came down two flights with a stick and says he would rather be in a thing that is wrong than outside of it). **No fourth position is offered and Lena Marr is careful not to offer one.** Owen Fitch, from under the awning: *Three things is a fact. One is a person. Four is an institution, and there is no form for an institution.* **Ends:** Milo's entry. Two things nobody foresaw. The second person cannot get there — Tomas Rusk's knee, a laundry on the low end, four inches of water — and a door on the second landing is shut, and a document is about to say somebody checked it and is going to be wrong on that landing for as long as it is on the wall. He reads the last line back the next morning and leaves it. The Field Book is silent after thirteen days and he writes the date down and **does not pair it**, because a pair of four days is one pair and he is not going to build a set to match the six-day and the nine-day.

### Chapter 42 — A Signal Is Not a Person

Monday the 27th to Wednesday the 29th. The substation feeder goes back in eleven days late and the count at the Ferry Road turn is on a signal again by Tuesday, and nobody finds out for two days. **Pell Harrow comes back at two o'clock on the Monday**, thinner, with a card, and puts it on the table face up, which he has never done. **He has not seen Tern**, he asked Ostrow one question he will not write down, and he got a true answer. He brings a second fact and stops: a woman in Wragg Street keeps a nineteen-year-old sheet for three rooms over a chandler's, with a fourth column of ticks in a copying hand — **the same tick, about six lines, four streets, four months** — and he will not go back and look, *because the day I go back and look is the day I am a man with a list instead of a courier with a round.* Rue reads the card over Milo's shoulder and comments on nothing; Milo puts a hand on the notebook, does not ask to open it, and writes in his own book that he is not going to put a boy of seventeen and a sheet of paper in the same paragraph. **Barrow comes on the Tuesday with the new sheet, four pages, made of a cardboard cover out of a district envelope.** The cycle turns over on the first; the diversion is permanent; the minute and a half at the Ferry Road turn is gone and the running time is eleven seconds shorter where a car comes out and goes past the box; and the box now reads **COUNT AT FERRY ROAD TURN — AUTOMATIC. NO STAFF TURN.** The hand came off on the nineteenth of this month because there was a form about a man doing it, and that is what a form does.
