He offers to minute anything brought to him and says plainly that if it has no authority behind it he will minute that it has no authority behind it, and *that is the most useful thing I can do for you and it will not help you.* He cannot put a person in a column because a column ends up in nine hundred pockets and there is nobody in this city who can put a name in a column and take it out again. **On the way out he mentions, once, that he has read the residents' sheet, that a man in his division called it a works thing, that the man was wrong, and that he did not correct him — because a file is worse than a sheet.** **Jo Lask refuses a second time and is right.** Asked in writing on the Sunday night for four hours a week, off the sheet, on his own account, with no roster and no column and no name in it. He is not suspended any more — the four weeks ran out on the twentieth — and he says that is the reason he can say no more freely and not less, and gives the same reason underneath: a document that can lean on him turns him into a habit, and Rue's sheet is the only honest piece of civic machinery in the district precisely because the promise at the foot means he cannot be leaned on. He asks Milo to tell the woman he said the same eleven words he said on the tenth. **Milo's entry: he was not asked, he was told.** **Ends:** Tarin Kest's application is refused, in four paragraphs, and the department notes among the matters considered that a Bench minute of the twenty-first permits one named person to walk the threshold on four dates and that no similar arrangement is proposed after the fourteenth of next month. Mara Quill's reading, exactly: *That is not a win. Nothing in it grants us anything.* It puts the four Thursdays into a division's file in Crown Heights, described as an arrangement, in a list of things that were before the person who refused it — which means it can be taken away. She says she will know of no such thing if anybody in this building asks her about a residents' sheet, and wants it written down that she said it first.

### Chapter 43 — Two Counts

Thursday the 30th and the Friday and Saturday following. **The second Thursday: the call goes, twenty-nine seconds of the district's thirty-one elapse, and it does not ask.** She stands at the door, and at about twenty seconds past two she comes back out and says *that's me stopping*, is told she need not give a reason, and gives one — her legs were cold — which is true and which nobody writes down. In the yard, to about fourteen people: *It did not ask. That is the whole report.* And she will not be praised for standing in the cold and will not be told next week that she has got the hang of it. **The count is done again** on Lena Marr's rule, on doorsteps, without knocking, because the Recovery officer has to answer a letter. **A rates authority has asked for a schedule of rooms, by street and dwelling, within fourteen days, failing which the schedule stands at two hundred and thirty-one and the per-head charge continues on it.** The officer says out loud that she has fourteen days and no way to make one, and turns the schedule face up for the second time, and says that eighteen people are short and she cannot tell you their names because she does not know them, and that **the two and four a head is five hundred and fifty-four pounds and forty a week**. A rate is built on a property and a person in a room is not a property, and the moment this city is told a person is a rateable thing about ninety forms get simpler and about two hundred and thirty-one people's lives get worse. And the works clerk has already asked Lena Marr, in a corridor, in words, whether the count of the seventeenth could be adopted, and she said no twice and he said *it is only a schedule* twice, and the levy is a schedule and the schedule is a contract and eleven weeks of a regrade is paid out of it. **The second count comes out on the Saturday evening at about six: two hundred and fifty-two, in fifty-five rooms, in eleven streets, thirty-three rooms on no list, twenty-one short.** Four up and one down; two rooms and one room gone; a wall with a boatyard behind it that nobody in this district has ever put on a piece of paper. **Lena Marr refuses to put it on anything**, because the moment you hand a rates authority a number they stop counting people and start building a list of rooms, and she will have handed it to them because she was angry in a yard. The officer puts the second count in her own bag and says it is going in a drawer and not on a schedule, and that on the fourteenth day she is going to write to a rates authority and say she cannot produce a rateable basis, and that this is the first time in nineteen years she has written a letter saying she cannot do her job. **Tomas Rusk gives the district its only instrument, from a trestle table, because he cannot get to the door:** thirty-one seconds is the interval in the street and **twenty-nine inside the pump house**, because the shaft is a two-inch flat on a six-inch drum and it takes up before it takes out and there is a lad with his hand on a lever; so the thirty-one is real and the twenty-nine is real, and a person at a door counting to thirty-one is counting a machine, and **the instrument is: do not count, wait until it stops, and then say it.** He pays the taxi driver an extra sixpence for waiting.

### Chapter 44 — Four Versions

Monday the 3rd and Tuesday the 4th. **The sheet is typed on the works office machine by the contractor's clerk**, thirty-one, two years in the job, no first name on the page, in the eleven minutes between the half-hour and the quarter to when the returns have gone up on the board, **with the employer's head on it**, because the head is on the machine's stationery and the works pays for the stationery, and Milo Rook is in the room and says nothing. Rue says he could have said something; he says the boxes that ruin people are never in front of anybody. **Rennick refuses to take the head off in nine words and then four hundred more**: every sheet that leaves the office has the firm's name on it, he has been sued twice for a document that left without it, he cannot take it off a document he did not type, and if the district wants paper of its own it must buy it on a district account — and the district office is Milo's. **The head stays, and it is the single most useful thing that happened to the sheet, because a sheet with a flood-control contractor's head on it is a thing a letting agent immediately understands to be a thing that is organised.** Four versions, dated, in a bundle tied with twine, held by Rue. The first went up on the porch wall on the Friday of the twenty-fourth of last month, columns only, and the yard argued about it for two days. The second had a box at the top for a **household**, and Lena Marr took it out with her thumbnail in four seconds, because a household on a sheet is a room and a room on a sheet is a rate. The third had the works' head and a route written out in full. The fourth differs in four respects and **every one of them is somebody else's decision**: the hour is *from about ten to two* because a mechanic said the person waits until it stops; the route is gone and replaced by *the way the round goes out at eight and in at four*, which is Rue's, because a route written on a wall gets walked by people who were not on it; the columns are **DAY, HOUR, TURN, DONE**; and at the foot, under a rule, in the same typing, are three sentences — nobody writes anybody else's name, a person who wrote a line may cross it out and write another and nobody may cross out a line they did not write; **when the week is done the sheet comes off the nail and it is not kept, and whoever took the last turn puts up the next one**; nothing on this sheet goes into an office and a person may take it off the nail — **and about an inch and a half of white space with nothing on it.** Milo wanted a word in it and wrote it in his own notebook and crossed it out: the word was *floor*. Rue: *it stays empty.* It will get filled — *and it is not for a floor.* **Hettie Brann agrees to hold it, spiked behind the dispensary counter, and not read it, and not enter it in anything, and to say *I do not read it* to a badge the same as to anybody — and tells Milo he is not to bring it to her, and that she has watched two of him.** **Milo's whole part, named on the page: the columns, the rule at the foot, four dated versions in a bundle, the arithmetic, and the reading-distance finding that forty per cent of a typed page is legible to anybody over about eleven and not to a man of sixty-eight at night without a lamp.** His finding: *a sheet of paper that has to be replaced every week, on a nail, in a building, by a person who can be found, is a thing that survives a timetable.* **At half past four, in a porch, at Lena Marr's request, in front of about thirty people, he refuses to be the author, the holder, the clerk or the person who puts up the next sheet**, and says the doctor told him and he has never written it down and has broken it twice, and gets a noise that is not agreement, and Rue says it is a surveyor's answer to a question that is not a surveyor's. **Ends:** the copy comes on the round from Neville Cobb, who lets four rooms behind a boatyard, who was in the works office at half past two on the Monday asking about a length of lead pipe, and who used the machine and put in a shilling and two coins for a ream. Milo has it for about six minutes at half past five. He reads the head, the date, the reduction, the four columns, the rule — **and the line at the top, which is not the round's route, which is the Ferry Road side: boatyard steps, towpath, under the embankment, tram steps, and back.** *That is the route the man from behind the boatyard described at this trestle table on the twenty-third, off the top, in full, to about thirty people.* Then there is a column of six names, he puts his hand flat over it, says it is a piece of paper about six people and he has been told about that, and takes his hand off it. **Rue picks it up and reads the names aloud, and the sixth is the name on Doreen Sankey's tenancy, and he has never been asked anything, because he came to a yard and asked for a way to the tram steps.**

### Chapter 45 — The Man Who Lets Rooms

Tuesday the 4th in the evening, and Wednesday the 5th and the Wednesday morning. **The route on the copy was walked on the Tuesday at about half past three, two hours before Milo Rook stood in a porch again and read a second one of them out loud, by two people who believed it**, and he did not know that for nineteen hours, which is the Wednesday at the counter. **The first copy was read on the Monday the 3rd at half past five, at the end of Chapter 44; this is a second copy, read out on the Tuesday the 4th at half past five.** Neville Cobb came in on the Monday asking about a length of lead pipe, used the machine for four minutes, and wanted to show three people looking at rooms that the block was organised, and a head like that means a thing a company has agreed to. **He filled in six empty boxes, and that is the whole of what he did, and he has never denied it and was not asked to.** Four names off his own rent book, and two names the woman at the door of the Board school gave him when he asked, in the way a woman who keeps a list gives a list, by reading it. **One of the two was the woman of sixty from the second landing of Cedar Court**, who hears about it on the Wednesday from Hettie Brann, in a queue, in front of six people, and comes down at half past five, which is the first time she has come down at that hour in nine years, and does not shout. *I have been on the second landing of a building with no number for nine years and my name is not on nothing... and a man I've never met has got my name in his hand because I stood at a school door and he asked me a question and I read him off a list. That is the fault. I want it said in this yard in that order and I don't want it softened.* And then: *I want my name off it, and I want to be the one who takes it off, and I want to be asked first before anything is done about it, and I am not going to be asked first because there is nobody to ask me.* **Doreen Sankey has no record.** He is called Doreen after his mother, who kept a laundry on the Ferry Road side, and has never changed it, and for nine years the only place his name had been written down was a rent book — which is where the copy got it from. The Recovery officer looks: no card, no index entry, no rate, no tenancy, no schedule, and never any. *This is not a refusal. This is me telling you the truth about a district, and the truth about a district is that if you are not written down then you cannot be taken off anything either.* He asks her to write him down and take him off. She writes four lines in her own hand, dates them, and keeps them: *No record of this person. No rate. No tenancy. No schedule. He is not on any list in this office and never has been. He was on a sheet of paper in a yard and that was not this office.* And she will not give it to him, because the moment it is a piece of paper about a person it wants a file number, and the moment it has a file number it is a record, and a record is a thing that can be produced. **The route gets walked: boatyard steps, towpath, under the embankment, and the short cut to the tram steps, which is not a path — it is the old wash-house channel crossing, a plank and a joist and eleven feet of rotten board with a rail on one side and nothing on the other.** Carraway puts his foot through it four feet from the far end, goes in to the knee in a tide, is got out with a boat hook in twenty minutes, and cuts his knee and his coat. **The boatyard owner nails the boards shut on the Wednesday morning with three-inch nails and says the crossing is out for five days, and that anybody who puts a foot through rotten boardwork in a tide is going to be in the river.** Five days put Doreen Sankey on the wrong side of his own gate; the dispensary counter is in Cedar Court, the milk is on Bridge Street, and Nell Crale goes and tells him about it in ninety seconds without being asked, and says she is not going to talk about a man who has not asked her. **The sheet is not withdrawn**, and Lena Marr says so first, before anybody can get there, and gives the reason: *If we take that sheet off the nail we have said in front of thirty people that it was ours... and then the woman of sixty is on a district list, and Doreen Sankey is on a district list, and I have spent eleven weeks keeping this district from making a list and I am not going to start on a Wednesday.* So: **a man of forty-one who has never been on anything in his life writes four words beside his own name in his own hand — *I only read it* — and the woman of sixty crosses her own name out, writes nothing beside it, will not be asked why, will not let anybody put a reason in for her, and goes back up.** *That is Owen's rule, and Lena Marr has been living off it for a fortnight and is not going to pretend she thought of it.*

### Chapter 46 — Three Things People Want

Thursday the 6th, Friday the 7th, Saturday the 8th. **The third Thursday, and the first thing the thing has given the district for nothing.** The call asks at the interval, she says it, and **about a second and a half later a voice from off the outside stairs, at about the height of the second-floor landing, says the same word, and the door goes.** Nobody went up, Rue said *stay where you are* to about four people who had started, and Milo Rook did not go, and left the word out of the line and put it in brackets on the facing page and wrote beside the brackets that he did not ask who it was and did not go, and that both are correct and he wants it on the page that he wanted to. Walking back, she says she is not going to tell anybody about it until she has spoken to the people on a landing she cannot get into, and that she has been on that landing eleven times in nine years and does not know which of the four doors it was. **The three answers, said properly on the Friday evening of the seventh and on the Saturday morning in a kitchen, and none of them chosen.** A **street** — the woman of sixty: a name on the index, a number on the door, a shop that sends to it, a post that comes to it, a doctor that can be found, a school that can be asked whether a child lives here, and she does not care what the bells were and has gone back to sleep every working day since the closure. A **way to walk** — Doreen Sankey: not the name of it, the way; eleven minutes; and he has decided he would rather be hard to find, *which is a stupid thing to want and I want it.* A **memorial** — Tomas Rusk, ninety words at a kitchen table with a stick against the edge of it: a road with people on it that you walk through, somebody who knows the people, a place to put flowers — three different things, and every one of them somebody's whole reason for being alive, and he will not pick, and he has decided he would rather be in a thing that is wrong than outside of it. **Nobody picked one, in the yard or in the kitchen, and the not-picking was the meeting.** The school is the worst of it, because it is where the argument has a number in it: about twenty-six people at the door of the Board school on the Friday at three o'clock, **nine children out until the spring intake, two of the nine gone for good because their mother is a named person on an instrument of a Bench, seven back in the spring if the intake opens, and the intake opens on a tenancy, a temporary registration or a Continuity referral and every one of the three asks for a place.** The woman at the door will not be the door-check and is right: a woman who stands at a door is a woman who will be asked whether the people behind it are where she thinks they are, and if the answer is no the next question is what she did about it, and she has thirty-one children in three rooms. **Rue stops Milo in the yard that morning and says one sentence: he will be in there with a piece of paper about thirty households inside of ten minutes, and she is not his doctor and is not going to pretend to be.** He is not one of the twenty-six; he stood a hundred yards off in the rain and looked at a wall for an hour and a half. **Ends at a washing line behind the boatyard at half past five on the Thursday, in the rain, where Owen Fitch comes down alone with a stick to say no outside, so that it is not a thing that happened.** A memorial route needs a turn and he is the only person anybody can think of. *You can have a memorial with nobody at the turn. It will just be a road with flowers on it and no way of finding out whether the people on it are where you think they are, and that is worse than what we have got now... and if you put my name on that sheet in any form I will take it off myself, in my own hand, and you will all watch, and none of you will say a word about it afterwards.* *A document only has to be believed once.* And Lena Marr at her own table at half past nine, with the wallet and the photocopy: *if the choice is going to get made then it is going to get made in a yard by people who live here, and I am going to be in it, and it is not going to be me on my own in a kitchen with a plastic wallet.*

### Chapter 47 — The Sixth of December

Saturday the 8th, Sunday the 9th, Monday the 10th. **The works' notice goes up on the hoarding at 09.10 on the Saturday, on the same hoarding as the plate notice, in the same typeface, under the same head, and Milo reads it standing up.** The coal hatch side access, from the ground-floor door to the works gate, **closed to the public from the ninth to the nineteenth of this month inclusive**; access by the path to the north; **the path to the north is not a public route**; no alternative provision; signed for the works by R. Rennick, site manager. Milo writes the time on the back of his hand, rubs it out, and writes it properly — *Eighth of the month, 09.25* — and the note is: eleven days, the fourth Thursday is inside them, and he has not said a word to anybody and is going to see the man who is better at this before he does. **Orrin Sable is in the trench**, which he has not done once in eleven weeks and which cost him a coat and boots he bought in the last two days. **The engineering, in his own words, in about two hundred words, and Milo writes none of it down while he is saying it:** the soft lens is eleven years of river silt with a lens in it about twenty foot across, settled nine inches over forty feet since August and two foot more since the third; the book said the last of it before the first of next month and then it stops, **and it stops on Monday**; a level is no use to anybody on ground that is still going down; **the floodgate test is on the sixth of next month** and the test wants a made surface on its flood side and is the only thing in this city that has ever tested whether the wall behind the district can take a river; **so the ground stops on the first, the certificate has to be made on ground that has stopped, the test is on the sixth, and that leaves him the second, the third, the fourth and the fifth of next month — four days** — and the laying of it means digging a trench four foot down and eight foot wide through the only made ground on the reach, and there is no way to lay a trench under a public path while people walk on it, and the north side is not a path, it is a place where the fill goes. He says the closing is not a measure about the district and not anybody punishing the district, and that he read the minute of the twenty-first when he read it, and has decided **not** to move the date, and says so out loud, and that Milo may write it down and it does him no good whatever. He puts **five pumps** on three streets he is not responsible for and says the gully has come up on the low end for the eleventh day in a row in a month with under an inch of rain, and the district flood line is nine inches. **Danner is brought back at full rate for eleven days by Rennick, which resets the review, and he writes on the form that he is doing it for the certificate and not the money, and Rennick initialled it and put it in his employer's file.** Petts is in the trench and is still saying *nobody told us* and nobody has asked him to stop and Milo has not asked him to stop and has noted that he has not asked. **The yard at half past one on the Sunday** has about forty people in it, the largest since the eleventh, all of them on the wrong day because the path is shut and the round is in. **The word November is said eleven times**, counted afterwards, admitted to Rue, told off for. Lena Marr: *I am not going to stand at a door that is not there. And I am not going to ask a foreman for a door, because the day I ask a foreman for a door is the day this is a work, and it is not a work... I am not going to say it was a coincidence either.* **Ends on the Monday the tenth at half past five with a variation of the second stage on the trestle table, in Danner's hand, the hours filled in and the *alternative considered* box filled in because he will not leave a space on a district form: reach brought to schedule level on or before the fifth of next month and the level certified on ground recorded as settled on the first of next month; no alternative available in the period; and then the sentence that is the whole of him** — *I record that the certification is made in the fourteenth day of a standard deviation in the readings at the trench, and I will not be able to say in a room that I stood behind it, and I am certifying it because the level has to be certified by a man and the only man is me.* *That is my version of what you lot call a document that cannot be corrected from inside itself, and I have written the untrue half of it in the box that only takes it.* And: *the joint finding of the twentieth says a surface was made level across a place which had been two surfaces for six years, and that finding went to a Bench on the twenty-first of October and sat in the file there, and the paragraph is true, and the paragraph is about me.*

### Chapter 48 — An Empty Line Is a Door

Monday the 10th, Tuesday the 11th, Wednesday the 12th. **The sheet came off the nail on the Friday morning at eleven because the clerk spiked the new week's page over it and nobody took the old page away**, and the old page was in the bin on the Saturday and not kept, and a woman of about seventy came into the queue and asked whether the paper on the nail was new and was told it was, and said it did not look it. Six lines on it, two done in pencil, both by the same person, one of them crossed out and rewritten and done again, **and nobody had taken a turn.** The boatyard crossing goes back on the Monday at ten in the morning — three new boards, three-inch nails out of the waste, an hour and a half, for nothing, *a channel is a channel and the tide is in it twice a day* — and Doreen Sankey does not speak to Milo at all, and the boatyard man has said that a man came into a yard and asked him a question about his own rooms and got the number wrong, and Nell Crale says he is not right, has told him so, has not been listened to, and that Milo should not go and argue with him about it because he would win. **Rue tells Milo at a quarter to ten, in nine words, and it is the only thing in ten days he did not see coming:** the rule says whoever took the last turn puts up the next one; nobody took a turn; nobody ever agreed to take a turn; we built a piece of paper with a column called TURN and not one person in this building put their name in it, and a woman of sixty did it twice in an afternoon because she was standing there and it was raining. **A fresh page goes up on the Monday at about ten, blank, all six lines, and the space at the foot blank, and it stays blank.** The argument about the space happens between ten and eleven in front of about nine people, and it is here in full because it is the only thing in eleven weeks the district has designed on purpose and got wrong on purpose. **Pell Harrow, seventeen, says it first:** *On the old one there was a floor with a name on it and nobody behind it, and I have thought about that name every day since the third of this month and I still do not know it, and that is the only thing in this district I have ever been frightened of, and it frightens me because the space is the frightening part. A name with nobody behind it, you can go and knock. A space, nobody knows what is supposed to go in it. A space is an invitation to whoever has got a pen and an afternoon.* Rue refuses a day in it, because she has had a hole with a name in it for six weeks and will not go up, and if you put a day in this one somebody will work out which building it is for inside a fortnight. **Pell supplies the second half: *Then leave it and put a line under it that says leave it. That is what I mean and I have been trying to say it for two minutes. Not left because nobody has come. Left because it says so.*** Nobody had thought of that, and Milo Rook, who has the bundle of four versions tied with twine and nothing else in his hands and has spent the morning on the only arithmetic anybody asked of him, reads out the line he wrote on Sunday: ***This line is left empty. It is not an oversight. It is for a person who was not here.*** And says in public that **he did not make the decision**, because the moment it has an author it is a document about a person, and it belongs to whoever writes in it, and it is the only line on that sheet that does not belong to him and the only one that is going to survive. **Rue writes it in at about eleven in her own hand, in the same pen as the rule, and reads it out twice.** **The woman on the fourth floor comes down at about four on the Monday afternoon, on her own, because there is nothing on the nail, and nobody has asked her and nobody goes up.** She is not named on the page; Rue has not said her name in a yard since the twenty-fourth and is not going to, and Milo has not asked and has written down that he has not asked. *You cannot have a column with a turn in it and not have anybody agree to a turn... and I have been on one of these since the twenty-fourth of last month and I have done nothing whatever.* She refuses to let the two pencil turns count as two turns — *that is the first lie anybody has told about me in nine years, and it went on a piece of paper in about ninety seconds.* Then: **it wants a space**, because the last time she came down six names had two of them touched by somebody and one line at the bottom with nothing on it and no words under it saying why — *You have left me a hole and you have not written down that it is a hole, and a hole with nothing written under it is not a hole anybody can leave alone. It is an invitation.* She writes **\*Second.\*** in the empty line, and under it, in her own hand: ***I was not here on the fourth of Sazur and I am not here now by anybody's asking. Take it or leave it.*** And she says she is going up, and that on Monday it will say something else, and that a line can be taken away from a person without asking the person, and she will not be the sixth. **She changes it to \*Third\* on Wednesday the twelfth. Nobody in the porch touches it except her, Milo is in the building, does not have the sheet in his hand, and writes that down as a fact about himself and not as a virtue.** The Field Book writes one line, five words, on the twelfth: ***An empty line is a door.*** He limits it on the facing page before acting, does not take the book near the porch until the fifteenth, pairs the ninth with the twelfth and the tenth with the thirteenth, and does not join them to the six-day and the nine-day.

### Chapter 49 — A Promise Made to One Person

Wednesday the 12th. **Mara Quill asks for the bundle in the porch at ten past twelve with about twelve people there**, and the asking takes four minutes and the answer takes nine. **How many copies are there:** three known, one went to a letting agent's office in Wragg Street, one into a house on the Terrace where a woman of sixty read her own name in a kitchen, one has not come back — and one came out of a man who lets rooms using a machine in a works office, and nobody in that building is going to be disciplined for it, and the man is not a criminal and has not been one. **Then she discloses the intake routing slip**, six years old, four sets of initials, three of people who have left, the fourth hers, in the box that means *I have seen that an address is affected and passed it on* — read four times since the fourth of Sazur, never read aloud to anybody, not even to him, and she will not read it aloud now, and that is what she is carrying into a room on the third of next month and it has nothing to do with him. **The decision, said in a yard because she is not going to do it in a corridor:** the promise of the twenty-ninth of Sazur — forty-four days — stands and is hers and **does not get wider**, because she will not be able to carry thirty people, and the day she promises a woman of sixty that nothing about her reaches her in writing is the day she has become a second Registry and she has a review about her own hand in three weeks' time. **What gets given away is the design.** A document is a document by the method it was made in, and he is the method, and there are now three copies of his design in the district and one of them has six names on it that four people did not agree to, and it will not be fixed by him going round explaining the intent. She puts a fifth version on the table: typed on Registry paper, which she is not supposed to use, told her clerk, and her clerk has written *unauthorised* in a book about it and initialled it. **Its columns are not his columns.** It is a sheet of turns and a sheet of hours somebody will not be there, and the difference between those two things is the whole of what has happened in that porch this month and it took her four days to see it and he had four days and did not see it. He admits he saw it on the ninth and did not write it down for two days because he did not want to be the man who said it, and she calls that the most honest sentence anybody has said to her in this district and worthless, and says she will use it anyway. **And she is not going to do one thing about the expiry**: no extension, no further minute, no conversion, no schedule, because a thing which is only ever four weeks is the only instrument she has ever made that could not be turned into a form, and she is not going to be the woman who finds out whether that was true. **The sheet is hers to hold on alternate weeks with Hettie Brann, and on the week it is on her side of the nail the rule at the foot is in her hand and not his, and that is the only difference and it is the whole difference.** Rue writes that sentence down; it is the only sentence in any version in Advocate Quill's handwriting. **Tomas Rusk comes down in a taxi at two o'clock**, at three and six, having been asked by telephone at nine in the morning because he is sixty-one and cannot be asked in a doorway. **His mechanic's sentence, one sentence, not written down in nine years:** *On the night of the closure the road shut in the middle of a run. Not at the end of it. In the middle. The crew came off the frontage to the pump house and the reach emptied, and a frontage with nobody on it is a road that is not being kept, and I have said that in about forty yards in this district and four of them were the district's own.* True, unsigned, available in a room, in the street, or in a book. *There is one thing I am going to want attached to the account itself, and I am not telling you what it is today, and I have not decided it... and I will not be asked about it twice, and I would like it noticed that I have said the sentence and held the condition in the same conversation and that is the whole of my character.* **Dr Sen stands at the edge of the awning in the rain** — the second time, since the thirtieth of Sazur — and makes him write the fifth finding down in his own hand, dated the sixteenth of Sazur, signed M.R., and takes the page for a locked drawer and will not give it back. **And then says the thing that makes the chapter:** *Now you have written it down, which means there is a piece of paper about a person and you are the man holding it, and I have made it worse, and I want that on the record in the only place either of us can put it, which is out loud, in a yard, at four o'clock, in the rain. And it will not help.* **Ends:** three lines in the book. The promise stands and there is nothing in it for him. The evidence for the surveyor's answer is a sheet he had in his hands for six minutes and did not read the names on, and a mechanic's sentence, and a woman on a fourth floor who came down twice, and he was in the building both times and did not have the paper in his hand either time. And there are five pairs in the book — six days, nine days, three days, three days — and he does not know what a pair is and is not going to find out this month.

### Chapter 50 — The Fifteenth

Thursday the 13th, Friday the 14th, Saturday the 15th. **There was no door.** The coal hatch side is behind a fence with a notice wired to it and a trench open in it, and there is a second gang four foot down and a man in a waterproof with a radio that is not working. Lena Marr stands at the **north** door at ten past one with Rue holding a count; the call comes out of the canyon; Milo counts twenty-nine and then thirty-one and then forty-five; **nothing asks.** She says *clear* anyway. **The plant does not stop and nobody in the trench puts their hands down.** She writes four lines on a card in her own hand and signs it and gives it to Hettie Brann, who reads it, and says she is not reading it and will hand it to the clerk at the gate: **the fourth Thursday is recorded as not done, because she was not at the threshold, and because a man in that trench has been saying *nobody told us* every working day since the twelfth.** **Friday the fourteenth, before noon.** Nine people came to watch the expiry, which nobody organised. The woman at the Board school door came with her list and said that she had asked Mara Quill in a corridor on the Thursday whether anything could be done in a room, and was told no, and that a thing which is only ever four weeks is the only instrument anybody has made in this case that could not be turned into a form — *and I think that is a horrible thing to be told by the only person in this case who has ever been able to do anything.* Mara Quill reads the minute out including the chair's fourth paragraph, at ten minutes to twelve, and says she is not going to say anything about it and is not going to stand there and be sad at them, and **is not going to put a fifth Thursday anywhere, and if anybody has a better idea than mine then it is not a room and I cannot help you with it.** **Pell Harrow, flat, having written it out first:** *I have been on the round since the twenty-ninth of Sazur... A service is a thing with a timetable and a man who can be fined, and this round is a man on a bicycle going to seven doors because a woman on the ground floor went out at eight this morning and could not get to the low end... You are not going to be able to make that into a route and you are not going to be able to make it into a form, and the only thing I can tell you about the fifteenth is that it is a Saturday and I will be here at eight and at four like I am here every day, and that is not an answer, and I know it is not an answer.* At noon it expires. **Eleven people in the yard on Saturday the fifteenth at eleven, the fewest since the third of this month.** Hettie Brann says the rain; Nell Crale says it is not the rain. **Then the woman at the school door comes down with a piece of paper that is not a list, from her letter box, addressed to *the person keeping the street*, with four other letters.** A copy of the fourth version: clean, legible, not forty per cent, the works' head, the four columns, the rule — **and the last line of the rule cut off in the copying, and the space beneath it filled in.** Rue reads it aloud: ***Owen Fitch***, and under it ***third floor***, in the same hand, with no words beside it. Not *not asked to*. **It is the fifth one anybody has seen.** The woman at the door says there is also one in a fourth-floor flat in Wragg Street, and a man who lets rooms has had one and shown it to two people, and she will not give the names, and that she has had a list in her hand for nine years and has never given it to anybody, and that this is not the same thing. **Owen Fitch comes down at ten past eleven, on his own, with a stick, and does not come into the porch.** *Read it again.* Then: **you cannot take my name off it.** To take his name off that paper somebody has to stand up in a room and say it was never ours — not that he did not agree, not that there is a rule at the foot. *And the minute on the twenty-first says the public name is not restored and that this arrangement protects nobody... and if a document that was never ours has got my name and a foreman's head on it and a rule at the foot, then either it is ours — and then the woman of sixty's crossed line is a line in a district document, and the man behind the boatyard's four words are four words in a district document, and the woman on the fourth floor who came down twice this month is a name nobody can say in a district document — or it was never ours, and then it comes off the nail, and on the fifteenth there is nothing.* He notes that Lena Marr has not said a word, which is the first time in a fortnight and is not a criticism. *I have been the man who stands at a turn on that street since before there was an order... So somebody in this city is writing down the people who keep this street, and it is not us, and we cannot stop it by being right, and I am not going to be on it, and I am not going to be on anything, and I would like somebody to explain to me how a man takes his own name off a thing he is not allowed to say was never his.* **Nobody explained it to him.** **Ends in the book, at 21.20 on the fifteenth.** Four Thursdays finished, three workings, and a fourth the named person recorded herself as not worked. The instrument stops at noon and nobody asked him to try, and he has written down that Mara Quill has said in a yard that she is not going to look for a fifth Thursday, *because in three weeks there will be thirty people in this district who will assume that somebody is working on it.* The thing that came instead is a name in a space, a fence, a mechanic's sentence with a condition, a foreman's certificate, a plate, a street under six inches, the Bench of the twenty-first of October that he sat in and did not speak at, a Bench after that which he is not going to be in, and **the sixth of next month, which is twenty-one days away.** Three sources of witnessing, and all three are documents with his method in them, and the only one that holds is Rue's, and Owen's promise is the only thing in it that is his. He does not join Pell's four ticking columns to the copy in the same paragraph, and says why: *joining them would be me deciding that they are the same, and a man who decides two things are the same in a book is a man who has started making the case instead of the record.* Eight remembered routes, no ninth, the line home walked twice and correct, the token cold, five pairs in the book and a sixth of one day looked at and not written.

## Volume 01, Batch 0006 (Chapters 51–60)

### Chapter 51 — Twenty-One Days

Sunday the sixteenth, Monday the seventeenth of the month. **Orrin Sable announces the test date and the altered Line 7 timetable together, twenty-one days early, out loud, in a yard, standing on a trestle table, to about sixty people, and says before he says anything else that he is doing it early so that nobody can say afterwards he did it quietly** — the same move as the plate and the same reason, and it is the second time in eleven weeks that man has been right about something that has nothing to do with engineering. The works notice: **the final test on Saturday the sixth of December**; the last reach laid, levelled and certified **on the second, third, fourth and fifth**, on ground recorded as settled on the first; access by the north path only; signed for the works by R. Rennick; **entered under the standing instruction of the office of the Prefect, ref. 11/D/6**, which is the first time that reference has been on a hoarding and the second time it has been anywhere. The Line 7 amendment of the first of the month, in the same notice: the Alder Quay–Bridge Street loop withdrawn and not reinstated, the diversion permanent, **nine seconds taken out at the Alder Quay interchange while the reach is made level beneath it**, and **the count automatic at both the Ferry Road turn and the interchange, with no staff turn at either** — so the count has nowhere to be handed at all. He corrects a district story on the spot: **the culvert has never been sealed**, it has been wound down to eighteen inches of sill since the sixth of Sazur, and a man in this district has been telling them otherwise to make his case easier. He says the gate will be driven out to full flow at about twenty past six that morning and returned to a working aperture afterwards and there is a form for it. **Barrow comes on foot on the Monday at a quarter past ten, to the yard, which he has never done, and minutely minutes it in four lines in his own hand** — the test date; the timetable alteration; **a temporary arrangement is not a reason to vary an order, in advance, in a file, and that having been said in advance it is very difficult to unwrite**; and the district's position that a count is a person, *recorded as stated, and not adopted, and there is a difference*. He then gives the district, in advance, the Crown Heights refusal of the twenty-ninth of last month and its fourth paragraph, because the department wrote down that it knew the district's arrangement had expired, and says he is telling them because they would find it out in a week and *the answer you have not brought with you is the one that gets minuted*. The yard's decision takes nine minutes: **no fifth Thursday**, because a fifth Thursday is a form and it dies on a date somebody in Crown Heights picks out of a diary; **no going to the Bench before the sixth**, because the classification is in the file and the department will find it in ninety seconds and after that the only two sentences anybody hears are ours and his; **and the count**, and the sentence that a count is a person saying a number to a person, and that if the count is a machine then something in this district is going to have to be a machine, and every one of them has spent six weeks building things so that no one has to be. **Rue stops him at the gate and says the sentence the batch is built on: nobody is going to ask you for a passage, and it is not going to happen, and on the sixth of December there is not going to be one person at a door who has agreed to be there** — and the reason is not a rule and not a woman with a clipboard, it is that on the fourth and the sixth of October and the eighteenth and the twenty-first and the twenty-sixth of Sazur he got it done, and on the tenth of November he stood under an awning with his hands empty and the door was shut, and the reason he did not go in that day was that there was nobody at a door. **Mara Quill's six items at about nine in the evening in a front room above a chandler's**, of which she can do none on the day: she cannot speak for a household, she will not produce the sheet, she can call three witnesses and the unsworn account has to go in as a document and not as a man, the order's safety case is the easiest thing on the list and will be the hardest thing to do in three weeks because it is the argument she would make if the order were right, the four Thursdays and their expiry, and **the dead man** — *on the sixth of December there is going to be an event, and an event makes a record that has to be written down by somebody, and if I am standing in front of the only record anybody has of a man who died in a pump house six years ago, then the record gets written, and it gets written in a room I am in. That is why we are going on the eighth of December and not on the sixteenth of November.*

### Chapter 52 — Four Conditions

Wednesday the nineteenth to Saturday the twenty-second. **The residents split over whether to cross at all, over three days, and nobody caused it and nobody stopped it.** Four positions, and the fourth is the one the resolution could not have been written without. **Nell Crale, sixty-three, Bridge Street, four rooms over a shop with a cellar that is the only cold store within four streets:** never — *I have been on that reach in my life… and I am not putting my house and my ice and my brother's boy into a hole to see what it does, and I would rather be thought badly of than be that woman, and I have been that woman once.* **Verrick Assal, fifty-eight, not a witness and in writing:** the works side, on the low end, with a hard hat and a man who can shout, where he would be in a photograph, *and a photograph of me is a document and a document can be produced, and I would rather the document be one I chose and stood in deliberately and where there is a foreman's name on it as well as mine.* **The woman of sixty from the second landing, unnamed:** a street — *I am not going to be a person who is frightened into a decision by a timetable.* **And Owen Fitch, from under the awning, unasked, in a fourth position nobody knew was there:** nobody should go in, and the reason should be written down in the district's own book — *a paper that says a person went in can be used against them and a paper that says a person did not go in can be used for them, and there is one of those and there is not one of the other, and every one of us has spent six weeks only ever writing down the second kind.* **Lena Marr's notice** on the porch wall at four o'clock — *SUPERVISED RETURN — Sat 6th Dec, from 11 a.m. / Anybody who wants to go goes with somebody who has agreed to go with them / Nobody goes alone. Nobody is counted in by the works. Nobody's name goes on a list / There is no charge and there is no form and there is nothing to sign* — with a strip of masking tape over the last two lines, taken off a minute later in front of about twenty people, because she had written the two lines first and then written over them and wanted both seen. **She hands it to Rue and does not run it.** Rue refuses to be handed a piece of paper and a photograph of her in a doorway, and puts it on the round instead: out at eight, in at four, first name and floor and nothing else, *and if you say it to anybody else then you are on somebody else's thing, and I do not want it.* **The woman of sixty takes the hours**, and writes the word *clear* on the round, and Rue says she will tear it up if she writes *street*. Nineteen names on the Thursday night, twenty-six by Friday, one of them **Tomas Rusk** with *I cannot get there* in his own hand, and one name written four times in the second column. **Owen Fitch's refusal, on the Saturday morning at the foot of the outside stairs, in the rain, to four people** — he will not be the turn, will not hold the sheet, train anybody, be in charge of a table, sign anything, keep a list, or be the reason a thing works. *If the return happens and it wants a turn, it goes without one, and a return without a turn is a return with a hole in it, and the hole is where I am, and it will look like a mistake and it will be deliberate, and I will not be asked about it afterwards.* He discloses, for the first time in six years, **that he has not been on that street since the night of the closure and did not go back when the order came**, and offers one thing and refuses to say why and will not be thanked: on the sixth, in daylight, once, at the ground-floor door, he will not say the word and will not go in and will be gone before the round is in. **Tomas Rusk's condition, in a taxi at two o'clock on the Thursday, and he says the no-asking-twice thing first so that nobody can say he sprang it:** it is not for him and it is not for his oath. **The road is not the lane.** *A true sentence about a place that does not exist can be used against a man who did not know it did not exist, and the frontage between the pump house and the low end has a name in the district's own drainage book and in the works' own schedule and has been a maintained frontage since before I was at the pump house. Bridge Reach.* What it costs him is the name — *I have asked you for one thing in six years and it is a piece of paper with my name at the foot of it asking for a road, and that paper is a record, and a record is a thing that can be produced… and I have decided that a road is kept by somebody or it is not kept at all, and I have been a man on a payroll for thirty years and I know what a payroll is for* — and it cannot be taken back. **Jo Lask's interval guarantee, in the yard on the Friday at eleven, and the price is his own name in a printed rule book:** three terms, a count recorded as a count and not a signal, no automatic replacement without the running time restored in full, no box filled by a person except under a published standing instruction naming the officer who issued it; and against it, **the extra Bellwether stop is not printed on the public timetable, the diversion panel carries no name, and the district makes no claim against the amendment of the first of the month in any court, forum or review.** *I have said no to a man three times and yes to a rule book, and a man can be argued with and a rule book cannot, and that is why the rule book is better and that is why it is worse* — and the paper ends his inquiry, because a man who has asked a department to publish his name as a guarantee is not aggrieved. He also offers, unasked, to take the count by hand on the sixth at the place where a car comes out of a run and there is nobody in the box, and not to write it down. **Ends in the book:** four conditions, none of them his, and *a mechanic of sixty-one has said a thing out loud that nobody has said in this case and I am going to be the man who wrote it down and it is going to be in a file in Crown Heights for a hundred years: a road is kept by somebody or it is not kept at all.*

### Chapter 53 — In His Own Hand

Monday the twenty-fourth to Friday the twenty-eighth. **He finds it on the twenty-fourth at about ten past eight in the morning, standing in his own kitchen, and it takes him four attempts to understand that it has happened, because on the first attempt he thought he had opened to the wrong page.** The Field Book is a foolscap book with a limp spine and the entries are in date order going up the left-hand side and there are two hundred and eleven of them and there is a fortnight of the Long Rain summer of his second year that he copied out four times because the first three were wrong. The oldest surviving thing in the book is nine lines, in his own hand, with the loop he has never been able to stop making on the capital L: *Out the door. Left. Eleven steps to the hall, the fourth one loose, take the second stair not the first. Down to the front. The lamp on the third step goes on the left and you wait four seconds for it. Along the front to the corner, do not cut the corner. Past the chandler, and the gate, and then the wall with the paint on it, and along by the water, and it is nine minutes and I have done it since I was nineteen.* **The page is there, his hand is there, the ink on either side is the same ink, and it is clean to the bottom of the line.** *This is not fading. I am not going to write down what I think that is.* He does not tell anybody until the Tuesday, and Rue's whole contribution in nine words is *two things* — he is going to be the worst man in this district for a week and everybody will think he has lost his nerve — **and do not say it to Mara Quill, because she has a review on her own hand on the third of next month and if she hears that the surveyor's route has gone she will spend it being kind to you instead of reading four paragraphs about her own initials.** **Dr Sen's number, in a dispensary with four people in the queue, and it is the batch's argument in one line:** there are **eight** remembered routes and there is a page, and they are not one thing, and the difference is the only interesting thing about either of them. A remembered route comes back when somebody marks it; a written line does not, because nobody can mark a page in a book you own, and the only hand that could mark it is the one that wrote it — **so somebody else is going to have to write it, from their own feet, in their own hand, on his page, and it will be done badly, and I am telling you on the Tuesday of the twenty-fifth of November so that when it happens you cannot say nobody told you it was coming and that you were not in a position to refuse it.** **Rue's eleven words on the Wednesday: *We do not ask him to remember anything. We walk him to the door and we walk him out of the door. Every time. Both ways. And it goes in the round and it is not a favour and it is not a rota and I am not writing it down, and if anybody finds him at the wrong door at the wrong time they are to say come with me and not where were you.*** And nineteen years in this city, and he has walked into the wrong building twice in six weeks on time with somebody waiting. **The marks, by other people, and every one of them unasked:** the woman of sixty from the second landing comes down on her own on the Wednesday at eleven and finds him at the wrong door and says four seconds — *you have been at that door for about a minute and a half and you have not gone into it, and in about another minute you were going to* — and does not explain it and says nothing else about it and is gone by five past; **and the ninth route is not this chapter: it is the seventh of December, when Ola Petrossian, twenty-three, four months in the job and never been past the tram steps, gives him at the door a card with a hundred and one steps and a date on it — *I am writing it on a card because you are going to lose that and not the pier, and I do not know how I know that and you are not going to tell me***; and on the Friday night at half past ten he walks home and up his own stairs and puts a chalk arrow on the inside of his own front door at about four feet with the date under it, which is a gate-man's practice and is not his. **Mara Quill's two things, on the Wednesday evening: the answer to the department's request for a schedule of persons resident at the withdrawn alignment is NO, and she puts the reason in a corridor and a yard — *any schedule I put in that box is a schedule I will be producing in a room in a year's time to a department that wants a list of people, and this department has had a list of people since the fourth of Sazur and has never once used it* — and she says the trap out loud, that an empty box with her own name and own date under it is the only reason she can stand in a room and ask for the order to be suspended, *if I had put three hundred names in it on the fourth I would have nothing to say on Monday at all*, and she asks him in advance to interrogate her about it in the corridor on the Sunday and be angry.** **Danner, on the Friday morning at half past ten: the soft lens has stopped, and the word is *there*, and he says he is not going to be the man who says a number like that twice.** Rennick brings him back at full rate for five days from Monday the first to the Friday, against a four-day window that ran from the second to the fifth, so the day he is paid for and does not use is the first, the day the ground stopped — the second reset of his review in eight weeks — and Danner gives the whole of it, including why he will write the certificate on the fourth or the fifth and not the first, and what will be in the *alternative considered* box: **the certification is made in the fourteenth day of a standard deviation in the readings at the trench, and I will not be able to say in a room that I stood behind it, and I am certifying it because the level has to be certified by a man and the only man is me** — and why he is writing it twice: *on the form it is a thing said by the certifying officer in the box that only takes it, and it goes into the file with the level, and it cannot be separated from the level, and anybody who takes the level out of that file has to take the sentence out with it… I have been told by two people in six weeks that a document is a thing that can be produced, and I have decided that the correct answer to that, for a man who has to put his name on a level over four feet of water in a soft bank, is to produce it first, with the sentence attached, while I still have something to lose by doing it. Which I do not, and that is why this is free.* **The fifth check at a door on the Friday at ten to two, and the first with no named person:** Rue declines to be one, Verrick says *ask it again*, and **Doreen Sankey takes it with Rue's count and Rue's terms — wait until it stops, and then say it, and say it again from the other side of the door, and nobody counts.** It asked, it was answered, nothing happened. **Ends:** nine remembered routes, and eight of them were there that morning, and the ninth is a page and not a route and he has been near to counting them as one thing twice and both times he stopped; and the first stage is not worse, eight is eight, six of the eight walked this week and none lost and he does not understand that; and *it is not persistent inside this month… and I am not going to decide that this month, and Dr Sen has told me in a dispensary that in a month I will be standing in a room saying the ninth, and the ninth is a page.*

### Chapter 54 — Full Flow

Saturday the sixth of December. **The gate comes out to full flow at twenty past six** and Milo is in the compound at ten to six, standing on the north path, because nobody asked him to be there and because the coal hatch side has been open again since the twentieth of November and a man who cannot find a door is allowed to stand near forty others. The culvert is a brick structure the width of a cart, built into the foot of the embankment in 1889 and rebuilt in 1931, with a sluice gate on a screw stem that takes four men and about an hour and a half if they are fresh; **it has been wound down to eighteen inches of sill since the sixth of Sazur, by hand, in front of witnesses, because a full seal would back water into the works zone and the low streets, and it has been in that state for sixty-one days, and the district has spent most of them being told by a man it liked that it had been shut.** The form: eighteen inches before, **eleven feet four inches clear** after, returned to twenty-four inches on completion, *and the culvert to be left silted and re-aligned and gated, and the re-alignment to be carried out under a separate instruction*, and the *alternative considered* box filled in Danner's own hand because he will not leave a space. Petts says *nobody told us* on his own initiative and Danner says *I have heard you* and Milo writes down that he did not ask him to stop. **The test is at nine and it is not a spectacle, and the answer at about twenty past nine is yes** — the clay held, the wall held, the low point came up on a schedule level, the third pump on Bridge Street can be taken off, and **about forty gallons a minute has been coming off the district's own drainage for eleven weeks by means of a fire engine with a man volunteering on it that the works has paid for out of a petty float it had no authority to spend and has not told anybody.** **At ten past eleven the deck moved:** the west side of the interchange goes nine inches out of true over about thirty feet in one movement, and nothing comes down, because the tower was rebuilt in November on a new pier with a note in the works file about differential founding. Jo Lask was driving through it, came out with nobody hurt, and got out and stood in the middle of it with his arms down, *because he has been in the middle of four things this year and he is the man who stands in them.* Four through Dr Sen's treatment room by half past two, and *You will want to be useful in about an hour. Do not be.* **At ten to two the interval comes out of the canyon, and there are about four hundred people on and around the interchange, and the gate is at full flow in the sight of eleven people, and nobody thought about that, and the aperture went back to twenty-four inches at 14.06, fourteen minutes after the call that asked went out.** The second call at eight minutes to two, the plant stopped, twenty-nine seconds which is the shaft and thirty-one which is the street. **It asked, and about four hundred people answered.** Not a chorus — *what happened is that a check went out on a reach on which there were four hundred people, and a check is a person saying a space has been cleared before the next turn, and there were people standing in spaces, and a space with a person in it has been described as clear by the person in it, in about four hundred separate and entirely ordinary ways, in under two seconds, by people who were on a tram platform, or in a trench, or on a fill, or in a doorway four streets away and one of them said it twice.* **And a woman of sixty-eight was not in this district on the sixth of December and did not say anything on the sixth of December, and nobody went up three flights of stairs about her.** Then: **the reach does not take the check and does not refuse it; it takes it and uses it.** The two feet of settlement, the eighteen inches in a day, the nine inches before it, were all *going down* — a lens settling, a surface made level across a place which had been two surfaces for six years, and a man with a spirit level and a bubble in the middle had been right about the bubble. **This is the two surfaces becoming one and the one not being level.** It is in four paragraphs of a joint finding made on the ninth, and the third paragraph is that sentence, and the fourth is that a reach which has taken a check in this way **did not return to its former state within the period of the finding and was not expected to.** A rail up through four feet of fill and over; a man of forty-nine gets a hand up about a fifth of a second before it and says he did not decide to; **the service door comes off its fixings at about half past two and goes into the works zone eleven feet from where it was, on its side, with the four bosses up.** The plant restarted at about six minutes past; the second call at three; **the third call at four, and it did not ask**, and there is a mechanic of sixty-one with a possible answer who has not been asked for it. Nobody drowned, nobody was killed, **eleven hurt, four through the room**, the low streets under nine inches by half past two against a district flood line of nine, a woman of sixty-three with a hundredweight of ice out of a cellar four inches deep at one o'clock and four at four, alone, telling nobody until the Monday, *and she is not in a yard and is not the face of anything.* **And a crew of nineteen and a sluice and a board of four questions stood on the north side and watched the wall not fail, and wrote the time in a box, and the wall did not fail, and that is the whole of the engineering, and it is the reason the seam went where it went.** He comes off the path at ten past four, goes in by the wrong door, and Doreen Sankey is on the second landing and tells him so, and Milo says he has known that for six weeks and has been waiting for somebody to say it so that he could stop pretending it was a coincidence, and Doreen says *it is a coincidence, and you are not to tell anybody I said anything about it, and go up to the fourth floor and be in your own flat by twenty past four.* There is a chalk arrow on the inside of his own front door, five weeks old, and it is the only thing in his own life that had not moved. **At 21.20, at the bottom of a page that already has the day's times on it, with a line under it and no date under the line, the only undated thing in two hundred and eleven entries: *Pinned.***

### Chapter 55 — It Does Not Close

Saturday evening, Sunday the seventh. **The word, with the definition and the limits on the facing page, dated, which is what he does**: it is not a new kind of anything, it is not a remnant type, it is not a new failure state, **it is a condition, and the condition is a seam that is being held open instead of sealed**; it is held open because the order is still in force and every timetable, gate, classification, notice and record in this district that touches the reach still points at it, *and a place that everything in a city points at is a place that is being fed*; **it is not going to close while the order stands, because there is nothing on the far side of it to close against**; it is not a collapse, because a collapse has an end and this has not; *it may give you one more of these and then stop giving you any, and then there is a street with nobody living on it and a man in a trench on the other side of a fence who goes to work there every morning for the rest of his life*; **a pinned seam is quiet, not healed** — written down on the sixth of December so that on some Tuesday in February nobody in this district can say they thought it was mended. And the mechanism, and it is not one thing and none of it is magic and every part of it is a document: the culvert went to full flow at the request of the district flood stage, the water went where a hundred and forty feet of frontage had been named and maintained and then withdrawn and then made level, the timetable took nine seconds out of a junction at eleven o'clock, two boxes have no person in them, and about four hundred people answered a check that was meant for one man at a door. **Survey Enforcement comes at a quarter past six on Sunday morning**, and Cass Ardery asks for the word because his form has a box that says *nature of hazard* and it has said *minor seam, unstable* since the eleventh and *minor* was true in a different ground; he reads neither instruction on the reach, *I am not going to do that here, you have four hundred people on a street and I have a form case and there is a rule about not reading documents to a crowd and I did not make the rule and I am going to obey it*; he asks three questions and wants six people to answer them and not the surveyor; he says he will write what he is told and eat it if it is untrue now, and he has thought about that for two days. **Tomas Rusk on a telephone at seven in the morning, with a clock on the table and his hand two inches from it**, put on the line by a woman of sixty from the second landing who told him it could not be done and that nothing in this district was arranged and who let him try anyway. *There was a length of ground between the pump house and the low end that was worked by the district… On the second of that June the crew came off the frontage to the pump house. That is the whole of it. And from the moment the crew came off, that length of ground was not a road. It was a gap. And a gap is not a road and it is not a hazard and it is not a street. It is a place where a road used to be, and the difference between those is a crew.* Then: **you had a crew on it on the sixth of December at ten to two, and that is the condition I have been describing since June six years ago, and paragraph six says the crew change is sounded from the gantry and the paragraph above the one that was taken out says the frontage is at all times a service access and not a public route.** He says it once, will not say it again, and hangs up, and says the call came in at about four minutes past seven and he waited for it to stop. Ardery asks for the word; **Milo says *pinned* and gives the definition, and gives the mechanism back to the man he has been arguing with since the sixteenth of October — *a believed route is a used route, and he was right, and I have been half right, and the half I have been half right about is that the map is a document and the work is a crew, and it was the crew that kept a road, and there was a crew on that reach at ten to two yesterday afternoon because the form says the aperture goes back to twenty-four inches at fourteen minutes past two.*** Ardery writes it on the back of his own classification, reads it back, crosses out his own paragraph in ink, dates it and initials it — *and I am going to say to you the thing nobody in that building will say to you: if it is pinned while the order stands, then the only way to unpin it is the order, and I have carried the instruction *a seal* since the fourth of October and I have used it nine times, and there is not one single person in this district who is going to unpin that reach by being right in a yard.* **And the triad accounting, once, in his mouth, as an argument about a document and not about a category: the order took the name off the index in the first week and closed the routes in the first week, and those are two things a document did and neither is in dispute, and in six years nobody has ever put a person on that reach — a thing a document does twice is a fact and a thing a document never does is an oversight, and an oversight is the only one of the three a different department in a different building can correct in about a year.** He agrees, on the condition that it stays on a truck, and Ardery writes it down and says a report is a thing that can be produced and this sentence is going to be in a file with his name on it, **and refuses the word *triad*, because he has read it twice in the file and cannot define it and *a term in a report that nobody in the report can define is a term that gets removed by the next man*.** **Orrin Sable, at about half past eight, makes the order and unmakes it in four minutes in public:** the crossing closes at nine — *and then I am not closing a crossing. I am telling forty people that the only way out of the only place they can reach is through the same place… Close the crossing and the reach becomes a road. A road is kept by somebody. And a road with forty people on it at ten to two is a crew, and I have got nine men and a form case and no authority, and I would be putting the district's own sentence into the reach against the order of the district, and I have spent six years watching a man do that to about four hundred people and I am not going to be the second one.* And not kindness: paragraph six, still in the file, never withdrawn, *and there is nothing in this city that has ever been recorded as a person in one.* He puts a woman of sixty-three on a wall with a whistle who is not on his payroll, says he is going to Crown Heights on Monday to ask for a variation he has not got and will be refused, and says that on the sixth of December the district goes into a room and asks for the order to be suspended **and I am going to be the man in that room who does not argue, and I would like it noted that I said that out loud, in a yard, at about ten o'clock on a Sunday morning, and that on Saturday I said the opposite about a fence.** **The chalk.** Idar laid a double arrow at about eleven on Saturday night and it was there three hours and gone at about two in the morning, and Rue says it in four sentences without softening: eleven at night to two in the morning, gone, and *a thing that holds for eleven weeks of afternoons and goes at two o'clock at night is not a broken thing, it is a working shift, and it holds for a working shift and it does not hold overnight, and nobody in this district is going to be asked to hold one overnight, and that includes the boy, and I want that said on the record because I know exactly what a yard does with a rule when the pressure is on and it is the first rule anybody drops.* Nobody had asked Pell, and he says so: *it was me on Saturday at eleven… and I am not going to do it again tonight because it does not do anything at two o'clock in the morning.* **And at about four in the afternoon Rue hands him a sheet with a heading, two columns, TURN and DONE, and the rule at the foot in her own hand — *That is a map I can read. And you don't hold it and you don't say the word in it and you are not the reason it works.***

### Chapter 56 — A Map With a Rule at the Foot

Sunday the seventh, from about half past three in the afternoon. **The written request, four lines, the fifth of its kind:** count Rue, outside, from the north path; walker Doreen Sankey, *who is not my chalk, who can stop it, and who is told now that if he says the word it stops and nobody is to argue with him on the way back, including me*; nine minutes; and the other one, which is not about the reach — *I am going to have to be walked to the front door of this building and up my own stairs afterwards, by somebody, because I cannot find it, and I have written that down and I am asking for it the same way I ask for the rest of it, and if it is refused I will not come in.* **Rue says she has got a thing at half past two and cannot do the count, says so at the table with thirty people in it, and does not fix it in advance** — *that is the count and it has to be outside and it has to be somebody who is not going to be affected by what is in there, and you are going to have to ask, and I am not going to be the woman who decides who watches the clock while a man goes into a hole.* It takes nine minutes and ends with **Hettie Brann**, who says no three times before she says a different yes, and who will not read a sheet, will not come in, will not be asked about anybody who comes out, and whose reason is that the day this district needs a count and there is nobody in it to keep one, that is the day it stops being a district, *and I have been on that counter nineteen years and I have watched it stop twice and I am not watching it stop a third time while I am standing up.* She keeps the tally in her apron pocket before anybody asks her to. **In at 16.06, out at 16.35, twenty-nine minutes inside**, from the low end of the reach, nine minutes stated, four conditions signed in October all satisfied. **Ola Petrossian walks him to the ground-floor door at ten to four and does not come in and does not go up**, and says the one thing. **The token was not cold.** For about four minutes it was the temperature of a thing taken out of a coat and put back, and the reach went quiet the way a room goes quiet, **and the negative space in the front of it lined up with the made ground from the pump house to the low end, a hundred and forty feet, exactly.** *I did not use it, and I had about four minutes in which I could have, and I did not, and I did not say why out loud to anyone, and there is one reason and it is not a rule.* And the reason: **if a street is open because of something he can take out of his coat, then every one of the twenty-six names on Rue's sheet and the forty who came out of the reach that morning and the man of forty-nine standing nine feet behind him are standing in a place a man's mother opened, and the three answers in this district are three people, and they stop being three answers the moment one of them is a thing I can take out of my pocket and put back. I have spent six weeks giving away a sheet of paper and four versions of it and a rule at the foot and the design itself, and if I open that with this then every bit of it was a way of keeping the key.** *There is a man of forty-nine on this reach who got his hand up a fifth of a second before a rail went over and he did not decide to, and he is not a token.* **He does not draw anything.** He reads the rule at the foot aloud on the reach in a voice that carried about forty feet, because the whole of the argument of six weeks is that a map with no rule at the foot is an invitation to whoever has got a pen. **Then the three things, in eleven minutes, in daylight, with about four hundred people inside a quarter of a mile.** One: the order's public safety claim, given as it is on the page — the finding that is not in the file any more, the replacement page filed eleven days late, the Crown Heights withdrawal reference that is not a district reference, and paragraph six, which is still in the file and is why there are forty people in a reach with no way out of it. Two: **the missing worker record, four sentences** — *there is a dead maintenance worker called Jonas Venn, one loss recorded, no cause of death, no next of kin, with a relocation due and the word pending on it… he died in a second-stage main in flood season in a real accident, and the accident is not in this reach, and he is not in this reach, and there is not one thing anybody has ever said in this district that puts him in this reach, including me, in eleven weeks… in about a fortnight a clerk is going to produce a piece of paper and the piece of paper is going to say that a hazard at the frontage and nobody at the frontage were the same sentence, and they were not, and the cost of six years of this has fallen on a man of forty-one behind a boatyard and a woman of sixty on a landing and a mechanic of sixty-one with a knee.* Three: the count, two hundred and fifty-two in fifty-five rooms in eleven streets, on the thirty-first and the first, on doorsteps, without knocking,
