by six people **whose names he is not going to read out because he has been told by a doctor not to be left alone with a piece of paper about a person**, twenty-one more than the schedule, thirty-three rooms on no list, one of them behind a boatyard on the Ferry Road side. **And Doreen Sankey makes him say the fourth thing**, which is on the record: *A cartographer went over the low end of this street on the eighteenth of October with a lamp and a piece of paper and a woman who was carried down in a laundry bag… and I have been on six crossings of this reach and this is the seventh, and I am not going in as a man with a piece of paper in his hand and I am not going in for Lark Street and I am not going to be the reason this place is kept, and if this works today then it works because there are about nine people holding it up and I am one of the nine and I am the one who can be replaced.* Out at 16.35, and Hettie Brann says *and* four thirty-five, and writes it on the back of her hand. **At six o'clock, in a yard of about thirty-one, he reads the crossing out because Dr Sen makes him and has made him five times: seventh, Sunday the seventh, 16.06 to 16.35, twenty-nine minutes, Mr Sankey, Mrs Brann, nine minutes stated, four conditions satisfied, and the fourth condition is the clinic in the morning and I have not missed one in eleven weeks.** And the cost: the ninth remembered route, **the hundred and one steps of the stair tower at the Alder Quay interchange**, given at a door that afternoon on a card with a date by a nurse of twenty-three, *and I am not going to say anything else about it, because you do not want me to and because in a fortnight you are going to want to know whether it is a cost or a habit and it will still not be knowable, and there is a word for a page you keep re-reading and it is not cost.* **The Field Book, one line, and the limit on the facing page before he does anything else with it: *Nothing on the reach. Nothing in the building. Nothing on the way out.* The token was warm for about four minutes and I did not use it… and then nothing for eleven days, and I have written six dates down and I have not paired them, and I am not going to. Eleven days is not a pair. I looked at it twice.**

### Chapter 57 — What People Chose

Sunday the eighth of the month, Monday the eighth of December. **Owen Fitch stood at the ground-floor door on Saturday the sixth at about eleven in the morning for about four minutes and said nothing and did not go in, and he was gone before the round was in, and nobody thanked him, and he has not been thanked, and there is no part of this that is going to be a wound that somebody in this district heals.** It is one line in the record of the seventh: *At about eleven on the sixth, at the ground-floor door, coal hatch side, in daylight, for about four minutes: a person who did not say the word and did not enter. Not a turn. Not counted.* Rue saw and did not go over; Doreen Sankey saw and said one word, *there*, and then said he was not going to say another word about it. **The record Owen Fitch asked for in November — a line saying that forty-one people declined to cross a withdrawn alignment on a works test — exists and is not the record he asked for:** the line above carries no figure at all, Rue has written down in her own book that he asked for a number, was not given one, and was not going to be given one, and **nobody explained his position to him and nobody thanked him.** **Nobody in the district is in charge of the sixth, the seventh or the eighth, and the reason is in four hands and the four hands are not Lena Marr's.** Rue runs it. The woman of sixty runs the hours. Doreen Sankey runs the way in and out. **Verrick Assal sits on the wall from nine until it stops with a spade across his knees and counts heads going out, and gives the number to nobody and has not been asked for it.** Lena Marr's part is a notice, a list of twenty-six names in Rue's hand, and two hundred yards of corridor at a school door; at about six on the Monday evening a woman of about seventy came into the yard and said *Which of you is in charge of this*, and Lena said *Nobody*, and the woman said *Good* and went home. **The woman on the fourth floor comes down on the Sunday morning at about eight, before anybody else is on the path, on her own, with her coat on and a bag, and nobody had gone up for her and nobody had sent for her.** She went to the foot of the made reach and did not go up it. *I am not going into a street… you would come out into a road with a made surface on it that a foreman certified on Friday with his name on it, and I have read that form, because I read everything on every wall in this building for nine years, and it is not a street and it is not going to be a street, and nobody in this district is going to be able to make it one by wanting it.* Rue refuses to say her name, having carried it on a piece of accountancy paper since the twenty-fourth of last month. **She walked it: ninety minutes, from about half past ten to about twelve, on the frontage, footpath side, at about two miles an hour, with about thirty people behind her at eleven feet and not one of them touching her arm**, and Doreen Sankey at eleven feet in front and Rue at eleven feet behind, which was a rule Rue made up in four seconds and told about nine people and did not explain. She wrote three things and would not read them and would not have them read: *I walked it. Once, in daylight, front to back, and I did not go in and I am not going in. / I am not coming down again for anybody. Not for the sheet, not for the door, not for a turn, not for a hearing, and not for a thing that is in a good coat. I have said that four times now and this is the fourth and I am not saying it a fifth. / The road is not mine and I have not claimed it and nobody is to put my name on it. If it is on anything that is my name on it, take it off in front of me.* **Nobody thanked her. Milo Rook was not in the yard and arranged to be about four hundred yards away and has written that down as a fact about himself and not as a decision he made.** **The twenty-six go on the seventh and the eighth, arranged, in daylight, with a count and a walker and a rule at the foot of somebody else's sheet, and that is the whole difference between the sixth and the seventh.** Eleven said no and did not go. Three said no and asked to be counted anyway and were counted from outside, and Rue allowed it in four seconds. **Five went out through the ground-floor door to Cedar Court. Six went back into the reach to the first forty feet and said the word. One walked it.** **Somebody says *then go and ask the old woman on the third floor*, and Rue says no first, before anybody else, and Lena Marr gives the real reason in a yard: *She is not ours. If we go up and ask her, then the district's answer on the eighth of December is a woman of sixty-eight, and every one of us in this yard goes back to being a thing that happens next to a woman of sixty-eight… and the report will be true, and it will be the end of it, because a report that says a person remained is a report that says a place is kept by one person, and one person is not a district and one person is what this whole street was closed over. And I have not been up those stairs either, and I live in that building, and that is not a thing I am proud of and I am not going to pretend it is.*** Nobody went up. Six people in that yard noticed who had not said it and who had, and not one of them said a word about it afterwards. **Dr Sen takes nine people through the treatment room on the eighth between about ten and about six**, and says in a yard of about eleven that the thing she wants said is *that a person going into a place that is not safe is not an injury. It is a decision, and it is often a good one, and I will not have that turned into an argument about a seam.* **Mara Quill's return document**: Registry paper, her hand, four pages, about nine hundred words, a column headed *the choice of the person* with no number in it and the condition that if a number is put in it the whole of the page is void; five conditions in her hand and not in a box, including *no person who went is to be asked to go again, and no person who did not go is to be asked why, in any proceeding, hearing, or room, by the district or by anyone acting for it*; ten entries in the people's own words, twenty-six names, no floor, no number, and the four pages read out on the Monday at ten past one by Rue to about nineteen people. **The map is deliberately incomplete and Rue says why in the yard at ten past five on the Tuesday**: four lines have something on them, two are crossed out — one because a woman of sixty-three on a wall is the correct use of a wall and if it is written it becomes a job, and one because it is hers, *and if I am on this then anybody can lean on me* — and the deliberate space at the foot is **still empty**, and went through both days empty, and Rue has written down in her own book that she checked it four times and has not written down who checked it and why, *because I did not check it and I did not ask anybody to.* **The eighth crossing, Monday the eighth, in at 09.06 and out at 09.33, twenty-seven minutes, count held by Pell Harrow with a counter and no words for twenty-seven minutes** — *the quietest half hour of my year and I am not going to say anything else about it.* Read aloud in a yard of about eleven with Dr Sen's tally, which now has eight lines on it, and she makes him say the number out loud *because in a year somebody is going to ask you how many times you went in*, and she corrects the finding out loud: the condition is not the fifth finding and never helped; **the condition is that you get walked to a door and walked out of a door, every time, by a person who is not you, and you say so out loud in advance, and they come, and if they do not come you do not go** — *and it took a line going out of a book to make a man ask for it, and that is not a magic cost and it is not a doctor's opinion either, it is just what happened.* **The Field Book writes one line that day, six words, at about four in the afternoon, on the reach, about a road and not about who keeps one: *A road is kept, not made.*** He limits it on the facing page before acting: it does not say who keeps a road, **a foreman put a crew on that reach for four days to make a level and a form sent the crew off at fourteen minutes past two in the afternoon, and eleven hundred yards of it went out of true in a minute, and the crew came back on the Thursday because he asked Rennick to put it back, and that is on a form** — *A book that says a thing about a road is not an instruction and I have been a man who took instructions from a book twice and both times it cost somebody a place.* And the tenth route: **the walk of the eighteenth of October with a lamp and a cat in a laundry bag**, marked on the ninth in Rue's hand on a card with a date, and *she would not do it herself, she made the woman on the second landing do it because that woman has not been on a list since the fourth of Sazur and was on a list on the third and would not have it again.*

### Chapter 58 — Quiet, Not Healed

Monday the eighth to Friday the twelfth. **The public safety appeal is lodged at ten past nine on the Monday morning**, four pages, on the eighth day of a month in which a reach had gone out of true twice and a plate was in the water, and **the box is empty**, and the box is empty because on the fourth of the month Advocate Quill refused to supply a schedule of persons resident at the withdrawn alignment and put the refusal on the file in her own hand with her own name and date under it — *a nil return is capable of being read as a representation that no persons are resident there. I do not know that no persons are resident there. I know that I have been unable to establish a number and that the number on the district's schedule is short by twenty-one and that thirty-three rooms are on no list at all.* It has a clock: six working days, so the answer is the **fifteenth of next month**, and she tells the yard on the Thursday and not before, because a state is a thing this case has been ruined by twice. **The Crown Heights letter is produced voluntarily inside two hours, as a matter considered, and read out by Mara Quill twice, on her own initiative**, because the difference between a document that ambushes you and a document you put on the table yourself is four seconds. **The works' filing comes on the Tuesday. The tram authority's on the Wednesday**, and it is four lines and printed and it is in a rule book: *Line 7 — standing instruction, reach of the Alder Quay–Bridge Street. Issued the third of the current month. Officer issuing: District Traffic Superintendent. Conductor called out: J. LASK, conductor, Alder Quay depot.* And Jo Lask reads it in a yard to about thirty-one people and gives it back and says one sentence: **that is my name in a printed book and the cost of it is that I will not be conducting on this reach again and I have been on it every day since the fourth of October** — and a woman of sixty-three says *say that again about the reach* and he says it again. **The Prefect's office writes on Thursday the eleventh at about four**, one page, on the office's letterhead, **signed in a hand that is not a hand with a name at the bottom of it, and that name is Halden Vey, and it is the only place in the whole of this case in which that name has appeared**, and a man in a car collects it at a quarter to five and will not come to the yard. Six paragraphs: the order **suspended** from ten o'clock on Friday the twelfth, and its schedule suspended, **and not varied, withdrawn or discharged, and the public name is not restored by the suspension**; the order **opened for investigation** into the grounds of its making, into the classification of the eleventh, and into the removal of the sentence *no evidence was found of any hazard to persons at the service court*, **the withdrawal reference against which is not a district reference and is held in the office of the First Surveyor**; **Orrin Sable suspended from duty and required to give account, and the letter says in terms that this is not a finding and does not imply one — it is made because an officer may not investigate his own order**; the investigation by Survey Enforcement reported to the office by a person other than any person named in the letter or the order; **the classification withdrawn and not to be relied on by any party**, with the department noting that it was made correctly on the day it was made and that its withdrawal is not a criticism of the officer who made it; and **paragraph six: all timetables, gates, notices, classifications, drains and records are to be re-cut to the extent that they point at the withdrawn alignment and are to cease to point at it, re-cutting to be certified by a person familiar with the route withdrawn.** That last four words are the variation requirement, and Milo Rook reads paragraph six out twice at the request of a man who came down afterwards and asked to be read it, and tells the yard in advance which part of the sentence is going to change. **The re-cut is three documents and a shovel and is the best piece of work in the case.** The **drainage record**: the 1904 book's own columns, a hand four inches high, *Bridge Reach*, 140 ft, maintained frontage, unstruck at the low end on the Thursday morning with the date beside it, *reverting on the suspension of CO/6/BELL*, and then four words to Rue at the coal hatch side — *Requested by a mechanic* — and the request is also a separate page **in the reopened order file, at Tomas Rusk's own request, with his name at the foot of it and no oath on it**, and he has said in a yard that it is the last thing he will ever put his name to and has not been thanked. The **works maintenance schedule**: a frontage with a name, a reach, a gang and a fortnightly inspection, costing a man in a portacabin about four hundred words, mostly about insurance. **Barrow's amendment**, one paragraph, and it **puts the name on the diversion panel** as *Bridge Reach (works frontage)* and reinstates nothing, and he was asked twice by men in his own division not to do it, and *a works frontage is a thing that appears on about four hundred sheets in this authority's files and has a maintenance schedule behind it and a man to inspect it.* **At ten o'clock on the Friday the order is suspended, and at about ten to two the third call goes out on a reach that was not named on anything, and it asks once, and Lena Marr — who said in a yard on the Thursday that she was not going to stand at a door again and was not going to be asked — is at the ground-floor door anyway, and says the word once.** And then **it does not ask again, for six days, from the thirteenth to the eighteenth, and it is the only thing in eleven weeks that this district got for nothing.** And Dr Sen: **it is not mended, it is quiet, and a fixed thing is one you stop watching and this is a thing you will have to watch every day, and the day somebody in this district stops watching it is the day it starts again, and it will not start with a noise. It will start with a schedule.** **The material state**: the culvert at full flow and not returning to eighteen inches, twenty-four inches with a stop-screw and a padlock on the wheel, silted to about a third of its diameter, re-aligned over two days by nine men under a separate instruction, and **it is not the culvert that was there before the order and it will not be**; the platform out of service with its nameboards in the water and a fence and a note; the tower a hundred and one steps on a new pier; **the four-inch plate on a bench in the works compound with a hole in it where a fixing was**, the door behind it shut and staying shut, and the reason the plate is on a bench is that the works found it and there was nowhere else for it; the reach out of true by eleven inches over forty feet, fenced, with a settlement plate and a date, and the rail cut off and in a skip on the Terrace. **And on the twelfth at about half past four, in a room in a building in Crown Heights, with a caution on him and a solicitor he chose himself, Orrin Sable began to give account, got three sentences out of the ten he had set himself, and stopped; and what is on the page is not that room but what he said afterwards to about six men in a portacabin at about ten o'clock that evening, in a yard that had emptied.** Three things he will say, in a yard that had emptied: the safety need was real and is still real and **the wall behind this district did not fail and that is the only thing I have ever wanted to be true**; **the concealment was not required by the safety problem and I have known that since the first week and I did it with a document and not with a lie, which is worse, and I put the true sentence in the box that only takes it, and a filing clerk read it back to me over the telephone and was quiet for about four seconds and said *that is a very unusual thing to find in an alternative considered box*, and I have never in nineteen years put a true sentence in a box that only takes it and I do not know why I did it**; and **there is a file in this district that I have never been able to get at, and I have stopped trying, and I am telling about nine people about it in a yard because there is a dead man in it and his name is on a ledger with the word pending against it, and I am not going to say the rest of it in December and I am not going to say it here, and I want the two words not and not noticed, and I am aware that I have used them, and I am aware of what I am doing.** Nobody asked him. **And the reopened file yields a carbon relocation schedule, copied off an older survey book and not off a district register, four routes, three placeable in about four minutes, and a fourth naming a destination that is real, dated, unplaceable and about an hour and twenty minutes from Alder Quay on an entirely ordinary road** — and **one mark in the margin, one, a question about the destination and not about the order, and it is not one of the two questions on the other carbon, and there are two documents in this case with a pair of marks on them and this is not a third pair, because a pair is two marks in two hands on one page and this is one mark in one hand and I do not know that a second is coming.** And, again, in his own book, that he is not joining it to the piece of paper in five houses with his arithmetic in it, and not to a tick in a fourth column in six houses in four streets — *joining them would be me deciding that they are the same, and a man who decides two things are the same in a book is a man who has started making the case instead of the record.*

### Chapter 59 — Open the File

Saturday the thirteenth, Sunday the fourteenth, Monday the fifteenth. **The complete file comes out of the store on the Saturday at about eleven, nine boxes, two men and a barrow, and about forty people from the river core stand in a Crown Heights corridor and watch it go past on a trolley**, which nobody sanctioned and nobody stopped. **The death record is in the third box.** It is a card, and every one of these is now on the page: *VENN, J. — pump crew, 2nd shift — relocation due, pending. One loss recorded. (Reopened for investigation, the thirteenth.)* **Cause of death, on reopening: fatality, second-stage main, pump house, lower level. Not entered in the original entry. The original entry carried no cause.** The original entry's contributory line, **in the same hand as the report**: *the frontage is at all times a service access and not a public route.* **Next of kin, blank for six years, filled from a department's card index in about four seconds: WIFE, living, address recorded** — and the address is on the district's own index and has been for six years, and nobody has ever sent her a letter, and a clerk read a card index and said nothing about it and is not going to be thanked. **Disposal: a ground named on the authority's return, a plot numbered and not on the district's map, in a ground whose register for that district was not kept after the closure. The return is six words long and it has a plot on it.** **Lena Marr goes on the Sunday morning at about ten, in a taxi, at nine pounds of her own money, with Doreen Sankey on the step who will not come past the first room in nine years and says so.** The woman is fifty-four and mends other people's clothes and **her name is not on the page and has not been for six years and is not going to be.** About forty minutes, four rooms behind a boatyard. Lena reads the six words off the return, does not make her ask a single question, does not make her write it down, does not make her ask where to go, and **does not say sorry, because she has said in a yard that she does not intend to apologise to a woman for something a department did**. *You have brought me the number. / Nobody has brought me anything in six years. Not even a bill. I have had a fortnight's relief in the first year because I applied for it and I did not know what I was applying for and a woman in an office said it could not be done and gave me the money anyway and would not look at me.* **Pell Harrow comes down on the Sunday night at ten past ten with a piece of chalk, puts an arrow on the outside of Milo's own building at about five feet, at the foot of the stairs, pointing up, with the fourteenth of December under it, and does not say a word and goes back up the yard at about four miles an hour.** **The room is the Hall of Wards on the Monday at about ten, and it is not a hearing and was not called a hearing**, and about nine people in the district have written down that it should not have been, because a hearing can be got out of on appeal and a department answering a statutory appeal on the sixth working day is a different and worse thing for the district. **Five things granted**, in the department's own terms: a **temporary route** as a supervised passage on named dates, in daylight, with a named person outside holding a count and a named person walking in and out, and no schedule of persons; a **temporary stay** against demolition and forced relocation until the conclusion of the investigation; **access** for every household to its own housing and compensation file, on application, in person, at a counter, and to no household's file by any other party; a **public hearing** to which the district is entitled as of right and not as of favour; and **the public name restored as a contested community and memorial name** — on the community and memorial register, **not on the street name index**, **not as a rateable place**, marked *contested*, and **capable of being produced against any person who uses it** — which the woman of sixty has read four times and says she is going to use anyway, *and I am not going to be one of the women who waits for the perfect thing.* **And the trap comes at twenty past eleven, and it comes because nobody in the room had solved it and it cannot be solved.** A tenancy needs a street and there is no street. A temporary registration needs a head of household, **and the register is read out by a clerk of the Recovery office in about four seconds, correctly, in front of about thirty people, and the answer is a floor and a file number, and nobody in the room said the name, nobody woke him, no clerk noticed anything, and a clerk of the Recovery office read a file number out at four seconds' notice and went back to his counter.** Mara Quill: *then the form cannot be completed on that line, and I am not going to ask anybody in this room to look up a file number, and nobody is going to wake anybody up, and I would like it minuted that Advocate Quill did not ask.* The third way, found by the clerk: a **temporary accommodation household with a named head who is a rateable person** — on a per-head additional schedule, not the street index, not a rate book, not a rateable place, so **the compensation is untouched**, and the seven children can be enrolled on a Continuity referral that names a responsible person. And: **a rate is built on a property, and there is no property, and a person in a room is not a property, and a rate cannot be built on a door, and if the district cannot build a rate on a door then somebody has to stand at the door, and it cannot be the third floor, and no clerk in this room is going to be the one who notices.** The department states the consequence because he has to: **two and four a head a week, charged to the named head and not to the district rate, for every person entered in the household, week by week, in advance, not a one-time sum, no term, ended only by the household ceasing to exist or by the district producing a rateable basis for a block that has no street.** **Lena Marr stands up at about ten to twelve, after writing both her questions down and showing them to a woman of sixty-three on a step, and signs.** The seven children are enrolled on a Continuity referral in the same hand in the same minute, **the two lost in October are still two, and she said so herself, twice, in the room, unasked.** Nobody thanked her. **And the count closes and the money is recovered**: the department accepts a certified head count of **two hundred and fifty-two** in place of a schedule of rooms, on Advocate Quill's certificate in her own hand with a declaration of interest at the top of it, the per-head schedule is adjusted from two hundred and thirty-one and **the twenty-one short is recovered**, the thirty-three rooms stay on no list, and **no room in this district is on a rate book today because of anything that happened today** — and the certificate is the third thing in a file about her own hand and it is the thing that finishes her review. **Mara Quill files her formal objection and public safety appeal at about ten past three**, nineteen paragraphs, in her hand, against the order, the classification, the substitution of an engineering classification for a safety finding, the removal of the sentence, six years, and a dead man; three attachments, of which the second is **the carbon relocation schedule with four lines in her own hand on the back in biro** — *a document in a file is a thing somebody can produce, and I have read it, and I would rather be the person who put it there* — and the third is the community and memorial register, whose entry's last line is the woman of sixty's request, noted. And Milo, in a corridor, at about one o'clock, writes the whole of the resolution in about a hundred words, and the fourth line of it is: **a woman of about thirty-four is on the per-head additional schedule for every person in a building with no number, at two and four a head a week, for as long as that building is a building — and a man of sixty-one in a knee is on a drainage record that nobody will strike out again, and a conductor of thirty in a printed rule book, and a foreman of thirty years in a file that a department can now produce, and a name of sixty-eight who is on nothing and has not been asked, and a surveyor's own front door with three chalk arrows on it and a book with a blank nine lines long in it** — *That is the whole of what I did in three months, and I have written it down, and I am not going to be able to add a line to it, and I am not going to want to.*

### Chapter 60 — What the Route Looks Like Now

Monday the fifteenth of December. **The route as it now is**, itemised and specific: a hundred and forty feet of frontage, a stop-screw through the stem and a padlock on the wheel, a fence with a settlement plate and a date, a gravel path nine feet wide with a dead lamp bracket on the wall above it that four people have said ought to be mended and nobody has money for; **Bridge Reach** in the district's drainage book in a hand four inches high with a date, a *works frontage* on the contractor's schedule with a gang and a fortnightly inspection, on a diversion panel in a clerk's hand, and **not a street, not on the street name index, not rateable, and it cannot be given an address** — *a works frontage is the only kind of street anybody in this district is ever going to get and I am not complaining*; a supervised passage on named dates with a count and a walker and a rule at the foot and **a deliberate space at the foot of it that is still empty**, which went through both days empty and about four people have checked; a temporary stop-and-go that expires with the investigation; access to files, with Advocate Quill's empty box still empty and going to stay empty; a hearing as of right, not yet set, with a reporting officer who must not be anyone named; and a contested name. **The things that changed and are not going to change back, in four sentences and a fence**: the culvert, the platform, the tower, the plate on a bench, the reach. **Orrin Sable is suspended and required to give account; the order is opened for investigation; that is not a finding and does not imply one; the classification is withdrawn and the department has noted in writing that it was made correctly on the day it was made; Cass Ardery has crossed out his own paragraph in ink, dated it and initialled it, and has asked in writing to be second-in-command on the investigation of a thing he classified, *because a department that cannot have the man who made the mistake in the room is a department that has already decided.*** And **the wall behind the district did not fail, and that is in the file and it was the only thing the man wanted.** **Mara Quill** has a two-year note, an appeal in one line, not stayed, preferred access gone, nineteen paragraphs filed, a review on the third of December that found she had worked the file off her own hours for six years without telling her panel, and the fourth set of initials in the annex box of a six-year-old routing slip that she has read four times and has still not read aloud to anybody. She read the finding out in a corridor to about nine people and about six of them did not understand a word of it. On the fifteenth she certifies a head count with a declaration of interest at the top, and says in a yard of about eleven that she will not be doing any more of this for a while, and then says the thing she has not said: **on the twenty-ninth of Sazur a man promised her that nothing of his bearing on the file would reach her in writing with a date on it, and she held that for forty-four days and then made him give away the design instead, and he gave it away in about nine seconds and did not complain, and that is the most decent thing anybody has done for me in six years and I have not said so to him and I am not going to.** Nobody in the yard says anything and about nine of them look at Milo Rook and he looks at the ground, and Rue says afterwards that it was the only time in eleven weeks she had watched two people in this district be in the same place and be unable to do anything about it, and that it was the first time she had wanted to. **Nothing romantic has happened and nothing is going to; she is not going to write his return route for him in Volume 1 and it was never hers to write, and he has not asked; the promise stands and is hers and does not get wider; neither of them has said anything about how either of them feels and neither is going to, and if a person in this district were to say so in a yard he would find about nine people looking at him.** **The Field Book, in the route room of the Civic Atlas Office, on the second of the two tables under the north window, open at a page in the middle.** None of it is on the public map, the contested entry is in a bound book in another part of the city, and the **street name index** has a line against the reach reading *no rateable place — works frontage — see Bridge Reach*, put there by somebody who found the only box there was, and nobody is going to have it tidied. **Dilley Farr: *I have watched three men in this room lose a route in eleven years… and you have written yours down, in your own hand, with a date, and you have written the rule at the foot of somebody else's sheet and you have given the thing away in a porch in front of thirty people, and I want you to know that I have signed no deletion in this room for six weeks and the reason is not principle, it is that there has been nothing in front of me worth signing a deletion for. Because of you.*** And he tells Milo to finish his page, and says he will not read it and will not be asked, and that there is a woman on a counter in his district who has been saying that to a badge for six weeks and it has begun. **He asks at about ten past five, and is refused twice by people who had no reason to refuse him.** Rue: *that is mine, and mine is three streets and a chandler's yard and a pie shop, and I do not know a step of yours and I am not going to learn nine steps in a hall and then have them in my mouth in your kitchen… write it down. Ask somebody who has to write it down.* Doreen Sankey: *No. And I will tell you why, and then you will not ask me again, and I am right. I have been asked for that route in about four houses this month and I have said no in all four… and if I walk it once to write it down then in a month somebody will be able to say the boatyard man keeps the surveyor's way home, and the second it is written down it is a job, and I know what a job is, because in a house in Wragg Street a woman has kept a sheet for nineteen years and her husband is dead four years and there is a tick in it she cannot account for. Ask a person who has to be there. Not a person who is passing.* **Nobody filled it in.** The last entry in Volume 1: **ten remembered routes, eight from the fourth of Sazur and none of them gone and all of them marked by somebody who is not him**, the ninth a card from a nurse of twenty-three, the tenth a card in Rue's hand on the ninth that she made a woman on the second landing do it; **and the line home is still a blank, twenty-two days, and he has asked for it and been refused twice and it is still a blank**; and the three things he cannot order — *it might be a cost, and it might be a bad memory, and it might be a page that needs another person's mark, and the honest state of the matter is that I do not know which*; the token cold on the fourteenth and the fifteenth and not taken out, warm once for four minutes and not used; **seven dated pairs — six days, nine days, three days, three days, two days, three days, three days — and he does not join any to any other, does not collapse the two threes into the threes, does not write down what he thinks seven of a thing means, and an eighth of a single day stays looked at and unwritten**; **a street is a thing people keep**, which he is not going to write in the book because the book wrote it about a road and it does not say who keeps one; and the paragraph that closes it — *So there is a hundred and forty feet of frontage with a name in a drainage book, and a fence, and a settlement plate, and a rule at the foot of a sheet of accountancy paper in Rue's hand, and a space at the bottom of it that is empty on purpose, and a woman of sixty with a contested name on a register that can be produced against her, and a conductor in a rule book, and a mechanic on a page with his name at the foot of it and no oath on it, and a man of sixty-eight who is on nothing and has not been asked, and a woman of fifty-four with a plot number in her own hand, and a boy of seventeen with chalk. And I am the man who wrote the columns and did the arithmetic and is not the author, and is not the holder, and is not the clerk, and is not the custodian, and has been in a corridor for ninety minutes this afternoon while a woman of about thirty-four put her name in a box in a hall. That is the whole of what I did in three months, and I have written it down, and I am not going to be able to add a line to it, and I am not going to want to.* **And across the city, at about ten past three on the same afternoon, the objection goes on a counter, and its three attachments are the order complete, the carbon relocation schedule with four lines in her own hand on the back — *I am not saying what it means. I am saying that a copy of an old survey book names a place this city cannot point at, and that it was in the order, and that nobody has produced it in six years, including me* — and the community and memorial register.** And none of the river core knows, and one of them is under the north window with a Field Book open at a blank, and there is a chalk arrow on the outside of his own building at about five feet with the fourteenth of December under it, **and it was the arrow that had got him up his own stairs the night before**, and a second on the inside of his own front door with the twenty-eighth of November under it, and a third on the wall of the second-floor landing with no date on it at all, **and nobody had ever asked who put that one there, and nobody was going to.**

# Volume 02 — The City Underneath the Survey

## Volume 02, Batch 0001 (Chapters 61–70)

### Chapter 61 — A Car That Is Not Late

Tuesday the sixteenth of December, 07.20 to about 23.30. **Car 214 of the Line 3 morning service left Crown Heights at 07:52 with thirty-one passengers on the manifest, a conductor called Nessa Bray, thirty-eight, sixteen years on the run, and a driver called Corin Mabey, fifty-one, twenty-nine years — and it was not late.** That sentence is the statement the authority put up outside Alder Quay depot at ten past ten and every map in the city agrees with it. **The yard notices at 08.41: the box has no return entry, the conductor's manual return has not been delivered, the yard's running sheet has the times in the left-hand columns and the right-hand columns blank.** At 09.20 at the depot: **the box registered the car at 08.06 at the Alder Quay interchange and there is no second entry anywhere on the roll**; the fare register counts **31 fares, the conductor's pass and the driver's pass**, written on the back of a returns docket by **Hester Coombe, fifty-one, twenty-two years a depot clerk, "That is thirty-three, and I have never had to say a number like that out loud."** At 10.10 the line controller's screen — **Percy Doak, thirty-four** — shows the car arriving on the shed road at **08.31, on time, on the correct path, drawn in four minutes and eleven seconds, because the running diagram has no length between the interchange and the water stairs.** **Service amendment 12/44, Monday the thirteenth of October, printed, dated, in an amendment list any competent person may read and did not read: approximately 600 yards, the down loop and the quay service access, reserved, no booked service, standing instruction of the office of the Prefect ref. 11/D/6, requested by Permanent Way quay section; the *requested by* line and the *instructing officer* line both blank.** The Atlas Office's quayside sheet follows the running diagram and has never had it; the district's street index has eleven entries against the quayside and none of them is six hundred yards of double track; five route readers dropped it in the week of the thirteenth of October along with about nine hundred other amendments and no note. **Nobody can find the passengers' names in a map, and that is not a difficulty, it is the shape of the thing.** Four things established, and the fourth is the only one that is a document: **the justification box does not contain a reason, it contains the filing number PW/Q/4 — a filing number is not a reason, it is a way of saying ask somebody else, and a box cannot tell the difference, because that is a box.** Nobody stole anything; there is no face in it. Rue refuses a lamp; Hettie Brann says the list of the missing belongs in a public building and not in a yard in the rain, and **"you start with the paper, and you do not put it in your bag."** Field Book, 23.15: three marks and none drawn; the token cold; **eight remembered routes and a page, the twenty-third day of it**; the book silent. Across the city, Mara Quill is told by a man in another division that the matter is not hers, writes **11/D/6** twice in a notebook of her own, and tells nobody.

### Chapter 62 — Clerks, Witnesses, Maintenance

Wednesday the seventeenth, 07.00 to 22.40. **Dilley Farr changes the method in the route room and says the second of four things Milo wrote is wrong in its grammar** — *there is no such thing as a map that is out of date; there is a map and a list, and the list gets changed by somebody with a form, and after that the map is right and the ground is wrong; I have watched it four times in thirty years; it takes an afternoon; nobody has to be in a room and nobody has to want anything* — and then, with his back to the three atlases, **look for the work, not the road: if a thing in this city has been kept in one piece for a hundred and forty years it is because about nine men were paid to walk it, and it will come apart in a week the day the pay stops, and no amount of looking under a map will tell you anything about it, because the map is the part that gets reprinted.** At the quay section office, four rooms above a fitter's shop: **Cyril Sale, works clerk, forty-six, four years in the job**, whose predecessor was Mrs Ivell for twenty-two. The gang's weekly sheet is one side, carbon-backed, six columns — date, length, men, hours, work done, condition, signature — **and there is no column for anybody's opinion**, and **Roy Pryce, fifty-four, gang foreman, thirty-one years, comes down to say so and does not offer to help because he was not asked to help.** Eleven years of sheets, the back length on **Mondays and Thursdays**, and it has never once not been on the sheet. **The copy is found:** a routing slip about four inches by six inches, *To: timetable division. Purpose: **for index.** Re: PW/Q/4 (3rd edn.) + gang sheet, wk. Dispatched 10 Oct. Received 10 Oct.* The **third** edition of the quay section maintenance schedule, dated the eleventh of September, has an **empty line** against the back length; the **fourth**, dated the twenty-second of November, has it full, with a margin note in the clerk's hand: *Length added to sched. per amd. 12/44. C.S.* So **the document that justified the change did not contain the thing it was justifying, and six weeks later it did, and nobody in between was asked to remember which of those two facts was true.** The bridge sentence, from a works clerk at his own desk, standing up: **a road is not a line. A road is eleven men and a hundred yards of drain and a sheet of paper a foreman carries out of a hut… the sheet is the only thing in this building that knows where anybody has been, and it is a record of work and it does not have a box on it that wants to know why.** And: **nobody has stolen anything; there is no man to charge; there is a box, a slip with two words on it, and a schedule that has since been made to look as though it always said what everybody now says it said.** **Pell Harrow** on the afternoon: a man of about fifty in a works jacket with a badge that says a number and not a name, on the quay on Monday the fourteenth and again that morning, who **took the gang's clipboard off the wall, stood in nine feet of rain holding it, and put it back exactly** — *a man who is going to steal a thing is quick*; no name, no copy seen, and **I have not had a word from Tern and I am not going to start now and I am going up the south on the twenty-eighth.** Field Book, 22.40: the new method is **look for the work**; a record of work is not a record of a reason; the join to November is written down **once, in the book, and not in a yard**; **and a filing number in a box and a clipboard off a wall in the rain are not going in a column, because a column is a case and he has made one twice in eleven weeks and both times it made itself into a case.**

### Chapter 63 — A Refusal That Is Correct

Wednesday the seventeenth, 14.20 to Thursday the eighteenth, 06.40. **The Survey School's repository** — about forty people in this city call it Thread Hall and the school calls it the Repository and it has not been called the second of those on any letterhead since before the war — **a stair of thirty-four steps, nine desks, nine readers, a plan chest, a window that does not open.** **Amos Trench, sixty-three, forty-one years at the index desk, in a coat**, refuses twice and is right both times. Cheap answer first: the back length is not in the plan chest and never was, because **a tram is not surveyed by this school; a tram is put in by the authority, and the authority's record of a tram alignment is a maintenance record, and the school keeps none, and the school holding a copy of another body's maintenance record is the most dangerous object in this city because a maintenance record is the only record in this country that is true by having been done, and a copy of one is a claim that the work was done somewhere else.** And the second refusal: **there is nobody to put a written request to** — the reporting officer on the open investigation has to be a person other than any person named in the Prefect's letter or in the order, and the department is not going to name one in December — and there is one person who could put her name to it and **he does not ask her.** **Milo's failure, and it is his.** He puts his hand on a rolled plan at 14.25 and **there is no edge anywhere, because the negative space in that room is made of people**, and he says out loud, to nine witnesses, *there is a road under this, I am not being dramatic, I am looking for where it went* — and then tells a school he telephoned on the Tuesday night that **a document is in this building**, before he has asked whether it is the origin or a reader of it. **Bettina Sallow, fifty-four, four years on a hand index at the ninth desk on the left, writes it down on the back of a finding aid because that is her work**, and does not raise her voice about it. On the stairs at ten to four she gives him the thing he needed: **eleven route origin sheets in the withdrawal file, nine in that building, two not, and an index is a description of what is here and not a description of what is not here**, and she has wanted somebody to walk into that room for four years. Field Book, standing up, having not crossed the room for four hours: **I did not ask her because she would have done it. That is the whole of it and it took me about two hours to arrive at it and I arrived at it on my own and nobody in that room helped me**; the token cold and the sentence that has not improved in three weeks; **the book silent, and three days now since the fifteenth, and those three days are the inside of the five that end on Saturday, and a day is not a pair.** **And the coat is paid off in the same entry, which is the only thing in the chapter that is not a document: he had it on because he had spent his morning in a building where he was somebody's witness, and that is why he had the answer ready before Milo asked it and gave it to him in the wrong order on purpose, and Milo did not see any of it for about two hours.**

### Chapter 64 — Thirty-One Names

Thursday the eighteenth, 09.30 to 23.05. **The working manifest goes out with the conductor and the depot keeps the carbon.** Six columns — time on, stop boarded, fares, alighted, run no, conductor. **Thirty-one names in a small neat hand that is the conductor's; the *Alighted* column empty on all thirty-one lines because the car has not finished; and the line at the foot where the conductor signs is not signed.** **The two documents that disagree and are both correct**, from a works clerk standing by the door: **a count on that length was taken by an automatic function, because the length is not on the diagram, so there was no box in it, so the only instrument that could have said whether a car was on that length did not know the length existed. That is not a fault. There is nothing to fault. There is a hole in the diagram and the box went in the hole.** Nobody in the district asked where the boxes are; about four people noticed that nobody asked. **The thirty-one are read out once in a yard in the rain at about five o'clock and nobody writes them down**, on Hettie Brann's reason — the list belongs in a public building in a drawer in a depot office, not in a yard — and about nine people write one name at home in their own kitchen and nobody collects those. **The district's own sheet: NAME and LAST SEEN, and the LAST SEEN column is about an inch and a half deep and it is empty, and the reason it is empty is that this city spent nine weeks agreeing that six hundred yards was not anywhere, and then a tram went there.** **The first family, at ten past four, the first of about nine and the only one anybody has written down afterwards: Paul Nye, thirty-eight, and Ellis Nye, eleven, the school route, and his aunt Maggie Nye, sixty-six.** Rue refuses the sheet in four seconds and gives the reason later, **in a kitchen in Boardman Street at eight o'clock, standing up by the sink, with nobody from the district there but Rue, in four sentences**: a piece of paper in a yard is a piece of paper about people; a name with a place beside it is a record that survives the people who made it and will be false in ten years if the boy comes back and a removal in four months if he does not; and **I have written about eleven of these sheets in three months and every one of them has gone somewhere, and a sheet that has gone is not a sheet that has been thrown away, it is a sheet that somebody will find in about four years in a drawer they have been told to look in.** Then the thing that is the whole of the day: **Maggie Nye writes her son's and her nephew's names on a sheet of her own in her own hand in her own kitchen with a date and puts it on the fridge under a magnet off a calendar from a shop on Bridge Street**, and tells Rue not to know where it is. **Doreen Sankey walked Tomas Rusk to the depot at a quarter to nine before he had done his own eleven and did not put it on a sheet, and Rusk asked for that to be entered in Hettie Brann's list as one true small thing**, and he is still on a drainage record and a works schedule and a diversion panel and he still will not swear. Field Book, 23.05: the *Alighted* column as the only column a person there can fill in; **no document in the authority, the district or the Registry has a box for where a car got off, and there never has been, and the reason is that a car has always been somewhere that somebody is responsible for**; and the two names that matter are on a fridge under a magnet and neither belongs to him.

### Chapter 65 — The Custody Register

Friday the nineteenth, 09.10 to about 18.00, **Mara Quill's chapter and the only non-Milo chapter in the batch.** She has no preferred access and finds out what her access was only when she tries to use it; her request is made by a clerk called Rudd because she is not permitted to make her own. **The code is one line and she can recite it: *an officer who copies, or removes, or causes to be copied or removed, a record held outside their access shall be suspended.*** She reads it twice at about ten past ten and does not read it a third time, **because the third reading is where a person begins looking for a way round it, and there is no way round it, and she has known that since the fourth of December.** She asks for the page. **Ninety seconds, at a table, in a records alcove, with the door open, with a pencil.** A man of about forty-five in Survey Enforcement walks past, looks at the page, and keeps walking; **she does not follow him, and the reason she did not is that a woman who goes after a man to ask him about it has a document about a state of mind in her file, and a woman in an alcove with a door open has only a door.** The page: *Withdrawn from running diagram: 13 Oct, service amendment 12/44, under standing instruction 11/D/6. Requesting body: PW quay section. Route custody after withdrawal: **nil.*** And on a continuation sheet in a different hand: ***one.*** *1. Route token, brass, hand-stamped **LARK ST.**, no number. Issue not in current series. Held by: **ROOK.** Authority for issue: [no entry]. Returned: [no entry].* Four lines in biro on the back, and then a page of her own, dated, that says **this is not the fourth set of initials** — that is a thing she has not done and has not chosen every day for four months — and that **she has had a day and a half and has not used it and is not going to, and would not use it if the car came back tomorrow**, and that **if he ever finds out that she knows, he will not thank her and will not be angry either, and she would like that written down first because she has worked out in advance that she will not be able to bear it.** In the yard at half past three she tells the river core four things, and the third of them is that the joining of the order and the amendment is **her** job and not a yard's and she would rather be stopped by nine people in a yard than by a panel in about four months. The middle of the chapter is the district's Volume 1 inventory, itemised and unchanged, in about four hundred words, **and it now also carries the five copies with a man's name on them and a floor on the paper that is not his, the foreman reinstated twice, the flag man, the twenty-sixth day of a nine-line blank he has not asked anybody to fill in for three weeks, and — two lines lower and a different man — the man asleep on the third floor who is not written down and whom nobody has woken.** The hearing **has still not been set** and the reporting officer **still has to be a person other than any person named in the Prefect's letter or in the order.**

### Chapter 66 — Saltmarket Row

Friday the nineteenth, 18.00 to Saturday the twentieth, 18.30. **Saltmarket Row: a hundred and ten yards off Cardew Street, eleven pitches down the north side, nine brick and two a hoarding, the wholesale fish and vegetable market until nineteen years ago, not a street, not on the street name index, and it cannot be given an address. Held up by about eleven habits and about four people; five of the eleven identified in about four hours and the other six are somebody else's Saturday.** Milo walks it at about ten past eleven with three people, one of them **Tomas Rusk, sixty-one, walked there by Doreen Sankey before he had done his own eleven.** Four things: a coffee man of about sixty who puts his hand on the corner of the same wall at half past five every day in his own time; a fishmonger of about forty-five who calls the lot prices from a doorway with no fish, *not for anybody*; six flat irons on a rail in an order put there by somebody Milo is **not allowed to make a map of** and writes as *irons*; a man of about forty who sweeps the Row badly every morning because it is swept. **A market scale at the north end, kept for thirty-one years by Ivy Marsden, seventy-four, who weighs anything, charges nothing and writes nothing down**, eleven sets of brass weights on a board with a hook for each and no name on the board, because *if one goes missing then it went missing on me and I will not have that on me.* **The Keepsake reading:** the cue is a wet stone floor with fish scales and cut herbs and coffee at an hour when there has been no coffee within a hundred and ten yards since the year before the war, **and the coffee is the part that is slightly wrong**; the service is the weights and a woman who tells the truth about a number; **the demand is an actual part of the routine and the only part available to him is holding the stone while somebody zeroes the beam**; the failure is that **the scale takes the name, not the weight.** He does not put a weight on it, and Ivy Marsden says one word: *Right.* **His second failure, in the field:** he tries to stabilise the boundary wall and **there is nothing to hold** — *a seam is a hole where two accounts of a place do not agree; a length of tram route that has come off the running diagram is not a hole, it is a thing, it has a gang and a number and an inspection interval and a wall at the south end and a rack and a tin and a foreman of thirty-one years* — and what he has spent four days learning to do is a thing you do to a wound. **Doreen Sankey's line:** *it is not a mystery, and if you go into that shed at Cardew Docks and ask the manager he will tell you the same thing I am telling you: they come back. The row is not a thing that happened. It is a habit that has got two doors.* **The first stable field map**, made from 13.00 to 17.00 on a sheet of accountancy paper about three feet long: **the frame is his, the content is other people's, in their own hands with dates, and the corner says *content not mine, do not correct it, ask the person whose hand it is.*** The rule at the foot is his: *This sheet does not say the Row is a street and it does not say the length is a road. It says who does what and on which day. If any of them stops, cross the line out and date it, and do not take the line off, and do not ask anybody to start again.* **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, in five words: *A trade outlives its pitch.*** The limit on the facing page before anything else, and the fifteenth-to-the-twentieth pair written down as five days, the eighth pair in the book. **And at ten past six Tomas Rusk says the sentence the whole week is built on**: *A road is a place and a crew keeps a road. A gap is a place where a road stopped being a place and a crew does not keep a gap. **And that length out there is not a road and it is not a gap and that is why it has got a car on it. It is the third thing and nobody has a word for the third thing because nobody has ever needed one.***

### Chapter 67 — A Sequence With a Voice On It

Sunday the twenty-first, 16.00 to about midnight. **A tram gong behind the cold store at half past four, eleven seconds, on the Thursday, the Friday and the Saturday, and on the Tuesday it went for two hours** — reported by Pell Harrow, who has been on the quay three evenings to watch a gang come in, **and who tells Milo Rook he did not hear it: *you were on your own front step at ten past four and I was forty feet away and I could see your face*** — and a dog in the front room of the house at the top of the Row that has not stopped shaking since Friday, and **a dog is the only witness in this case that nobody can put in a file.** At about seven **Jo Lask**, a conductor of thirty with his name in a printed rule book, states the reversal in a yard of about nine people and does not sit down: *A tram is not a thing. A tram is a sequence with somebody on it. The list is no use whatever on its own; what holds it is a person saying the next one out loud… **Your car is not lost.** A thing with a sequence and a conductor in it goes on being a sequence, and it goes round, and nobody has called the turns since Tuesday, so it has got further away, and it is not lost and it is not stuck and it is not in a room. It is going round.* And the answer to the question everybody is actually asking: ***Nothing is holding it out of here. It is being out of here.*** *A car in a street is a car in the present. A car in a length — a length with a gang and a number and a wall at the south end and a market that is not there on the other side of it — is a car somewhere that is not the present, because a length is not a street and a street is the present. **The thing that is keeping your car out of this city is the same thing that is keeping the coffee smell on that Row. The length is different, and different is a kind of place, and your car is safe for as long as nobody makes it the same.*** Why it cannot be forced, in four items: *you can stand still, go in, say the word at the turns, come out; **you cannot stop a sequence and you cannot seal a length, because there is no edge on it to seal and there is no door, and if you put a thing across it you have made a pinned thing with thirty-three people in it.*** At about ten past eight **Tomas Rusk, walked to the yard, refuses to sit down: *A length has got a crew on a sheet and a wall at the end of it… The writing down is the crew. The crew is the writing down. Take the writing down away on the tenth of October and you have not removed a road. You have removed the crew's reason for being there on Tuesday morning, and the gang still came on Tuesday, and the gang's reason is a piece of paper, and nobody has ever taken a piece of paper away from a gang in this city, and that is the only reason your car is still going round.*** And his condition, asked for in advance: **I am not going to be asked afterwards why I was there and I am not going to be thanked, and I want both of those written down before anybody asks me for either of them.** The plan at a quarter to eleven, on four sheets of accountancy paper, nine people, Milo drawing the frame of two and the content of none: **four turns — the north end signal (not a turn, and a box, and Jo Lask says so), the down loop, the wall, the water stairs end with its buffer**; Rue's turn sheet with a **third column headed SAID BY added in her hand, because the printed rule of the third of December is on the reach and the length is not the reach, and the deliberate space at the foot is still empty**; the four conditions read out in the rain by everybody together; Doreen Sankey says he will walk a foreman down and out and is not going to be on the sheet about it. **And at about twenty past eleven, in a porch, Advocate Quill writes his return route and milestone 2 lands.** He does not ask. She does not offer. *I am going to write a route, and I am not going to do it kindly… **There is no instrument in this city that says a man came back. A man who cannot find the way he came is a man who has to be produced from somewhere afterwards, and if the only person who can produce him is his own memory then in about four months a department will produce him instead, out of a filing system, and it will not be the same man.*** Forty minutes, accountancy paper, biro, heading, date, *out* and *back*. **Two of her four words are wrong and Rue finds both in about ninety seconds** — the down loop is on the left going out and the right coming back, and the signal is not a turn — **and Rue's two corrections are in Rue's hand on the back and the date and the version number are in his, because he is the only one of the three who writes dates. Three hands. Four copies.** She did not wish him goodnight.

### Chapter 68 — The Ninth Crossing

Monday the twenty-second, 07.30 to 20.40. **The gang's ordinary walk at half past seven, fifty minutes, eleven men, and Milo Rook is not on it** and stands at the north end; a man of about twenty-six stops in the down loop to look at a drain every time and has for two years and nobody has ever asked him and Doreen Sankey is not going to start. **Ola Petrossian at the water clinic at a quarter to eight, not asked, and says she would rather be a nurse than a piece of paper with a number on it.** Nine people and a woman of sixty-three with a Thermos and a man on a bicycle with a coat open, **Lena Marr telling about nine people at half past nine that a hundred and forty feet of made ground and six hundred yards of tram route have exactly one thing in common and that is that a man walks them — and Milo strikes the paragraph out and writes it again as two, and refuses to write it as one sentence until a mechanic has said it out loud.** Nell Crale and Verrick Assal sit on the same wall from nine until eleven and do not speak to each other. **Jo Lask sits on a crate and does not go onto the length, twice asked, same answer: *because it is not my turn and it has never been my turn… if I go out onto that length then in about four months somebody writes down that a conductor of the authority attended, and then in about four years the whole thing is a thing a department did, and the gang is a man in a coat*** — and his hearing **has still not been set.** **Roy Pryce says no for about forty minutes and is right to.** *I am not walking it twice for a department, and I am not walking it twice for a man off the news, and I am not walking it twice because a car is missing, because a car is not a thing my gang keeps.* Then he asks for the manifest, has the carbon read down a telephone with four seconds of silence at the end, and says: *Somebody has been on that car for six days with the bottom of their sheet blank. And a man of twenty-six on my gang does not have to be told what that is like.* **His condition:** the second entry goes in the hours column and the reason says **a car is on the length and the gang is the crew on the length** — and **he will not write one word about thirty-one passengers, because there is no column for it, and because in about four years a fella in an office will say *Mr Pryce, you witnessed thirty-one persons*, and he has been the crew on four lengths for thirty-one years and has never once witnessed a person and is not going to start on a Monday in December.** **In at 11.06 and out at 11.41, thirty-five minutes inside, from the north end of the length, with the quay section gang.** All four conditions: Sankey walks in and out and can stop it; **Pell Harrow holds the count with a counter and says no words for thirty-five minutes**; twelve minutes stated; the clinic at a quarter to eight. **The map is Rue's — two columns, a third headed SAID BY, the deliberate space still empty — and he drew none of it and read the rule out loud on the length, and there are two rules on it: Rue's at the foot, and nine words in grease pencil in a foreman's hand in the top corner.** Four turns and the word. **And at about twenty to twelve a gong, and at four seconds into it he cannot call up the pitch of the third gong of a Line 3 morning car, and he needed it**, because a conductor who has heard a person say *clear* answers and he could not tell whether she had — so he waits until it stops and then says it, and the instrument he uses instead is the nine words in grease pencil. **A man of about sixty comes out of a frontage on Saltmarket Row at ten past twelve and stands in the street for four minutes and goes in again, and nobody in this district knows it and nobody is going to find out in December.** **The car comes round:** eight men and a foreman and the man of twenty-six say *clear* on the loop, and **Jo Lask, who walked the length on his own time and was not a party to any of it and had not been asked, says from the north end, in a voice that carried six hundred yards and was not loud: *That is us. That is us and not a tram and it is the same noise.*** And it stops at a signal that works by itself twice an hour, and the signal is where it has always been. **The read-out at ten past twelve, seventh time, to about nineteen people, tally with nine lines: *I did not lose a route, and I have no idea why I did not lose a route, and I am writing that down because I have gone eleven weeks expecting to lose one every time and nothing has come off the list, and I do not want to be a man who has got lucky and calls it a method.*** And Dr Sen on the three weeks of not asking: **I want you to notice that you did it quietly and that a thing you do quietly is a thing that is going to get done.** At four o'clock **Rue walks him from the yard gate to his own front door without letting him look at the paper, on purpose, at his request and on one condition**, and it takes nineteen minutes and he could not have told you which way the chandler's gate was. *You can get home off that card. And you cannot get home off yourself.* At twenty to five **car 214 comes off the length, out through the north end, on the present's rails, under its own power, and runs its whole round and comes into Alder Quay depot on the shed road, and the yard man with the flag wrote the time and did not look inside the car.** The screen said 16.41 and **the screen had been right**, and the carbon came in at ten past five with **thirty-one alightings — twelve at the interchange, nine at the water stairs, ten at Crown Heights — and the line at the foot signed N. Bray against 16.36**, five minutes before the car came in, **and nobody has been able to account for the arithmetic.**

### Chapter 69 — The Car Comes Back

Monday the twenty-second, 17.10 to Tuesday the twenty-third, 16.00. **Hester Coombe goes down the carbon with a clerk's pencil and says the twenty-second line twice, and the second stroke goes through the first, and says: *I have never ticked a name off a sheet in my life because a name is not a thing you tick, it is a thing you read out. And I have just done it, and I would do it again tomorrow, and I would rather somebody saw me do it than found out in a month that I had.*** **In a shed at ten past six with about forty people in it, Nessa Bray, thirty-eight, gives account standing up, having asked for three things first and got all three — shed shut, carbon in the drawer, no reading out in a yard in the rain.** *I have not been lost and I have not been frightened, and I have been on that car for five days and two hours and I have said the next one out loud every single time, and I am the reason that car is a car. **If I stop talking it is not a car any more. It is a room with wheels.*** Nothing happened on the first day, and that is what she could not get through her head: *There was a corner… at about ten I stopped being able to tell you which lap we were on, and at about eleven a man of about forty sat down and did not get up for eleven hours, and I asked him at about four to move so that two people could get past, and he said no, and I said the word anyway, and he moved.* Second day they asked where they were going. Third day a woman of about seventy asked if her sister was on the car. **Fourth day a boy of about eleven asked her whether it was Tuesday and said *it was Tuesday on Friday too*, and she did not have an answer and has not got one now.** And then the moment: *Then this morning at about ten past twelve somebody said the word on that length and it was not me… **I have been on a car for five days and I have not been frightened of anything until that.*** She got one thing wrong — the sheet was in a depot office, not a yard — **and nobody in the shed corrected her, and Milo wrote both of that and the reason down, and only one of them is about the case.** **The record of the ninth crossing, in the authority's own instrument:** one side, carbon-backed, six columns, **07.30, fifty minutes; and 11.06, thirty-five minutes**, work done *walked, both turns, boundary at wall checked*, condition *serviceable*, signature **R. PRYCE**, and in the hours column the reason in nine words — **and no column for the thirty-one.** On Tuesday morning at ten past nine, **the record that cannot be recreated, in nine words from a works clerk:** *There is no form.* The fault box would say *ran through a length not on the diagram*, and **not on the diagram is not a place, and a length that is not a place is not something a car can be held on.** *It gets fixed in a Registry or it does not get fixed at all, and the Registry will want a name, and a name in the Atlas is entered against a rateable place, and a length is not a building and it does not have a street and it cannot be given one. **Which is the fifteenth of December, Mr Rook. That is your reach. That is the box somebody put *no rateable place* in.*** So the passage happened and the record of it is a line on a sheet about a drain, and **in about four months a department can say that no passage occurred and the district will not be able to prove it, and there is no appeal from that.** At eleven o'clock on the Tuesday, two sentences, in that order, because one has to be said first: **Mara Quill — *Nobody died.*** And **Hettie Brann — *a document is the only thing in this city that bites*** — **and the next one will not be a tram, and the next one will be something with children in it.** Pell Harrow puts Tern first, unasked, and then puts the man in the works jacket second, and **says he did not follow him and that he would do it again**, and has written down what the man was wearing. **A newspaper man at the depot gate, two men from a Council transport scrutiny panel in Crown Heights at two o'clock, and by four o'clock on the Tuesday there is a suggestion of a notice about a tram in three shops on Cardew Street** — and Hester Coombe reads the whole carbon out to the two men from the panel, from the second drawer of her own desk, in daylight, at a counter, to a person who asked, and says *I am not going to be able to give it to you again because I have not got a second copy and I am not going to pretend to anybody that I have.* **It shows where thirty-one people got off. It does not show where it was, and it cannot, and that is not a fault in my drawer.** The Field Book is silent on the twenty-second and the twenty-third.

### Chapter 70 — What Is Now On The Schedule

Wednesday the twenty-fourth to Sunday the twenty-eighth. **The Registry, the tram authority and Crown Heights shut at noon on the twenty-fourth and are shut four days, with thirty-three people alive on the Monday the twenty-second sitting in eleven kitchens waiting for machinery that is not going to run until the twenty-eighth, and about nine people have said since that the four days were the worst part of the week and that the worst part was that nobody could be told anything.** **The blank is marked three times and comes back nothing:** a nurse of twenty-three on a card on the Wednesday afternoon, a woman who keeps a round on a card on the Thursday morning — *that is a mark on a page and it is not me writing your route for you* — and a boy of seventeen with chalk on a second-floor landing on the Friday night, undated, and there are now two arrows on that wall and one of them is dated. **It is still blank. It is the thirty-fifth day of it, and the Field Book carries, on the twenty-seventh at 21.40, the only sentence in two hundred and thirty-odd entries that he has ever written in the imperative about himself: *The line home is not coming back. Write it down and stop asking.*** On the Friday the twenty-sixth at a quarter to eleven, **Pell Harrow and the second tin:** *a thing that exists in one place is a thing that has not happened yet*; the gang's weekly duplicate has gone in a tin at the end of a corridor for years and now goes in **a second tin in the plan room of the Civic Atlas Office, on the bottom shelf under the repair schedule**, with Dilley Farr's signature on it and **two copies is not a record and no department in this city is going to be stopped by two tins.** On Sunday the twenty-eighth at ten past ten, in a corridor in Crown Heights with about nine people in it: **a hearing still not set, a reporting officer still not named, and an amendment nobody can put in a room** — Cass Ardery, about forty-four, sixteen days of suspension, second-in-command on an investigation of a thing he classified, who is not the reporting officer because **there is no rule in this department that says a man who made a mistake cannot report on it, and not one rule that says he can, and that silence is the reason**; who noticed the document in her bag on about the sixth of December and **did not ask, and asked for that to be minuted**; and **the Lark Street grievance is named once and refused once and is not resolved and is not softened** — Rue: *In August you wrote a rule at the foot of a sheet and gave it away in a porch. In December you went into six hundred yards of tram route because a mechanic said a gang was the crew on a length. Those are the same decision twice and I have watched you make it twice and I have not said anything about it and I am not going to.* Nobody wrote it down and about four people noticed that nobody had. At half past two on the same afternoon, **four pages dated the twenty-second of December, issued under the office of the First Surveyor: SEASONAL SEAM AND GREYWAKE OUTLOOK, for the season from the second of January to the fourteenth of April, forecasting eleven weather-driven seam events** — *seam events are expected to be preceded by weather, not followed by it* — **and entering Mireward, nil, under a column headed *district stability*, with the reason given as *no maintained seam, no recorded loss, no resident witness, no schedule. Entered as stable.*** And: *A place is not stable because nothing has happened in it. A place is stable because there is nobody writing down what happens in it.* **The office of the First Surveyor is a working desk in an instrument; it has a name on four pages; it signed no name; there is no date anywhere in the document for the thing it is standing on; and the two questions Milo has wanted to ask have both been *how long has it been like this* and *who signed it*, and there was nobody in the building to put either to, and a man who is not asking is a man who is deciding, and he wrote that down too.** The last entry of the year: **nine pairs — six, nine, three, three, two, three, three, five, eight — and a tenth interval of a single day from the tenth of last month that was looked at and not written; the token cold on eight of thirteen days; two things gone, a sound and a taste, and *I do not know whether that is one thing or two… the order they frighten me is the order I would choose, and I am not allowed to choose*; and the sum of the batch, which is the outline's image: **I am not the author of any of it. I drew the frame of a sheet and read two rules out loud on a length. There is a line on a piece of paper in a rack that says *A car is on the length.
