The gang is the crew on the length*, and it is in a foreman's hand, and I am not in it, and I did not put a single word on the sheet that records the passage, because there was nowhere to put one.**

## Volume 02, Batch 0002 (Chapters 71–80)

### Chapter 71 — The First Of January

Thursday the first of January, and **the first of January is a weekday and the works did not shut.** Eleven men on the back length at half past seven in nineteen degrees, and it is the second walk of the week that runs from Monday the twenty-ninth of December to Sunday the third of January, so both walks are on one sheet and the reason column is empty on both, and the sheet goes into the rack on the Friday the second and the duplicate into a tin and the duplicate into the second tin in the plan room. **The city did not stop:** a man sweeps a hundred and ten yards badly at ten past six, a woman calls lot prices from a doorway at about twenty past five, a coffee man of about sixty puts his hand on a corner at half past five and walks eleven minutes back to a shed, the clinic counter opens at eight with nine people in it. **Seven children go back to school in the spring on a referral signed in the same minute and the same hand as the levy, two of the nine are still lost, a woman of sixty-eight is on nothing, a man of forty-one is on no list, and a man asleep on a third floor is not written down and has not been woken. None of it moved because it was the first of January, and about four people have noticed that the correct behaviour of all of it is not the same thing as it being all right.** The offices open at nine with a queue of about eleven at the district index counter, and every one of them wants something that cannot be given. **A clerk of about thirty-four does not say no, and that is the finding of the day: the book is not a record of what happened, it is a record of what is where; the rateable reference is the only one of three columns it will not do without; and about four months ago a man in this city tried to fill the second box in and could not and wrote *no person available* in it.** He spends from ten until four going round five counters and comes out with **seven doors**: the tram authority's running sheet, whose fault box cannot be completed from a record that has nowhere to say where the car was and has been half filled since the twenty-second of December — *a half-filled fault box in this authority does not generate anything; exceptions have to be cleared, and a half-filled box does not*; the Atlas Office's incident book, which is for people who were hurt; the Registry's continuity file, where a tram service amendment is not within any of the three matters; the gang's sheet; the custody register; the index counter; and nobody. *Seven doors and a place nobody can name, and none of them is a lie.* **Ends:** he does not cross, the tally has nine lines, the page home is the thirty-ninth day and blank, the token is cold, and the book has not written since the twentieth of December.

### Chapter 72 — There Is No Box For It

Monday the fifth, in four rooms above a fitter's shop, and a foreman's week turns out not to be a walk. **Milo Rook's attempt is a proposed entry in the district index: a name, a nature, a rateable reference reading *none — not a rateable place*, and a maintained-by line — and it is refused three times in four days, correctly three times — two of them in the quay section office on the Monday and the third at the wall on the Thursday, which is why it is a Thursday practice and not a Tuesday one.** A works clerk of forty-six: **a reference is a place other people can write into, and not one of the nine people who can find my four numbers has a shovel**, and *nil* in a rateable column is a hole with a sign over it, and **the rack will be cleared in about eleven years and no piece of paper will stop it and the thing on your paper will make it worse.** A man of thirty-one years in the route room: **a public map is not a place to put a length — a public map is a place people go to find out where they are, and the day there is a mark on the public sheet for six hundred yards that is not on the diagram, then a man reads the sheet and comes and looks for it, on his own time, with a dog, and there is nobody on it.** And a foreman of fifty-four at the wall in the cold in about nine words, who will not have a reference on his schedule because a row with two numbers on it is a row somebody asks about in four years, and who says: **ask me again in about four months, when you know what a fella in an office is going to say to me, and not now, because now it is a Thursday practice and in four months it is a document.** A working day is described in about two hundred words: a requisition, eleven rows of which one has a reason written in it in four years, stores, a write-off with a reason about a cost, a man of about fifty-four, and a man of about twenty-six who stops in the down loop on every walk. **The fourth answer, from a hut with a kettle on, in about ninety words: the sheet is the gang's, and it is not a record of the length; so where is the length's record — there is no such thing, there is *I am the record*. *You have spent four months finding out that a place is kept by people and documents, and that is the half of it. The other half is that a place is kept by people, and the document is how the next people find out.*** He writes the admission down: he wanted a piece of paper to say a thing happened, and that is a want about him and not about a tram.

### Chapter 73 — The Proceeding The District Cannot Attend

Wednesday the seventh, ten o'clock, a room above the Parcel Street canteen with a barometer in it, and **the authority's amendment committee under rule 14, and rule 14 paragraph three says a party must show an effect upon a person — a change in access to a route, a service, a rate, a residence, a burial place, or a school place — and is not an effect upon a document, a schedule, a map, a diagram, a practice, or a place that has no rateable unit and no address.** **The district cannot be a party, and there is exactly one person in eleven streets who can be asked the question, and she is the one the department made rateable on the fifteenth of December so that seven children could go to school in the spring.** Lena Marr, about thirty-four: *There is no effect on me. That is the answer… **The thirteenth of October is what keeps me whole.*** Asked whether she is content, she says it is a thing she has come to say, and asks it recorded that she was asked by a member of a tram authority's committee whether she is content. **The only person the committee will hear is Hester Coombe, depot clerk, fifty-one, twenty-two years, who works for the authority and came on her own time and told nobody: the fault box is completed from the car's own return, and the return has no column for where the car was, and there never has been one, because a car has always been somewhere that somebody was responsible for — and a half-filled fault box is the most dangerous thing it can be, because an exception has to be cleared and a half-filled box does not.** Then the chair puts the question that has been printed on the notice since the twenty-eighth of December, and **Advocate Quill takes about eleven seconds to answer it, and looks at the chairman and then at the table: *I hold a document that I am not permitted to produce, I am not going to say what it is, and I would like it recorded that I have declined to identify a document in my custody.*** She does not name it, does not confess to taking it, and is not asked; the chair declines to act on the statement because it is a refusal and not an assertion. **Cass Ardery, at volume, in a corridor, with a clerk in earshot, says he is not going to ask her and will not ask in February, and has decided it on purpose, and that he wrote a classification on the eleventh of the current year and has had four months to find out what it is like to hold a piece of paper that somebody else wants.** **The finding, in four paragraphs, includes the truest sentence anybody has written about the thirteenth of October: that the committee has been unable to identify the person who requested the amendment, and records that it has been unable to identify the person who requested the amendment.** Rue in the corridor: *There is not a person in that minute. There is a reference number and a blank line and a filing number in a box.* **Milo Rook does not know what she refused. He writes that the refusal was worth having and he believes it, and what he does not write down, and does not for six weeks, is that she looked at the table for about four seconds first, and that he learned in December what four seconds of somebody's hesitation looks like.**

### Chapter 74 — Somebody Else's Work

Thursday the eighth, and Pell Harrow is up the south and is not in the district, and the round is one man short and took twenty-two minutes longer. **The gang is on the back length at half past seven and it is not an event, and the sheet goes into the rack on the Friday the ninth.** The recovery is four people's work and Milo's part in it is nothing. **On the quay at about ten past seven, Rue asks a man of about twenty-six one question — *You stop in the loop. What for* — and he says: *Whether it still talks.*** There is a mark on the cover of it, a cross and a bar, put there by a man called Ferrier in about 1971 to say the drain was his, and a man of about forty put the surveyor onto it years ago and neither of them ever mentioned it, and it has had air in it every Monday and Thursday for two years. *I have not said it to you before today and I am saying it to you because you are not going to write it down.* / **I am not going to write it down.** / *No, and that is why I have said it.* **In the depot office at about eleven, Nessa Bray, thirty-eight, comes on her own time and asks for a copy of the carbon**, because her mother is sixty-nine and asked her where the sheet was, and Hester Coombe makes it on a machine about as old as the office with the door open and a witness in the room, and then tells her what the copy is: *There are about nine people in this city who have written your name on a piece of paper in a kitchen. You have a copy. That makes it ten, and it is the worst one, and I have said so in my own office.* **And Rue changes the round — it now goes through the quay section gate on Mondays and Thursdays only, four minutes longer each way, unpaid, not on a schedule** — *a tram authority's amendment committee cannot hear anything about a stop on a round, and I am not going to stand in a canteen and ask a man to put my round in a book so that it looks like the same fight. It is a gate that is open twice a week.* **And a man of about fifty in a works jacket was on that quay on the Monday the fifth at about ten past eight, in a doorway out of the rain, notebook out, wrote twice, and was not there on the Thursday; a card goes face up under a brick and nobody follows him on either day, and Rue: *He is not ours and we are not following him and I would rather be wrong on a Tuesday than right on a Sunday.*** The Field Book is four lines long and says so at the top of it.

### Chapter 75 — A Hearing As Of Right

**The hearing is set on Wednesday the seventh at half past four, twenty-six days after the order was suspended, and the reporting officer is named: I. Sarratt, Survey Enforcement, with C. Ardery second-in-command, and the notice says the hearing is claimed as of right and listed as of right and the entry is on the register and the register is not a departmental document.** It took three weeks and four floors and nobody obstructed anything: the department passed the file down, a clerk of about thirty-four crossed two names off a list of officers, and the answer came back on the seventh. **Cass Ardery said in December that it was not going to be set in January; he was wrong about January and knows it and nobody has told him so.** Ivo Sarratt, fifty-one, twelve years, asked in writing for the exclusion and for the order of reference on the letter of the eleventh of December, which is the only document that would let him apply to another department, and got it in twenty-two minutes. **The hearing of Monday the twelfth has three items and none of them is the neighbourhood: the grounds, the classification of the eleventh, and the removal of the sentence *no evidence was found of any hazard to persons at the service court*.** Milo does not speak. The removed sentence **cannot be restored by anybody in this city** — the withdrawal reference is not a district reference, is held in the office of the First Surveyor, has been asked for twice and acknowledged twice and not answered: *the department's position is that it cannot account for the removal and has never been able to.* The classification: *it was correct on the eleventh, it is not correct now, I confirm both halves of that, and it stays withdrawn.* **And the price, said out loud in a hall before she pays it.** Four pages on Registry paper, ten entries, twenty-six names, five conditions read into the record in order, and then: **Twenty-six names go into a file.** *I have told about nine of them that their names are not in any schedule, return, roster, roll, index or register, and one of those nine is a woman of sixty who has been off every list for nine years, and I am about to make that untrue in one line of a minute, and I am doing it because the alternative is that there is no hearing at all, and I would like it recorded that I did it knowing.* Nobody said anything and about nine of forty people looked at each other. **What is bought is the only thing this district has won in four months that nobody can take back on a Tuesday, and it is four lines and it is the reporting officer's and not the department's:** no schedule of persons is to be obtained from the district, in this proceeding or in any proceeding, while the answer of the fourth of December stands — *I have undertaken a thing I can deliver for about two years, and in two years I will either be in a different job or I will not be, and if I am not, this is a line with my name on it in a file and somebody can look at me.* **And the order is suspended and not discharged and the matter is adjourned to Tuesday the fourteenth of April, which is a departmental list day and not the reporting officer's, and Milo Rook hears it read out and understands it in four seconds** — the fourteenth of April, and a foreman of fifty-four who said in December that in about four months a man in an office is going to ask him whether he witnessed thirty-one persons. *I am not saying it is a conspiracy and I have not got a figure for it. I am saying that the department has a quarter and a list.*

### Chapter 76 — Six Columns And A Seventh

**The failure, and it took four seconds, and it is his.** On the morning of Saturday the tenth he writes a form headed *BACK LENGTH (unnumbered) — MAINTENANCE*, with the gang's own six columns copied off a weekly sheet, and **a seventh he writes before a single line under any of the other six: *If any of them stops.*** *Every one of those six columns is a thing that happened. Nothing in that form is capable of telling a person in a room in April what the state of anything is in June.* On the Saturday morning at about half past ten, at the north end of a hundred and ten yards, in a coat with the button off, he writes six lines about six people. **The last line is a woman of seventy-four with a scale and thirty-one years, and he writes *Ivy Marsden, 74* and then puts a single pencil line through the whole of it, undated, unreasoned, and folds the page in three and puts it in a pocket, and nobody saw him do it.** A man of thirty-one years reads the seventh column aloud in the route room, which is the only time in eleven years: **a box that wants to know why is the amendment** — *a man in a telephone in September asked a clerk for a schedule for index and did not want to know anything, and eleven weeks later six hundred yards of a city was not on the map and nobody had to want anything* — and refuses to sign it, and that is not a refusal. A seam physician reads it out on a counter on the Tuesday afternoon in a room with nine people in it, on purpose, and says the second time is worse than the first and gives the difference: **in November you held a page; on Saturday you filled it in.** *A thing you have destroyed has not happened and a thing you have hidden has happened twice, and I have watched four people in six years in this district be eaten by a page they could not get rid of.* **And at about a quarter past nine that evening it goes into the second tin, on the bottom shelf, under the repair schedule, because a foreman's rule in grease pencil says you cross the line out and date it and do not take the line off and do not ask anybody to start again, and it applies to this.** Dilley Farr has not read the seventh column and never will: *the last line is a name with a line through it and I am not going to know whose name it is, and I have never in thirty-one years been glad of a thing in a drawer more than I am going to be glad of that one.* **A woman's name is legible in a building in Alderquay and nobody will read it and it may be produced in about eleven years by somebody with a box.**

### Chapter 77 — A Map Other People Can Correct

Wednesday the fourteenth. **At half past four a tram gong goes off behind the cold store and at four seconds into it he cannot tell what it is, and he needed to, and it does not come back** — the second time, a sound, no face and no place and no person and no name. At about twenty past five the fishmonger calls the lot prices, **and the two minutes is not two minutes of prices**: *There is a crack in it. I put my thumb in the crack. That is all. It is not for anybody and I do not know why I do it and I have never told anybody.* He writes down *a wall* and does not write down a time and keeps that promise. At half past five the coffee man puts his hand on the corner, and **Milo Rook holds it — the first time in four months he has held anything at all, about ninety seconds, and it is a market and not a wound, and it is about the size of a smell, and he cannot tell whether his stopping and the hand coming off the stone were a quarter of a second apart or four minutes apart because there is nothing in the world to count with on a wall at half past five.** At about half past seven, in a yard of about nineteen, **a map of who does what and on which day changes hands and nobody asks Milo Rook anything**: a column headed *wrong*, and a new rule at the foot in Rue's hand — *cross it out, put the date on the strike, and put your own initials beside it. Do not tell anybody. Do not take a line off. Do not ask anybody to start again. **A line that is not right for two weeks in a row is not a mistake, it is a place that has changed, and it gets written again.*** And: *I have not had your advice and I did not ask for it, and I am telling you that on purpose, in front of nineteen people.* **The first line in the new column is written at about ten past eight by the man in the down loop, in his own hand, with a date on the strike and no name on it and none to be asked for**: the wall at the south end is also looked at twice a day by two people who are not the gang and who do not know each other, one at about ten past five and one at about six, and neither has ever told anybody, and he found both of them because a surveyor stood at a doorway for four days with a watch out. **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, in eight words: *A length is missed before it is lost.*** *It does not say by whom. It does not say what missing a length looks like from the inside. And a length is not a thing anybody owns, so it cannot be lost by anybody.* **Twenty-five days, the tenth pair, the first large interval in the book**, and he joins none of them to any other.

### Chapter 78 — A Box About Persons

Friday the sixteenth, in four rooms above a fitter's shop, a working day in which nothing whatever happened, and **the week's sheet for the week of the twelfth goes into the rack at ten in the morning, and the second tin now holds four weeks of carbon and one page that is not a gang sheet.** The cost that is not curable is a printed form: **the annual maintenance return, one side, in use since before the war, about four hundred of them in the authority, and the fifth line is a box headed *persons observed on the length during the year*.** A works clerk of forty-six says so in a yard: *It is a good box. Somebody thought of that box for a reason and the reason was a man in a cutting.* He fills the fourth line in about twelve words, comes to the fifth on the Monday afternoon at about half past four, writes **nil**, and puts the pencil down, and the two men in the room with him are silent for a minute and a half. **The reason, given out loud: if he writes persons in there, then next January there is a return in a file that says persons were observed on the quay service lengths, and the only name available to be asked is the foreman's, and a foreman of fifty-four will be asked in a room what he observed on a length of tram route, and there is no column on that form for the answer.** *A nil on a length is not a nil on a street, and the reason it is not is that there is nobody on a length to be observed and there is a gang on it, and those are two different facts and they are in the same box.* **Milo's counter-argument — write the crew in it — does not survive being written out loud, because the crew was not observed; the crew was the observer.** On the Wednesday the fourteenth, at about ten past ten, the sixth line is filled, and it is fifty-four words in a clerk's own hand, signed *C. SALE, clerk, 14.i.*, and it is the only true sentence anybody in Alderquay has written about the thirteenth of October: the length was added at the fourth edition in November per amendment 12/44 under 11/D/6, the margin note was his, **the third edition did not contain the length, and the amendment's lines for requested by and instructing officer are blank and he cannot supply either.** The foreman signs the return on the Wednesday morning with the sixth line not read out to him. **There is no copy, the district has no standing to ask for one, and Milo Rook will not find out in this volume.** *That is where a true sentence goes. Everywhere else it is a speech.* On the Friday afternoon, **the only thing an advocate with a two-year note can do with a form in another body's file, in nine minutes**: a letter to a company secretary asking that it be produced in original at the adjourned hearing, *I have no right to the document I ask for and I do not assert one* — and she says the rest of it is about a piece of paper in a drawer in her own flat, that it has nothing to do with an annual return, that she has not said it and is not going to, and that she has written down in advance that she was not going to and will read it back to herself on the day. **The batch's sum: the reason nobody can do anything about a box in a form in a file is that the reason the box is empty is a decision somebody in this district made correctly on the twenty-second of December and would make again. He was not wrong. The price of being right is on somebody else's form and nobody told the form.**

### Chapter 79 — Mireward, Nil

Monday the nineteenth. The gang is on the length at half past seven, and there is no sheet yet for that week in any rack, and the sheet will have two dates in it when it is signed off on the Thursday. **Three people act on a four-page forecast in nine days and two of them are right.** Dr Iona Sen opens the water clinic every day of the week of Monday the twenty-sixth from eight until six and tells about nine people at the counter: *I am not going to be told by a four-page document from an office that does not sign its name what day somebody in this district is ill. That is a claim on my counter and I have refused three of four.* Rue takes the round out at eight and in at seven for seven days and says the price out loud: *Seven days instead of five. The days are the expensive part. If somebody dies on the Tuesday of that week and I am out on the Friday with a satchel instead of a woman of sixty-three, then I have done this and got it wrong, and I would rather be the second one.* The tram authority does nothing, the Registry does nothing, and **the Civic Atlas Office cannot enter the forecast at all, because it came by hand, with no series number, no distribution list, no return and no address — *in about four years there will be a shelf of about nine hundred four-page documents in this room that are not in the register, and every one of them will have been on a table, and nobody will be able to say who put it there, and that is not a scandal, that is just what a table is.*** Then the query he wrote on the Friday the ninth, the four-line district returns query, which went into the internal post the same afternoon, and the card that came back on the Friday the sixteenth, seven days later, instead of an answer: *Mireward. Return required: quarterly ground and seam return. Last received: —. Reason outstanding: —. **Officer responsible: [blank].*** And the finding: **that column is not empty by error — the reason column is the machine's own dash and the officer column is a line under words, and a line under words is a place a person can write anything at all.** A man of about fifty in the drainage section, nineteen years in the room, says it plainly: **there is no district office and there never has been; Mireward is on the returns because a return list is a hundred and forty years old and has never been printed from a list of offices; a man of fifty-eight goes out on the first dry week with a rod and a chain and has been going since 1974 and writes four lines on a pad, and the pad goes in a drawer from this year back to about 1969, and nobody has ever asked him for one of them — and a man who goes out in a summer and writes four lines about a drain is not an office and does not become one, and in forty-one years nobody has ever written the word office next to that name on a piece of paper.** *It is not sinister and there is nothing to find and I have wanted to say it to somebody for about three years.* Dilley Farr gives the other half of his December sentence: **nothing is a very good answer, and the moment you put nothing in a column it stops being nothing and becomes a hole with a heading on it, and a heading is worse than nothing, because a heading is a thing somebody can look up.** And at about five o'clock, **Pell Harrow is back on the round, twenty-two days, on his own money, and says nothing, and puts a blank card face up under a brick** — *He is back and he is on the round and he is not going to say anything about it and nobody is going to ask him, and I would like that written down by somebody who is not me, and I know who is going to read this in a year and it is not a person in this yard.* **Tern is not found. A man of seventeen went twenty-two days up the south on a lead about a brother and came back with a blank card, and there is no document anywhere in Alderquay that says what that means.** Milo's standing refusal of the office of the First Surveyor: *I would be asking whether there is a man behind it, and if there is not then the forecast is a machine, and if there is then it is a man, and I do not want either answer this month and both of them are going to arrive anyway.*

### Chapter 80 — Who Keeps It

Wednesday the twenty-first, and the batch's answer and its larger question. **On Sunday the eighteenth — the seventeenth is a Saturday in this batch's chain — a foreman of fifty-four wrote seven lines in grease pencil in the corner of the return-route card, for nobody, and the card goes to version two: *The gate is shut Mondays and Thursdays 07.30 to about 08.30, for the walk, and the key is on a nail inside the hut and eleven men need it… Do not go through that compound in a car. There is nowhere to turn. **This is not a route and I am not redrawing anybody's route.** R.P., 18.i.*** **And for the first time the four copies differ — three say version one and one says version two — and four copies is not a document, three copies of a thing and one other copy of a thing is a thing two people disagree about, and somebody has to hold it.** On Monday the nineteenth at ten past nine, **the holder is a woman of sixty-one at a clinic counter who has been on it nineteen years, who is neither Mara Quill nor Milo Rook, who was asked for a route once in December and said no, and who takes the envelope with one condition and refuses to be thanked:** *I am not doing it out of kindness… I am taking the envelope because there are four pieces of paper in four buildings and somebody has to know whether they are all saying the same thing.* Her rule, in her hand, in about eleven seconds: **first working day of the month — the four copies are taken out of the four places and looked at, the version and the date are compared and nothing else is read; if one of them is not the same, ask the person whose hand it is and nobody else; the holder is not M.Q. and is not M.R. and will not be.** *The first working day of the month was the first of January and there was no holder then… The next one is the second of February.* And: *If you are ill, then the round does it… and I would like that written down by somebody as the second cost of this card and not as a favour.* Then **the run number** — the manifest has a sixth column, every booked working has a run number against it, **a run number is not a destination**, Hester Coombe has not looked it up and says so, a card goes under a brick on the Wednesday afternoon reading *The run number on the carbon of the 07:52, 16 Dec. To be looked up in the working timetable. Not before Friday. — H.C.*, **and it is still under the brick on the Thursday morning and nobody chases it, and the reason is on the page in eleven words — *a card under a brick is a thing, not a job* — and the run number is not looked up.** Then eleven items on the schedule, and the standing question: *A length is a distinct kind of place for exactly as long as the people who walk it keep walking it, and the whole of the machinery in this city is designed to find out who is responsible, and there is nobody, and there has never been anybody, and the only reason six hundred yards of tram route is still a place is that about fourteen people in this district are not being paid for any of it.* **Ends:** *I am going to be at a north end at ten to seven tomorrow morning with a chain I will not use, and I am going to be at a counter on the second of February, and I am not going to write any of this down again until it has moved.*

---

# Volume 02 — The City Underneath the Survey

## Volume 02, Batch 0003 (Chapters 81–90)

**The season arrives.** Monday the twenty-sixth of January to Friday the twentieth of February, twenty-six days, and the volume's immediate promise is answered and is not re-opened, and the volume's larger question is moved one step and not answered. **Milo Rook does not cross.** The tally has nine lines and the crossing count is nine and the remembered-route list is ten. Atlas residue was used **once in ten chapters, in Chapter 89, in six words.**

### Chapter 81 — Nobody Can Name The Day

The week of Monday the twenty-sixth: a water clinic counter open eight until six for seven days and a round out at eight and in at seven for seven days, both decided by one woman each, **and the four pages that caused them cannot say which day, and neither of the two women wanted to be told.** Four days of nothing. **Then, at about twenty past two on the morning of Thursday the twenty-ninth, rain off the south-west for about forty minutes, and at half past seven that same morning the gang is on the length and the man of about twenty-six stops nine feet short of a drain he has put his ear on every Monday and Thursday for two years, and does not go down, and gets a shovel.** The cover is up out of its frame by about two inches with the ballast washed from under about a foot of it and a hole the size of a dinner plate at the north side. **The foreman writes four words in the condition column — *Cover up. Ballast washed. Hole.* — and the reason column is empty, and there is no column that wants to know why a drain cover came up at twenty past two in the morning.** *It filled. There was rain in the night… and I did not come down here, because it is a drain and it is not a road and nobody was on a car.* Nobody was on a car, and a foreman of fifty-four said it, and it is the truest thing said about that morning and has not been improved on. The clinic shut at six on the Wednesday and Dr Iona Sen writes the two facts **in the same line with no mark between them**: *The clinic was shut four hours before it and I am writing both of those down together on purpose and I am not putting a line between them.* Two inches standing in the low streets by ten on the Friday — **a Tuesday and not a flood, and four months after four inches in a month with nine-tenths of an inch of rain.** Water under the back door of the cold store, nine inches inside; the boatyard boards at the low end under, and the only way round is the wall: *I could not get the boards under at the low end and I went round the wall, and the wall is the only way round and it is not a road.* Seven doors, the same answer seven times, and an eighth place with something in it that is nothing about the night: a heading, and four under it, and one of the four now arguable. Field Book: the sixty-seventh day.

### Chapter 82 — The Allocation On The Day

Friday the thirtieth, in a drainage section, and the machine that prints about nine hundred district returns cards a month also prints the only stationery anybody has ever printed in order to have something to be given to on the day: a **SEAM EVENT ALLOCATION** sheet, five rows, one side, columns for *events forecast / event / allocated to / officer responsible*, and a line at the foot that an allocation which cannot be returned is a sheet in a drawer. The forecast column is not typed — it is the four pages' own figures, transferred by hand with a pen by a man of about fifty, fifty-one next month, nineteen years in the room. **The first entry anywhere in the city, on any form, about that night is five words and a time and two initials in his own hand in the river core row: *29.i. 02.20. Cover up in loop, ballast washed. Back length. No persons. — G.S.R., 30.i.*** *No persons* is the only honest answer available and it is the answer the form wanted. Then the **correct refusal**: he fills four *officer responsible* boxes off a wall board that has never once been wrong about a building and will not fill the fifth, because *if I put a name in the box for a district, then in about four years somebody asks that person what they were doing on the night of the twenty-ninth of January, and there is no column on this form for the answer.* And the cost of being right: **the sheet goes back with a line missing and the machine answers on Friday the sixth of February, seven days later, with a district returns card whose reason column now has a sentence in it where it had a dash — *allocation sheet w/e XXVI, officer column uncompleted* — and a sentence is a thing a person can be sent to look at.** Mireward carries a typed **0**, and that is the third nil about Mireward in nine weeks and the first two were a card and a column entered by an unnamed desk: ***nothing about Mireward is a record of Mireward; a place is entered as nil because there has been nobody to write down what is in it, and the two look identical on a form and they are not the same and the second one is worse.*** And the batch's institutional answer, in nine words: ***a seam event is not a gang going out… a sheet like that is for having something on the day to give somebody, so that a district is told. It has never once told anybody what happened. It has only ever been the thing that was given. An allocation is a claim that somebody was told, and the gap between those two things in this city is about eleven months and a filing cabinet, and that gap is where this season is going to happen.*** Field Book: the sixty-eighth day.

### Chapter 83 — Ten Hours And No Reason

Saturday the thirty-first, the sixth day of seven, forty people in ten hours, and the chapter is the least remarkable thing in the batch and is written to be the one that is still true in nine years. **The person in the day is Ola Petrossian, twenty-three, four months in the job, who has never been past the tram steps and has never been thanked for a card with a number on it.** She writes eleven clinic forms with a time, a name and *R1/R2/R3* in a box that is not printed, **fills in four of the five printed boxes headed *nature of attendance*, then puts a ruler over the column and does not use it again: *everything I would write in that box is a sentence about a person, and the minute it is a sentence about a person it is a record of a person… I have not torn it off. It is still there and anybody can lift the ruler and see that I did not fill it in, and that is better than tearing it off… a time is not a person.*** At about ten past four **Doreen Sankey** stands at the counter in a wet coat and there is no box on that machine for a man with no rateable unit, and Hettie Brann writes his name on a form and puts it face down under the counter and says, at volume, in front of nine people, that she has made a piece of paper about a man on purpose, is sixty-one, and is not going to be the one who is asked about it in four years — and refuses to be thanked. And the counter's method, said out loud unasked: **I know which people in this district are not ill. And it is not my job to say so, and I do not say so, and I have never once said so, and that is the reason this counter has never had a fight on it.** And the **Stage 2 cost, its third instance, a taste**: nine cups of the same tea in the same pot and at the ninth he put the cup down, because he had wanted to know whether he had been awake since seven and had used taste as an instrument and it was not there; he uses a watch instead. *A taste. There have been a sound and a taste… neither of them has a face on it and neither of them has a place or a person or a name on it.* The ten hours end at six on the Sunday, the last of the seven, and the doctor turns the closed-side-out card back in the frame and says it six times in a doorway and nobody writes it down. Field Book: the sixty-ninth day.

### Chapter 84 — A Form For A Season

**The failure, and it is his, and it is the same mistake for the third time in five weeks.** Saturday evening, nine to eleven, a cold cup of tea: a form headed *SEAM EVENTS — RIVER CORE — WEEKLY*, the gang's own six columns, and a seventh headed **SCHEDULED**, which is not a question. *It is a column of days. It wants to know when. And a date is the one thing in this district that nobody can supply, and he supplied it, and he supplied it in about four seconds.* He fills nine days of it in nine minutes **off a card two inches by three in a frame at the water clinic, which says what time the round goes past the door and is there because a nurse needs to know** — *a thing somebody built for one purpose, and it is the wrong shape for another.* He reads it out standing up in a yard of about nineteen on the evening of Sunday the first of February, and Rue reads it out after him, word for word: *You have written my round in a column with a day against it, and I did not give you the days… you have got it off a card on a wall because there is a nurse in that room who needs to know when I walk past.* **Lena Marr, in forty seconds of arithmetic:** *if she stops — and she will stop — then there is a piece of paper in a drawer in Crown Heights with a date on it that says the round went out at eight and in at seven for the week of the second of February. And that paper will not know whether it happened or not, and it will say it happened, and in about four years somebody in an office will be able to look at it and say that the round ran… and they will be right, because it is on the form.* That is our tram. **Dr Iona Sen asks one question from the back of the yard in a wind — *What happens on the day it does not come* — and he answers it himself, in a yard, out loud, in about eleven seconds: *then this district is a district that was expecting weather-driven seam events on the ninth of February… and a district that expects them and does not get them is a district that has been asked a question about them. And Mireward is entered as nil… and I have just written the column that makes this district the same kind of thing, and the only difference between this district and Mireward is that about nine people have been in this district and nobody has been in Mireward.*** He has produced the case against his own district and it cannot be unsaid. Pell Harrow says the standing refusal about the brick — *four sightings is not a pattern, two cards on one brick is not a pattern* — and the other thing, which is that nobody has asked him and nobody is going to, and that the not-saying is getting worse on a schedule. The sheet is not destroyed: he strikes the seventh column, dates the strike, writes no reason, and puts four people's objections on the back in their words, and **it stays in his own coat**, because *there is nowhere in this city to put a bad idea of mine that is safer than me* — and the doctor's finding is that **getting better at hiding them is not the same as being right and is further from being right than the first one was.** Field Book: the seventieth day, standing up.

### Chapter 85 — The Wrong Shape For A Day

Monday the second of February, the first working day of the month. The gang reset the cover at half past seven — *Cover reset. Rocking.* — the sheet goes to the rack on Friday the sixth, **and by then the second tin holds seven weeks of gang carbon and one page that is not a gang sheet, and nobody has reported the cover and nobody can, and the reason is nine words from a works clerk of forty-six: not on the diagram is not a place, and a length that is not a place is not something a cover can be reported against.** At about half past nine a man of about forty sweeping the Row badly asked a woman of about forty-five whether a piece of paper in a yard was right, and she brought it up the Row at ten past ten, **and she makes the first initials that have ever gone on the *wrong* column**, because *a thing done somewhere else is not the same thing and this sheet has got nothing at all for the same thing done somewhere else.* Then Milo Rook says the half of Rue's rule nobody has said for three weeks: **a column headed *wrong* is a column about a person; a line is somebody doing a thing on a day; and the ground is not somebody and the day is not somebody, and so the column is the wrong shape for what happened on Thursday night.** And what comes out of it in eleven minutes is not his: **the sheet has a second side, headed in the fishmonger's hand *THE SAME SHEET, OTHER SIDE* — *A day nobody stood in. A thing a day did to a place. Not right, not wrong, not a person* — with a rule at the foot in her hand: *Nothing on this side is anybody's fault and nothing on this side is a reason. A side with nothing on it is a day when nobody stood in the weather.*** The first line on it is the man of about twenty-six's, and **he signs it, for the first time, and gives the reason: the first side is about me and you do not put your name on a line saying you have stopped doing a thing; this side is about the day, and if somebody asks me in four years I will say I stood in it and I will not have to say why.** And the problem with the second side is named out loud by Rue and not hidden: *it is the same box with different words on it and one of the words is how many times, and a form that says how many times is a form somebody will ask about in a year.* Field Book: the seventy-first day.

### Chapter 86 — Where The Paper Is Soft

Monday the second, in the half hour the counter is shut for, and again at ten past five. **The recovery and it is somebody else's work.** Hettie Brann compares version and date and nothing else: three say version one, one says version two — **and the only one of the four people who ever puts a date on that card cannot say which is right, because a version number is a claim and he made the claim and checked nobody.** At about ten past five, in a corridor, she asks **Doreen Sankey, forty-one, on no list at all, one question — *how do you know a paper has been read* — and the answer is the whole of the chapter: *you fold it back wrong… paper's soft where it's been handled. It's not a mark and it's not a hand and it's not a date.*** He goes and does it in about an hour on the tide. He reports **three** of the four: one has been out once, one has been out and put back and out again, one has not been out since it went there. **He refuses to describe the fourth, and the reason he gives is that nobody asked, and the reason nobody asked is the rule at the foot of the envelope — and Hettie Brann reads the rule back and finds out what her own rule is for: *it is not for stopping two people disagreeing. It is for not making a person admit where they keep their papers.*** And her finding about the rule at the foot of his July–January sentence: **the only question anybody in this district has ever been asked is written on a card, and there is nobody in the world to ask it.** Then the standing limit, in his mouth: **a crease is not a date. A fold is not a chronology.** What she does is forty seconds long: the date on the outside of the envelope, in her own hand, four copies back in four places, nothing altered, nothing read, and the versions still disagreeing and due to disagree on the second of March — *I have not fixed anything and I want that said in front of about four people.* And Doreen's other sentence, the wrong shape again: **the card says how to turn and it does not say whether there is a way to turn** — which is not to be put on the card, because a foreman wrote *this is not a route and I am not redrawing anybody's route.* Pell in the yard: three things under the brick, one of them his and blank since the nineteenth of January, and *four sightings is not a pattern, two cards on one brick is not a pattern.* Field Book: the seventy-first day, and the batch's inventory of the one shared failure: every method in this district answers the question it was built to answer and **not one of them answers whether a person is on a page** — and the instrument that does answer it is a person on no list, and it does not scale, and he is not going to build a version of it.

### Chapter 87 — Twenty-Six Names, And One Of Them

Thursday the fifth of February, and **the cost is paid in a room Milo Rook is not in**, and he is told about it twice in two yards by two people who each told him a different half and neither of them asked him for anything. Lena Marr's three things, in that order: the twenty-six names, and that the woman of sixty on the second landing is the ninth and is the one she has told fewest people about; the three questions — *is it in a file, can it be taken out, can I see it* — and one answer, and the second and the third are the same no; and the reason they are the same no, **which is the district's one permanent win.** *In January this district won the only thing it has won in four months that nobody can take back on a Tuesday… and that is the reason a woman of sixty on a second landing cannot get a copy of the only piece of paper in this city that has her name on it. Because the way she would get it is a schedule of persons, and schedules of persons are exactly what is now forbidden, and it is forbidden by us, and we asked for it, and I drafted the ask.* And Milo Rook, in four seconds, the fourth time in five weeks he is the fastest person in a room: **that ruling is doing two jobs — it is protecting two hundred and fifty-two people from a schedule and it is preventing one woman of sixty from finding out what is written about her; and a minute records what a department agreed and not what a department's victory cost.** *I am not criticising him… I am saying the other half of it out loud in a yard, because in about four years there is going to be a file with twenty-six names in it and one line saying that nobody may ask for the list, and somebody in a room is going to read those two things next to each other and come away feeling very well.* **The woman of sixty's twenty-three words**, repeated in a yard on the Friday and not improved, on the condition Lena Marr will repeat them word for word or not at all: *I have been off every list for nine years and I did it carefully and I did it on purpose and nobody in this building ever asked me why, and that has been the arrangement for nine years… The file does not bother me. What bothers me is that I cannot get at it. I cannot see it, I cannot correct it, I cannot come off it, and I cannot ask anybody whether my name is spelled right.* And her other two: that Lena is going to come to about nine of these rooms one at a time and this is the first and it is not the same as coming about a form, **and she is the only one of the twenty-six who is going to notice that and it is hers**; and that **Lena is the list** — twenty-six names in her head and in a plastic wallet, not written down, deliberately, and one day somebody will ask her for it and she will have to decide then whether she is a person or an office. Field Book: the seventy-fourth day, and the token at the sixtieth, and the number left alone.

### Chapter 88 — What It Is On The Morning After

Thursday the twelfth of February, a whole working day, and **the answer is given by nine working people and not one of them is a surveyor, and the surveyor is the one asking.** From half past seven to about four he asks the same question in nine places — *what do you do when it does not come* — and gets eight answers that are each the whole of that person's job (I do the walk. I sweep it. Badly. I fill in the boxes. I print a card. I nail the boards. I open the counter. I put my hand on the corner. I pay for it.) and one that is an answer, from the man of about twenty-six at the north end, unasked, who tells Milo to write it down and then put his own name on it as well: ***you stand in it on the day it does not come as well as on the day it does… you cannot tell, from inside a day, whether a thing is going. I have never once known. I just turn up. And if I only turn up for the ones that happen then I have not been keeping anything, I have been collecting.*** And then the cost, given away in nine words by the same man without knowing: **you will not be able to tell which blank was a day nobody stood out in and which was a day there was nothing to see. So a blank is not a record of nothing. A blank is a record of a distinction that cannot be made** — *and that is the whole of what I have got to say about the second side and I have said it to a surveyor in the open on a length of tram route and I am not going to say it twice.* Then **the collision, found by a woman of sixty-one at a counter off a card on a wall**: the season runs to **the fourteenth of April** and there is a hearing in the Hall of Wards on **the fourteenth of April**, *and one of the two is about weather and one of the two is about a sentence somebody took out of a report six years ago, and neither office has told the other that they share a day* — and it cannot be put in writing by anybody who is allowed to write anything, because the district cannot show an effect upon a person, and a counter cannot write to a company secretary, and a surveyor would make it into a document with a reference on it. And at about five past four, a hundred and forty feet of named frontage behind a fence with a settlement plate and a gang on a fortnightly inspection, **eleven minutes on the far side of a fence writing nothing down**, and Rue saying one sentence to the fence and walking on: **it will not start with a noise, it will start with a schedule, and there is not one in this district that I know of and I have been looking for one since the twenty-second of December.** Field Book: the eighty-first day.

### Chapter 89 — The Second One, In Daylight

Thursday the nineteenth, rain off the south-west from half past one to about ten to five, and **the second weather-driven event happens in daylight in front of about nine people and nothing at all happens.** The gang was on the length at half past seven that morning and saw nothing and wrote four words about a cover. At about ten past five, on a hundred and ten yards of frontage that is not a street: **fish scales out of a wet stone floor for the first time in nineteen years**, about nine thousand of them, in the run of a gutter, confirmed by the man of about forty who has swept that row since he was twenty-one; **coffee at ten to five, forty minutes early, in rain, with no coffee sold within a hundred and ten yards of that Row since the year before the war, and six people who could not say where it was coming from, and the man it belongs to was not there and has not been asked**; **seven marks under a rail with six irons on it**, measured at about five inches by three, standing water in each, no iron absent that anybody can name — *and there is no list of irons anywhere in this city because nobody has ever needed one, and a thing with no list cannot have a thing taken off it.* He goes toward the scale at the north end and **slows down about nine feet short of it and goes back up the Row, and nobody saw either of those two things except a woman of sixty who is not going to say anything about it** — and the reason he does not write the poise down is the entire standing consequence of the tenth of January: *I have got one sheet of accountancy paper in a building in this city with a name on it, undated and unreasoned, and I am not going to be the reason there are two. If that is the only thing that keeps me off a piece of paper then it is the best thing anybody has done for me in five months and it was done by accident by a foreman writing a rule in grease pencil about not taking a line off.* **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, six words, in the field, on a wall, at ten to six, in the rain:** ***Weather uses whatever door is open.*** *It does not say whose door. It does not say what is behind it. It does not say whether the door was open before today. And I am not going to find out what a door is by shutting one.* **The second half of the day is procedure and dread: the second event is not allocated, and the reason is nine words long — *I have got one row and it is a district. A row of frontages that is not a street and has no address is not a district and it is not a length, and there is no box on that form for it*** — and the Row is not a fourth kind of place and is not a new category, and the hole is the same hole as the first of January: *the city's paperwork is built out of places and this is a place and it does not fit.* And a **fourth sighting** of the man in the works jacket, at about ten past two, forty yards off the north end, in a doorway, notebook out, wrote once, not there on Thursday the fifth, and Pell Harrow's refusal in a yard: *four sightings is not a pattern, two cards on one brick is not a pattern, and if I put a line under any of them I will have made a column out of a doorway and a book on a shelf.* Field Book: **the ninth of the nine written since the twenty-ninth of January and the thirteenth pair — one six, four threes, one nine, one two, one five, one eight, one twenty-five, and a three and two sevens added in February**, the pair in front of this one seven days off the twelfth, the four threes not collapsed, the single-day interval from the tenth of December still looked at and not written.

### Chapter 90 — The Fourteenth Of April

Friday the twentieth, and the run number is advanced without the volume's final image being written. **It was looked up in the last week of January, in four minutes, by a depot clerk of fifty-one**, and a run number in that authority belongs to a crew and not to a car, so the working timetable for the week commencing Sunday the fourteenth of December carries it against two things on the morning of Tuesday the sixteenth — the 07:52 booked working, and a depot move to the works at a quarter to eight, a light engine and a two-car set. **He writes both down on the back of the card under the brick and chooses neither, and the line on the front of it is not taken off.** And the rule that makes it work, from Doreen Sankey: *a card under a brick is a thing and not a job, and about four times a day somebody looks at a job and decides it is not theirs, and four times a day is a very different instrument from once.* **A letter arrives on the Tuesday the seventeenth from a company secretary of about sixty — four paragraphs, three correct refusals, and a fourth that confirms the return is filed under its own reference and bears the date of completion while expressly not treating that as a disclosure of its contents. A reference and a date and no copy: the seventh door again in a different building.** Mara Quill has written a second letter and has not sent it and has written down in advance that she is not going to and will read it back on the day, and has said out loud in a corridor, on purpose, twice now, with a clerk within thirty feet both times, that she is not going to ask him about the seventh of January. **The corridor, in about eleven words each way, and nothing else: he has not crossed and the tally is nine; she is saying it out loud because in about two months the first weather-driven event anybody allocated will be on a printed sheet and there will be a great deal said in offices about what the Atlas can do in a season, and none of it will be true, and the truest thing about the winter happened because two women put seven days into a counter and a satchel on their own signatures.** Then the schedule from the twentieth of February to the fourteenth of April, **eleven items, not one line of it in his hand**: the walks and the racks and the tins; the annual return with the box about persons filled nil and the sixth line filled once in fifty-four words on the fourteenth of January with no copy and no standing and no way for him to know; the two working days that are on nobody's list; **the second of March and the first of April, four copies out of four places compared for version and date and nothing else, and it is known since the second of February that three will say version one and one will say version two and that the fourth has been read and will not be described**; the fourteenth of April in the Hall of Wards, adjourned, a reporting officer's name on a register, a line with a name on it and not a department on it, a sentence removed from a six-year-old report nobody can produce, and a departmental list day and not the reporting officer's; the four pages ending the same day; the January return that will be filled in again in January and in every January after it; the page in the tin; the card under the brick; the second side with four lines and about nine blanks; **and the district's prices carried and none of them moved, including one reference to a woman of sixty-eight on nothing.** Then the standing question, in the same two mouths and not answered: three nils about Mireward in nine weeks, a man of fifty-eight with a rod and a chain in the summer, nobody gone and nobody going, four of the eleven in the river core, two things that have happened here in nine days, **one of them a line in a man's own hand in a printed sheet with a district in the row and the other one happening on a hundred and ten yards of frontage in the rain with no row anywhere in this city for it** — and *a place is not stable because nothing has happened in it, and a place is entered as nil because there is nobody writing down what happens in it, and those two facts look identical on a card out of a machine and they are not the same, and the second one is worse.* Field Book: the eighty-ninth day, the tenth of the ten, and the answer he will be asked for and the answer he is giving in advance: **is this district stable? — there is no such question about this district, and there never has been, and the reason it is not being asked yet is that eleven streets with a clinic counter and a round and a gang and about fourteen people who are not paid for any of it cannot be entered in a column, and a district that cannot be entered in a column is not a district that can be asked to be stable, and if this city ever gets round to asking it then somebody is going to have to open a drawer and find a man asleep on a third floor of a building with no number, and that is not a joke and it is not mine.** And: *I am not going to be the author of the back length's maintenance, and I have not been for five weeks, and a man of fifty-four walks it twice a week and a woman of about forty-five puts her thumb in a crack twice a day and a man of about sixty puts his hand on a corner for four seconds twice a day and none of them knows me and I have known all three of them for five months and I have not asked one of them for anything, and that is the only thing I have done this winter that I would be willing to write the word method next to.*

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0004 (Chapters 91–100)

**The second month of the season, and the fortnight before the fourteenth.** Saturday the twenty-first of February to Wednesday the first of April, forty days. The volume's immediate promise is answered and is not re-opened, the hearing is answered and is not re-opened, and **this batch does not resolve the volume and does not write the volume's final image.** **Milo Rook does not cross.** The tally has nine lines, the crossing count is nine, the remembered-route list is ten. Atlas residue was used **once in ten chapters, in Chapter 95, in six words.**

### Chapter 91 — The First Two Blanks

The second side of the sheet of who does what and on which day has four lines on it and **every one of the four is on a Monday or a Thursday, because the round and the gang are on the district on a Monday and a Thursday and the sheet travels with the round.** The fishmonger calls at ten past five every day; the sweeper sweeps at ten past six every day; the coffee man puts his hand on a corner at half past five every day and has done for nineteen years. **A blank on that side is a day when nobody stood in the weather, and the side has been blank on five days in seven since the second of February** — the first two blanks are Saturday the twenty-first and Sunday the twenty-second, because the round does not go at a weekend. Rue wrote that rule in eleven minutes on the second of February and has now been told by Milo Rook that it is not true on five days in seven, and she says: *I did not think about it, and I have been thinking about it for nine days.* Milo has been sitting on it since Saturday and has not written a form and has not written a column. The fifth **SEAM EVENT ALLOCATION** sheet comes off the machine on Friday the twentieth for the week commencing Monday the twenty-third, and **a man of about fifty leaves it face down on his own desk over the weekend for the first time in nineteen years and cannot afterwards say why he decided to.** The river core row still says **4** and he has not reduced it, and Mireward still says **0**. And the sentence of the morning: ***in January the wrong thing on that sheet was a hole and a hole has a shape, and this month the wrong thing on it is a four.*** Hettie Brann tells the room they have all got the face they had on the twentieth of January and that it is not going to be a different face by the fourteenth of April. Field Book: **the ninety-second day**, token the seventy-eighth, and no entry on the twenty-first or the twenty-second and the reason is not a reason a book can use.

### Chapter 92 — Allocated To

The sixth allocation sheet, for the week commencing **Monday the second of March**, and, four days later than the Monday she had it written down for, the **second of the three monthly comparisons** at the counter at about four, in the half hour after four that the counter is shut to the public. The chapter's object is a column. **The SEAM EVENT ALLOCATION sheet has four columns and the fourth — *allocated to* — has never been filled in by anybody in nineteen years, and a man of about fifty explains the difference in eleven words: the box under *officer responsible* is a promise that somebody is on the other end of it, and the box under *allocated to* is a record of the promise being kept, and it is the only one of the four on that printed side that is about a person doing an act, and the act is handing somebody a piece of paper on a day, and there is no day.** Three names go in off a wall board that has never once been wrong about a building, three of them unasked and untold. Mireward is refused in twenty words and **the refusal is a different one from January's and it is better**: *there is nobody to hand it to… if his name is on a piece of paper then somebody in about four years is going to ask Percy why Mireward was not allocated in the first week of March… and I have just done the January thing to him in a different column and got further with it.* The river core box is left blank, and the reason is not a principle: **Dilley Farr is in this building and walks to about eleven o'clock for a cup of tea and a man of fifty has watched that for nineteen years**, and a name in that box is a claim that he was handed a piece of paper about a seam event on a day, and there is no day. **A district returns card comes back on Friday the sixth with a sentence where it has always had a dash: *allocation sheets w/e XXIII and II, allocated-to column uncompleted*** — *a machine is a much worse thing than a man about being difficult, because a man stops and a machine does not.* And at the counter, **Hettie Brann writes a number on the outside of the envelope in pencil for the first time — *1 of 4 differs*** — and says it is the only kind of number she has ever been willing to put on a piece of paper, because a count of houses goes up and a count of people goes up or down and a count of documents that do not agree with each other is not a complaint about the world. Field Book: **the hundred and third day**, the gap in front of it **eleven days**, and he is not writing the two ends of it down because a silence is not a pair.

### Chapter 93 — Six Stops On A Hand Barrow

**Nell Crale, sixty-three, Bridge Street low end, four rooms over a shop, twenty-two years of a milk-and-ice round in two baskets on a hand barrow, six stops, forty-one pounds, and no machine on that street because a continuity order of six years back and a temporary route and a fenced hundred and forty feet of frontage and about nine other things took them away and were replaced by nothing.** A working day, **not in a yard**, and the ordinary thing is done well by somebody who is not the protagonist and who beats him. Stop one is a corner shop that has had no ice since about 1971 and has never said so; **she will not take a machine because the machine would have a plate on it and a plate has a number on it and a number is for a schedule, and the day she is on a schedule one of these six stops becomes an appointment, and an appointment can be moved, and nobody in the world needs an appointment to be moved.** Stop two is the cellar — six inches of silt with salt in it, a frost that has not gone out in nine years, a pipe laid before she was born, the works' answer is November — **and nobody came in the March either and she has stopped saying so.** Stop three: he measures the water at a door with a rule and is wrong by about a third, because he measured a roof and not a road, and she gives him the instrument he does not have: *everything that came off nine roofs… I know it is ninety feet because I have walked it and I have not measured it, and **grit in an axle is a low place and it is not a roof and it is not a measurement and it is not on any form and you cannot buy one.*** Stop six is a house of a woman of seventy-nine who does not get up before eight and does not go on a Friday because her daughter comes at four and not on a Friday — ***I have a week in my head that nobody in this district has ever written down, and it is not a schedule and it is not on a rota and it is not a column, and I have never had to say it to anybody, and I have been doing it since the second year.*** And at about half past ten, a **board outside a portacabin with four questions in a clerk's hand and the fourth one *subsidence observed*, a tick and a time and no column for anything else**, which is what her mother's boy died of, and which he does not write down, and which he asks her nothing about. Field Book: **the hundred and eighth day**, the pair in front of it a **five**, and a taste that is not coming back and is not being explained.

### Chapter 94 — Why There Is No Line

**The failure, and it is his, and it is the fourth time and it is the same one.** Saturday afternoon at a kitchen table: a form headed **SECOND SIDE — DAYS WITH NO LINE**, the columns of the sheet it is about, and a seventh headed **WHY THERE IS NO LINE ON THIS DAY** — *a question about a practice, and there is no such question in this district that has ever been answered by anybody.* He fills in the Saturday and the Sunday **and fills them in well**, and both reasons are true and neither is about a day. On the Sunday he reads it out standing in a yard of about nineteen and **Doreen Sankey says four words before he has got to the bottom of the page: *There is a why in it.*** Then the fishmonger, from her own doorway, on what a rule at the foot of a sheet is for: **a rule at the foot of a sheet is for the person whose hand it is going to be asked about, and your seventh column is a rule for nobody, because there is not one person in eleven streets whose hand is going to be in the answer** — *and the answer is for a man in an office in about four years, and I have known that since about the tenth of January, and I have had a fish stall to run.* Then the arithmetic, and it is not the arithmetic of the first week of February: **you have not asked anybody, and it does not matter, because you have written the sentence down. The asking has already happened. You did it in advance, in a kitchen, in your own hand, under a heading.** And the cost, which is not a form: **he breaks his own written undertaking in the same yard on which he swore on the sixth of February that he was not going to say that in a room again for about a year, says so out loud, and asks for it to be written down; nobody wrote it down, which is the eighth time in nine weeks.** And the resolution, which is the first of his that leaves a district without him: **he puts it face up on a trestle table in the rain, squared, takes his hand off it, and does not take it, does not burn it, and does not give it to anybody** — *giving it to somebody is a favour and I have had two of those this winter and one of them was a page in a tin.* At about ten past four a man of about twenty-six puts a line on the second side of the sheet for the fifteenth of March, in his own hand, with his initials, and writes nothing else. Field Book: **the hundred and twelfth day**, the pair in front of it a **four**.

### Chapter 95 — He Is Not There On A Wednesday

**Two wrong shapes and a correction, and the correction is not his.** *One.* On Monday the sixteenth a gang of eleven and a foreman try to report a cast-iron cover that has been rocking since the second of February, and **the report cannot be made, and the reason is correct, and it is nine words from a works clerk of forty-six at his own desk**: a permanent way defect report has five boxes — *location, chainage, description, risk to passengers, action taken* — **and there is no box for a length, a section's reckoning is not a chainage, and the risk box wants a nil and a nil is a lie about the twenty-second of December, and the box wants a place it can send a man to and a man with a barrow on a Tuesday cannot find six hundred yards of track that the diagram took off in October and was right to take off.** He writes on the back of the form, in his own hand, that the box is left empty and why, and puts it in the correspondence tray, and says he has done that before in January and has noticed himself doing it again. *Two.* **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, six words, on a wall at ten to six on Monday the sixteenth: *A habit is not a calendar.*** *Three.* And on **Thursday the nineteenth at about ten past six in the morning a man of about forty put a broom down and said nine words: *He is not here on a Wednesday, and he has not been here on a Wednesday since before the war, because there is a stall at Cardew Docks that opens at six and that is the day it is busy.*** And the finding: **the second side has been blank on every Wednesday since the second of February, and a blank on a day-side is not a fault, and a thing that is not wrong cannot be struck out, and there is no column on that piece of paper for a man who is somewhere else** — a day with no line on it is a day when nobody stood in the weather, and a man of about sixty has been standing in the weather eleven minutes away every Wednesday since before the war, **and the sheet has been right, in its own terms, seven weeks running.** The correction is **twenty-eight words under the rule, in the sweeper's hand, undated and uninitialled**, and the reason is the first side of the same sheet. The fishmonger refuses to add a paragraph to her own rule: *the moment there is a second paragraph there is an instruction in it, and a person can say no to an instruction, and nobody can say no to a line.* The coffee man is not asked and is not told and does not know a piece of paper exists. And a **sound, the fourth instance**: a tram gong at the end of Cardew Street, eleven seconds, four seconds in, and he could not tell what it was and needed it, because a Line 3 car comes down that street at about that time and the coffee man walks against the traffic, and he wanted to know whether a car was coming so that he could say so. He did not get it, and the man was not in the road. Field Book: **the hundred and fifteenth day**, the pair in front of it a **three**.

### Chapter 96 — Nine Doors

**The recovery, and it is somebody else's work, and the person is twenty-three.** **Ola Petrossian has never been past the tram steps and on Sunday the twenty-second she walked four hours in eleven streets in her own time and came back with nine doors and no names**, and the reason is eleven words in a yard: *Then what is a water clinic for on a Tuesday morning, and who is standing in the kitchen?* The rule she set on the doorstep is four words — **doors and not people** — and the reason is that she has been taking the sheets since the middle of January and *a sentence about a person is a record of a person, and a door is not a person.* Nine doors, eight of them houses, **and the ninth a side door on a store that is shut, because a store is not a dwelling and nobody in this district has ever counted a store.** Milo Rook objects in eleven seconds — *a list of doors is the first step onto a rate book* — **and is refused by the person the objection is about, and is right, and writes that down: *being right is not the same as being the person who decides.*** And at the counter **Hettie Brann makes the second piece of paper of her life on purpose, says so out loud in front of about eleven people, promises not to make a third without saying so, and says the sentence the volume is about: *I want it to be wrong-shaped. The moment a thing can be used it is in use.*** Both go face down under the counter, on top of the January one, and neither goes in the machine. Field Book: **the hundred and nineteenth day**, the pair in front of it the **second four**.

### Chapter 97 — Line Six

**The cost that is not curable, paid in a room, over a fitter's shop.** A form comes back down to the quay section office: **FORM 4 — QUERY ON ANNUAL RETURN**, six boxes, the fourth of them **DOES THE SECTION STAND BEHIND THE ENTRY?**, and the query is on **line six** — the fifty-four words a works clerk of forty-six wrote on the fourteenth of January and a foreman of fifty-four signed without reading out. **And the query does not ask what he meant.** It asks whether the section stands behind him. The cost in about eleven minutes: the answer must be signed by the officer of the section, the officer is the foreman, the only honest answer is yes, the only answer that keeps eleven men out of a room in April is no, **and there is no column on that form for what a sentence meant** — so he leaves the box empty, rules a line through it, dates it, **and does not tell the foreman that the line was his, because the foreman would have said yes in about four seconds.** Four seconds, and the clerk says **no** when he is told *read it to me*, and that is the whole of what happened in that office. And the other half, which is worse: **nobody has ever asked about line five** — a nil on *persons observed on the length during the year* has now been read by a man with a pencil in a corridor with nine returns and has not been queried, **and that is the second time in six months this district has been protected by a clerk being fast.** And in a yard, the shape: *I have got one. It is in a tin on a bottom shelf in a plan room in my own office… the only thing that keeps it in that tin is a rule in grease pencil about not taking a line off, and I did not tell the shop that rule existed* — Rue says *that is the same shape*, and he stops after about four seconds, and asks for it to be read back to him in April. Field Book: **the hundred and twenty-third day**, the pair in front of it a **four**, and the third of the four fours in March.

### Chapter 98 — The Fifteenth Of April
