**The answer, said by working people and not one of them is a surveyor.** On Monday the thirtieth a woman of sixty-one asks a man of about fifty in a room with a machine in it a **returns question** — *what do you print in the second week of May?* — and the extraordinary question comes out of the ordinary one, and he puts his pen down for about four minutes, and the answer is four words: **there is nothing.** *I have been in this room nineteen years and I have seen the one that came in December and I have never seen another and I have never asked for one and I have never known anybody who has, and I am telling you that as a fact about a room and not as a finding, because I have never once in nineteen years been in a position to know whether a thing does not exist or whether it exists and has not been sent.* **There is no seasonal outlook for the season after this one, and nothing whatever is printed in this city for the fifteenth of April.** Her answer to *what do you want to happen on the fifteenth*, which took nine days to find: **Nothing** — *a district that has spent the winter being careful not to write anything down is going to be standing in the weather on the fifteenth of April with no reason to think anything is different, and about nine people in eleven streets will be the only people in this city who do that, and they will do it out of a guess and for nothing.* And what her job has been for nineteen years and has never been asked about, in about thirty seconds: **I read across. Two departments, two documents, and I am the one who notices when they disagree… and I have never once been told that any of it was a season, and I found out this year because there is a piece of paper with a date on it, and I have had forty-one of them.** And Rue's refusal, unasked for: *you have just said a good thing and it is going to be used… you have been saying nothing in a yard for four months and you have just stopped.* Field Book: **the hundred and twenty-seventh day**, the pair in front of it the **fourth four**, and the whole of the list of pairs written out with the figure at the end of it.

### Chapter 99 — A List Is Not A Hearing

**The fortnight, and a document nobody expected.** A single printed side comes to the district by hand, in an envelope, because the district is a party: **HALL OF WARDS — DEPARTMENTAL LIST DAY, BUSINESS OF TUESDAY THE FOURTEENTH OF APRIL**, six items, and the fourth line of it says *it is not a notice of hearing and it confers no right and no obligation and nothing in it may be produced in any proceeding except the proceeding on the item to which it relates.* **Nobody in this district had ever seen one.** Item one is the hearing and the reporting officer's name is on it — **I. Sarratt, Survey Enforcement** — and item two is the three matters, **and item six says *presented*, in a line about forms, about six hundred yards of reserved tram route**, four lines away from a hearing about a sentence nobody in this city can produce, and neither of them is going to find out about the other on the fourteenth. **Item four is a line about outstanding returns, and Mara Quill says, unasked, that she cannot tell which sections are on it and is not going to find out** — and he does not ask her anything, and writes down that the not-asking has stopped being procedure and become something else, and that **he did not tell her about the season and that not telling her was not the correct procedure and was not a courtesy, and that if he goes on not telling her after the fourteenth then it is not a rule.** And the sentence the chapter turns on, in about eleven seconds: ***this sheet is the only piece of paper in five months that has this district's name on it, and it has our name on it in the first line, and it has asked us for nothing, and it has given us a chair. A hearing is a thing somebody asks questions in. A list is a thing nobody asks anything in, and a list goes on a wall and gets read by about nine people and then goes in a file for eleven years.*** And two voices in a yard: **a document in the hand of a party is a thing that can be produced against the party**, and every line on the sheet is correct and the one that is wrong is item four and nobody is going to touch it; and **a list is a thing you read and a minute is a thing you are in** — *the only thing this district has won in five months is on that sheet as a number, and if a man in an office reads that sheet in April and says *and what is item one about*, then somebody has to be in a room on the fourteenth and say it out loud, and that is not a document and it cannot be made into one.* The second tin held **fifteen weeks of gang carbon and one page that is not a gang sheet** on the thirty-first. Field Book: **the hundred and twenty-eighth day**, one day after the last, and a silence is not a pair.

### Chapter 100 — The Crew That Did Not Work

**The number that did not change and the fact that did, and the standing question.** At the counter on Wednesday the first of April, the third and last comparison before the fourteenth, and **the odd one out is a different piece of paper and the number on the outside of the envelope is exactly the same: *1 of 4. — 1.iv.*** *In February the odd one out was the copy in a shed… today it is a different piece of paper in a different building and I could not tell you which, and the number is exactly the same, and if anybody in four years reads *1 of 4* on this corner they are going to think they know which month it was written in, and they will be wrong.* And she will not write *and it is a different one*, because **a number is the one thing about four people that she is allowed to have.** And the run number advances one step and stops: **a depot clerk of fifty-one, asked by nobody, looks it up on a Wednesday because a person who is going to do a thing nobody asked her to do should do it on a day when she has a half hour**, and finds that **the same crew's number is entered in the working timetable for the week commencing Sunday the twenty-first of December and is not worked on any day of it** — *there is no column on that page for a week in which a crew does not work, because a crew that does not work does not happen, and the row of a thing that did not happen is the same blank as the row of a thing that did.* He does not write it down, leaves a card under a brick, and is told not to come back. And at about half past four **a woman of sixty-one who is not M.Q. and is not M.R. and will not be writes nine lines of his own route home down on a counter in front of nine people, unasked, and refuses to put it in the envelope** — *the envelope is four copies with three hands and a disagreement that started in January over a gate, and this is a different thing and it does not go in there.* **It is there on the eighth, and on the twelfth, and on the fourteenth, and it is the fourth mark in a fourth hand, and it is not a cure and it is not an absence of one — it is a line somebody else can walk him home by.** Then **twelve lines of what is on the schedule** — the gang and the racks and the tins, **the second tin at sixteen weeks plus the one page on Friday the third of April**; the return with its nil and its sixth line and its query; the two working days on nobody's list; the defect form with no box for a length; **Wednesdays**; the first working day of the month, done three times, with no fourth; **a Sunday in eleven streets**; the district's prices carried and none moved, **including one declared reference to a woman of sixty-eight on nothing, the sixth across six chapters of this volume**; the page in the tin; the card under a brick; the second side and about nine blanks; and the nine lines in a satchel. Then the standing question, unchanged and unanswered: **eleven events, two gone, nine remaining, seven unallocated, a row of districts a hundred and forty years old printing a zero against eleven square miles of fen nobody has been to, nothing whatever printed for the fifteenth of April, and a hearing and a season ending on the same day and neither office told the other.** Field Book: **the hundred and twenty-ninth day**, one day after the last, and the closing entry: **twenty pairs, of which seven are threes and none of the original four is one of the other three, and four are fours all in March; the one-day interval from the tenth of December still looked at and still not written; ten routes, ten; the token cold on the hundred and fifteenth day; the tally nine; and the writer of the map is not the maintainer of anything.**

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0005 (Chapters 101–110)

**The resumed hearing, the eleven days, and the fortnight after.** Saturday the fourth of April to Saturday the twenty-fifth, twenty-two days, and it includes Tuesday the fourteenth. The volume's immediate promise was answered in Batch 0001 and the January hearing in Batch 0002; **this batch does not resolve the volume, does not discharge the order, does not restore the public name, does not move or re-author the adjournment, does not restore the removed sentence, and does not write the volume's final image.** **Milo Rook does not cross**, and the tally stays at nine lines, and the remembered-route list stays at ten. Atlas residue was used **once in ten chapters, in Chapter 108, in seven words.**

### Chapter 101 — Eleven Days

**Eleven days, said out loud in a yard on a Saturday, and nobody used it, and the reason is Rue's: *a number that everybody can say out loud is a number that nobody is responsible for*, and there is not one instrument in this district that will hold it.** On Sunday the fifth a nurse of twenty-three walked eleven streets for the second time, unpaid, in her own time, and got nine doors again, **the ninth still the side door on a store that is shut, because a store is not a dwelling and nobody in this district has ever counted a store**, and said in advance that if she writes it a third time somebody is to stop her, and nobody has been told she is going to. Hettie Brann read nine lines, gave it back, and said **a thing somebody else made is not mine to keep.** On Monday the sixth at half past seven a foreman of fifty-four read an **empty condition column** on the gang's sheet, wrote the three words about a cover, took them out with the flat of his thumb, and squared the sheet — **the cover is flush, nobody reset it, nobody packed it, and the last time he had his hands on it was the twelfth of February and it was two inches proud then**, and his reason is *a foreman of thirty-one years walked past a thing that had stopped happening for four weeks and did not notice, and that is the difference between a hazard and a place.* **There is no column on that sheet that wants to know why a column is empty.** At ten past ten the same Monday, at a fence at the low end of Bridge Street, **a settlement plate with a name and a date on it is face down in the grass on the far side**, found in passing by a woman of sixty-three with a barrow, and her sentence is *then nobody ever knows it was off, which is what a plate is for.* **All four doors are closed and each is correct:** the drainage book has no line for a notice's condition, the works own it and their answer is November, a district with no address cannot write to a company, and a surveyor would make it into a document with a reference on it and in four years there would be two. **And nobody can say whether it was one of the eleven, and the four in the river core is still four, and the sentence of the block is: a forecast does not come down because a thing happened, and it took a plate in a field and about two minutes to learn it.** Wednesday the eighth: **a blank on the second side again, and the twenty-eight words still under the rule undated and uninitialled, and a man of about sixty at a shed at Cardew Docks who does not know a piece of paper exists**, and the written line home the hundred and thirty-sixth day, and there. Field Book: the **fifth** (a six) and the **tenth** (a five); **the list reads twenty-two on that date**, two sixes, seven threes, one nine, one two, three fives, one eight, one twenty-five, two sevens, four fours.

### Chapter 102 — A Continuation Of An Adjourned Matter

**The Hall of Wards, Tuesday the fourteenth of April, from eleven, about thirty people, and a register on a table by the door with a name in it in ink.** A corridor at ten past ten first: **Mara Quill asks whether he is going to speak, and he says no, and gives the reason in about eleven seconds — *in January the district had nothing to say that a minute could hold… there is one thing in that room this morning that is not a document, and I am the only person in it who can say the shape of the thing that is not there*** — and she gives him three conditions and says *I would be able to* when he says she would not. **Sarratt reads the list of the business of the day aloud, because it is not a notice of hearing and it does not tell you what is being heard, and says: *This is a continuation of an adjourned matter.*** The three matters are not re-argued. The grounds: not in dispute as to validity, not determined, and the department contends neither way. **The classification of the eleventh: withdrawn, not to be relied on by any party, the withdrawal stands, and the department's own four-month-old finding is read out in full — *there is now in this city an instrument capable of placing a person upon a reach… and that instrument did not exist on the eleventh.*** The removed sentence: **not determined, the department cannot account for the removal and has never been able to, the reference is not a district reference and is not held by the department, and the sentence cannot be restored by this department.** **Milo Rook stands up for about two minutes and says the volume's structural statement out loud for the first time** — that the district cannot show an effect upon a person not because nothing happened to it but because a name in this city's records is entered against a rateable thing and there are three places inside the boundary that are not rateable things, and all three are held by people the department cannot name, pay, write to, or ask without producing a document — and the chair says it does not stand as stated, and he says *I have said it badly, and the advocate will do it again properly, and I have said it out loud in a room, which is what a thing that cannot be made into a document is for.* The line of January is read out and **Sarratt says it is conditional and the condition is not his.** And at half past three: **he has no further date to give, it is not a decision, a department does not set a date for a matter it cannot hear, and he asks for the distinction between *stood over* and *adjourned* to be minuted, and the minute will not distinguish them, and he has asked anyway.** Cass Ardery, in a corridor, nine words: *You asked for a date. There is not one. I am sorry.* Field Book: the **fourteenth**, four days, the fifth of the fours; and **the sentence that is the batch's relationship beat, at the top of the entry — *I did not tell her about the season… I have not told her. So it is not a rule.***

### Chapter 103 — Word For Word, Or Not At All

**The cost that is not curable, paid in a room, in about four seconds, by the woman who drafted it.** On Wednesday the eighth Lena Marr went up one flight to the woman of sixty on the second landing, unaccompanied, and **could not repeat the twenty-three words word for word, and the condition she had set was word for word or not at all, and she did not do it, and came down and stood in a doorway about four minutes** — *she has got about ten words of that conversation and I have got about ninety and that is how it is going to stay, and neither of us can put the other one's share back together.* On the fourteenth, at about half past one, **Sarratt says he is going to ask the party one question and would rather ask it now, because there is no column in this proceeding in which to ask it nicely, and asks whether the district stands the answer of the fourth of December; Lena Marr says yes; he asks her to say it again into the record for his own sake; she says it again.** Then she says the rest of it, in the record, and it is the whole of the chapter: **either the line goes this morning and two hundred and fifty-two people go into one schedule a man with a pencil and nine returns fills in about four minutes, or it stands and one woman of sixty on a second landing, the ninth of twenty-six names in a file in Crown Heights, cannot see it, cannot come off it, and cannot ask whether her name is spelled right** — and **I drafted the prohibition that is stopping her. It is forbidden by us. We asked for it. I wrote the paragraph, and I would like that minuted, and I know exactly what the minute is going to do with it: which is that it is going to print that I asked. And that is all it is going to do, and I am asking anyway, because there is no other instrument in this city.** Then the **minute in six paragraphs and about four hundred words**, every word of it correct, and the whole of what six months bought is paragraph four, and **paragraph five says the matter stands over, is not adjourned, and has no date.** Milo Rook said nothing and has written down that he said nothing, and gave his reason in about nine words: *if I had stood up at that point and said anything at all I would have been the man who made a minute out of it.* **There is no Field Book entry for the fourteenth afternoon, and the reason is on the page: the second half of the fourteenth is in the book as four seconds and a paragraph and a woman who could not say twenty-three words, and I could not improve any of it.**

### Chapter 104 — Thirty-Four Steps

**The fifteenth of April, and what the district decided to do about it, and the working day in the same chapter.** On Wednesday the fifteenth a round went out at ten past eight and in at about seven, four hours longer than it needed, unpaid, **and about nine people stood in the weather between five and seven for no reason at all, out of a guess, and they were the only people in this city who did that, exactly as Hettie Brann said in a yard on the thirtieth of March she expected.** **Milo Rook did not go and was not asked, and the reason is nine words — *I was the only one who would have had a reason* — and Rue's answer is that if he had stood in it then on the Wednesday after that he would have had a habit, and by the middle of May a column, and *you have been careful not to write anything down for six months and you nearly ruined it in one evening by being right about something.*** The second side of the sheet has a blank on the fifteenth and **the sheet is right**. On Thursday the sixteenth, **thirty-four steps down into a room beneath Crown Heights with nine desks on the left, a plan chest, and a window that does not open and a chair under it, a working day with Bettina Sallow, fifty-four, four years at the ninth desk on the left** — the ordinary thing she does four times a day, unthanked: **she will not tell you a document does not exist unless she has read the whole card, and she will tell you what she looked at, how far she went and what she did not open.** And the volume's structural thesis in a reader's mouth: **every card in this room is a place… there is no card in it for a drain, a cover, a length, a row of shops, a fence, a woman, a market, or a hole… the index is not a record of what happened, it is a record of what is where** — **and a card in an index is a claim and not the document, which is the same sentence the index clerk of that same room said to him in December in other words, standing up, to nine people, with her in the room, and the word *claim* is in both of them, and the two of them have never said a word to the other one, and he has heard both halves and has not joined them and did not tell her he had heard the other half in front of her, and she did not ask, and that was the only correct thing either of them did that morning.** And a broom, in her hand since the twelfth of March, unused: **nobody sweeps this room… the day I sweep under that chest I am the person who sweeps this room, and then somebody in about four years can ask me what I did in here, and I will have to say why, and there is no why in it.** And on Thursday the ninth, **the FORM 4 query came back a second time and has stopped asking about the sentence and started asking about the box: *Return received. Item 4 not completed. Section to complete.*** It goes up empty with a second ruled line and a second date, **and nobody has ever been asked about line five, and the nil on persons observed has now survived being read, a query on the line above it, a second query, and a defect report that could not be made.** Field Book: the **sixteenth**, two days.

### Chapter 105 — A Column That Wants To Know

**The failure, the fifth time, the same one, and the district knew the shape of it before he had finished.** On Saturday the eighteenth, at a kitchen table, a form headed **CO/6/BELL — MATTERS NOT DETERMINED**, six columns, and a seventh headed **WHY THIS MATTER WAS NOT DETERMINED.** He filled in two lines and filled them in well, and both reasons are true and neither is a reason anybody can do anything with. **Cyril Sale said the shape of it before he had got to the bottom of the page — *you have got a seventh one and I know what it says… I have watched four men in this city fill one in, and every single one of them did it in a kitchen, and you are the fifth*** — and then the half that is the finding: **a column that wants to know why wants a person to put their name against the why… there is no name on your page, and you are about to write one, and in about four years the name on that page is the name of a woman of about thirty-one who drafted the paragraph that caused the third matter, and you will have made her the officer responsible for a sentence nobody can produce.** Nobody wrote it down; that was the tenth time in thirteen weeks. **And he did not tear it up, did not put it in his coat, and did not leave it on a trestle table in the rain — because doing that a second time would not be a method, it would be a habit.** The resolution is new and it is not a resolution: **he did not finish it.** A heading in the seventh column, a line under words, two of six columns filled, the first of the two headings written and the second not. **A half-filled box in this authority is not an exception and generates nothing; it is the exact opposite of an exception, because an exception has to be cleared and a half-filled box does not** — and the reason out loud, which is not modesty: **there is no row on this piece of paper for me, and I have been putting myself in a row five times.** Hettie Brann reads nine words of it upside down, tells him she is not taking it to the works, and announces that she will say out loud on Wednesday that it is the third thing under her counter. Field Book: the **twentieth**, four days, the sixth of the fours; and the undertaking from the second of February — *I am not going to be the man who says that in a room again for about a year* — is **seventy-seven days old, and the not-saying is now a practice and not an undertaking**, and nobody wrote that down either.

### Chapter 106 — Nobody Else

**The changed method and the recovery, and they are the same person, and she is a counter, and the method is a line she wrote in January about four pieces of paper in four buildings and he had never read it as an instruction.** The rule at the foot of the envelope: *if one of them is not the same, **ask the person whose hand it is and nobody else.*** **He read it in a corridor in March, took the first half, and left the second, because the second half is about four people he does not know the names of. On Tuesday the twenty-first at a counter she says: *It is not about four people. It is about a hand.*** And the method, in his own book, as the first line of the entry: **custody is a thing a person holds. Not a tin. Not a coat. Not a table in the rain. Not an office. Not a drawer in his own office that a man of thirty-one signed for in December and has never opened. Not under a brick. A drawer under a counter is wrong-shaped and cannot be chased, cannot be produced, and cannot be given a reference, because a counter is not a repository.** **She puts the half-filled form under the counter face down on top of two others, says the count out loud, says that if he is in the yard on Wednesday she will hand it back to him in front of everybody, and tells him she is not going to explain why — because the moment a surveyor of about thirty-one is standing in a yard when a woman of sixty-one puts a third piece of paper under a counter, that piece of paper is now about a surveyor, and it will be in a yard in about four years, and somebody will ask a question about it that is a question about him.** He does not go, and the reason is that he understood the rule for the first time at four minutes past two that afternoon, and it is a thing he has had in his own book since the seventh of January in the imperative and has never read as an instruction. And she says the other half of it, about a brass token in the inside pocket of his coat, and **he has not written down what he did about that and wants it in the book as a fact and not as a resolution.** Field Book: the **twenty-first**, **one day, and he is not pairing it, and the difference between an entry and a pair is what he has finally written down.**

### Chapter 107 — Nobody Keeps This Row

**The answer to the volume, in the mouth of a person who has been doing it and not writing it down, on a day the district's own map cannot see.** No coffee on Saltmarket Row at ten past six on Wednesday the twenty-second, and there never has been on a Wednesday, **and about nine people were standing on it anyway and none of them had told anybody they were going to and none of them had been asked.** A woman of about forty-five called the lot prices from her own doorway with no fish behind her and put her thumb in a crack in the wall; a man of about forty swept the row badly. **A man of about twenty-six came up on a Wednesday, in his own time, and wrote a line on the second side with the date and his initials and nothing else, and nobody asked him why he came on a Wednesday.** And the finding Milo Rook states: **he has read the twenty-eight words the man with the broom put under the rule in March, and he has not initialled a correction, and he has not asked the man with the broom about it — because the second side is a day and he was not on the day, and there is no column on that piece of paper for a man who is somewhere else, and the correction is under the rule where anybody can put a thing and nobody can be told to do anything about it.** Hettie Brann says **the count out loud at half past six in front of about nine people, on a Wednesday, in the open, because there is no coffee and about nine of them are there anyway.** And then the fishmonger, to a doorway, in about nine seconds: **Nobody keeps this row. That is what it is. If somebody kept it then there would be a book, and in the book a column, and in the column my name, and the other four of us would be a line under mine, and then in about four years somebody could ask me what I did, and I would have to say why, and there is no why in it. There has never been a why in it. I do it because I do it and I have never once in nineteen years thought about doing it.** And the second half of it: **it is kept by nine people doing it and not writing it down, and you cannot have a book of that, because the moment you have a book of it somebody is keeping it, and then it is not nine people, it is one person with a book and nine people who have been asked.** And: *I have said it is not for anybody five times… it is not for anybody and that is not modesty, it is the arrangement.* **She has not given her name to anybody, has not been thanked, and the coffee man has not been told any of it and is not going to be.** Field Book: the **twenty-second**, one day, and not paired, and **the answer of six months arriving out loud in a doorway on the last day of a season nobody could name.**

### Chapter 108 — The Fifth Of May

**The escalation nobody expected: the same envelope, the same hand, the same door, and the matter is not on it.** On Thursday the twenty-third a printed side came by hand because the district is a party: **HALL OF WARDS — DEPARTMENTAL LIST DAY, BUSINESS OF TUESDAY THE FIFTH OF MAY**, six items, the same fourth line. **The matter is not on it. Item one is *Continuations*. The order number is not on it, CO/6 is not on it, the reporting officer's name is not on it, there is no hearing and no sentence on it. The district's name is in the first line.** And the ground-and-seam-returns line is the same line as on the first list, and **this district still cannot tell which sections are on it, and that has not changed, and nobody has asked.** The advocate, in eleven seconds: **a matter is adjourned by a chair in a room on a minute, and a thing does not lose its date by being left off a list — it loses its date by being discharged, withdrawn or varied, and none of those three words is in this district's name anywhere** — and **the only instrument in this city that would have put the district in front of anybody again is a list, and it goes on a board in a corridor that about four hundred people walk past every day, and on Tuesday the fourteenth nobody in that building could have found us and did not need to, because we were on the list, and on the twenty-third we are not, and there is no procedure for a party to be told that it is not on a list, and the envelope is the only notice anybody gets and it came three days after the board changed.** **He stood in that corridor for about twenty minutes and nobody spoke to him and there is nothing in this city to stand in a corridor about.** Doreen Sankey in a yard on the Friday, saying the standing sentence for the fourth time and then giving it a second half: **in March it was about a list giving us a chair; in April the same sentence is about a list taking the chair away, and nobody has sent for the chair, and nobody has taken it, and there is no form on which a chair goes back… it was never ours, it was a number in a column in a hearing, and a number in a column can go down as well as up and nobody has to do anything for it to go down.** And: **I want it recorded, in about four years, by somebody who was not here, that a list gave this district a chair on the thirty-first of March and took it away on the twenty-third of April, and that nobody did anything, and that the reason nobody did anything is that there is no form for it.** Also in the chapter: the allocation sheet for the week commencing the twentieth came off the machine on Friday the seventeenth, **the four is still four, the zero against Mireward is still zero, there is not one line in the event column in any of the five rows, and the second column is a forecast and a forecast does not come down because a thing happened.** **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, in seven words, on a wall about forty feet from a hearing room: *It is dated before it is read.*** Field Book: the **twenty-third**, two days.

### Chapter 109 — Eleven

**The honest arithmetic of a season nobody could name the day of, and the four things that have happened in this district and cannot be shown to anybody.** Thursday the twenty-third at half past seven, the gang on the back length, **the condition column empty and it has not had anything in it since the twelfth of February, six walks in April with an empty condition column, and about nine hundred blanks in six weeks in a building in Crown Heights are the only other record of it and the nine hundred blanks are the machine's dash and not a reason.** The sheet into the rack on Friday the twenty-fourth and **the second tin now holds nineteen weeks of gang carbon and one page that is not a gang sheet, and the nineteen is the last figure he will write in that book about it.** Then the arithmetic, in four lines: **eleven were forecast for a season that ended on Tuesday the fourteenth; either eleven happened or eleven did not and this city cannot tell you which; there is nothing printed in Alderquay for the fifteenth; there has never been a second outlook and a man of fifty-one in a room with a machine in it has been in that room nineteen years, has seen one, has never known anybody who has seen a second, and cannot tell whether the second one does not exist or whether it exists and has not been sent.** Two of the four in the river core have happened, in the only sense this city can put a number to; **a third thing happened at a fence on the sixth of April and nobody has said it was one of the eleven and there is no form on which to say it.** A works clerk, counting alone and refusing to say the number out loud: **if I say three out loud in a room then in about four years there is a form with three lines on it, and a form with three lines on it wants a fourth.** And the four things, named out loud in a yard in about forty seconds: **a rectangle of standing water on the twenty-ninth of January; a hundred and ten yards of frontage in the rain on the nineteenth of February; a plate in a field on the sixth of April; and about nine people standing about in eleven streets on the fifteenth of April for nothing at all** — and Rue says *a plate*, and he says **I am not going to say anything else about the plate, because in about four years somebody is going to ask what this district did in April, and there is no form, and if I put a why next to any of them then in four years there will be a document with four lines on it, and a document with four lines on it wants a fifth, and a fifth is a schedule.** Then Mireward: **eleven square miles, a typed zero, three nils in twenty weeks, not going to get a fourth, a man of fifty-eight with a rod and a chain nobody has ever asked for a pad from, nobody has gone there this winter and nobody is going to, and a settlement plate behind a fence that he has not gone on the far side of either** — *and those two facts have the same shape and I have put them next to each other once this afternoon and I am not going to do it again.* **No Field Book entry for the twenty-fourth: one day, and not a pair, and the reason is that a man of forty-one has told this district four times that a list is a thing you read and a minute is a thing you are in, and a woman of sixty-one has told it that a number which cannot get better is the only kind of number worth having, and both of those sentences are the same sentence, and the same sentence is about a week, and a week is not a calendar.**

### Chapter 110 — What Is On The Schedule Now

**The run number advanced one step, and the step is a column, and it is not the volume's image.** On Friday the twenty-fourth, in a room above a canteen on Parcel Street, **a man of forty-one on no list at all asked a question nobody sent him, because about four times a day somebody in this district looks at a job and decides it is not theirs**, and a clerk in the timetable division with nineteen years in it and nine items in front of him and no first name said no, he had not been sent, and then said one thing: **there is a column for run number and there is a column for depot move and they have never been the same column; a number in the run column is a crew booked to a run and a number in the move column is a crew going to a gate; the occurrence in the run column is the 07:52 booked working off Crown Heights on the morning of Tuesday the sixteenth of December, which is the car that went round, and the other occurrence is a light engine and a two-car set going to a gate, and it is not it, and I am telling you which one so that you can stop** — and, in nineteen years, **he has never once seen a crew entered in the run column for a morning when the car did not go out, and if a car does not go out the column is empty and the row is not there, because a crew that does not work does not happen.** And the limit he set in advance: **ask nobody, and do not come here, and do not send the man from the round, and if you do any of those three things then I will say that the timetable division does not discuss working timetables outside the department, and it will be true, and it will be in a book, and it will keep.** **The card goes back under the brick with a third line on the back of it in a hand that is not Milo's and not Hester Coombe's, the line on the front was not taken off, and there are three things under that brick and there have been three since the twenty-first of January. He has not written down what the clerk told him.** **No destination is named, no passenger's name appears, there is no thirty-one in it, and nobody in that timetable came back.** Then **fourteen lines of what is on the schedule now**, in his hand, not the department's — the gang and the six empty walks; the January return and its two queries and its nil on line five; the two working days on nobody's list; the defect form with no box for a length; the allocation sheets on Fridays with the four and the zero and the line that is a forecast; **Wednesdays, and that there was no coffee on that row on the fifteenth of April and there were about nine people on it anyway**; the monthly comparison, done three times, the next on the third of May, and **1 of 4. — 1.iv.**; the second Sunday round; **the three pieces of paper under the counter, counted out loud in a yard**; the page in the tin; the three things under the brick; **the district's prices, none moved, and two declared references to a woman of sixty-eight on nothing which are the seventh and the eighth and which he counts and does not act on**; and the nine lines in a satchel. Then the standing question in three parts, and it is sharper than it was in January: **why does a region the Registry enters as stable appear on a forecast of weather-driven seam events at all; and why is there no second outlook and who would print one and is the absence a decision or an oversight; and what is it about an instrument that a person cannot produce which makes the thing it cannot produce the most important thing in a district** — *and it is not going to be answered in April, and it is going to be answered in about a year and a half, and I do not know how, and I have written that down so that I cannot pretend in a year that I do.* Field Book: the **twenty-fifth**, two days, and **the list reads twenty-seven — two sixes, seven threes, one nine, four twos, three fives, one eight, one twenty-five, two sevens, six fours** — and the closing sentence: **the writer of the map is not the maintainer of anything, and that is a list of about fourteen other people and not one of them has been asked to be on it.**

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0006 (Chapters 111–120)

**The penultimate block of the volume's last movement, and not the volume close.** Sunday the twenty-sixth of April to Thursday the twenty-first of May, twenty-six days, and it includes **Monday the fourth of May** and **Tuesday the fifth of May**. The hearing, the season's end and the machinery that is still printing are all answered and none of it is re-opened. **This batch does not resolve the volume, does not discharge the order, does not restore the public name, does not move or re-author the adjournment, does not restore the removed sentence, does not print a second seasonal outlook, does not add a fourth nil about Mireward, does not send anybody to Mireward, does not make a fifth sighting or a fifth refusal, does not move the run number at all, and does not write the volume's final image, which is in Chapter 130 and one batch away.** **Milo Rook crosses nothing**, the tally stays at nine lines, and the remembered-route list stays at ten. **This batch adds NO reference to a woman of sixty-eight on nothing**, so the count stands at eight across eight chapters of this volume. Atlas residue was used **once in ten chapters, in Chapter 120, in eight words.**

### Chapter 111 — A Monday Like Any Other

**The first walk after the season, and a column nobody reads.** Monday the twenty-seventh, 07.30, eleven men down six hundred yards, the weekly sheet carrying two dates, the twenty-seventh and the thirtieth, **the reason column empty on both and the condition column empty on both — the seventh walk in April with an empty condition column and the first one in which the foreman did not look at it.** Roy Pryce, four sentences and a correction: *the last walk anything went into that column was the twelfth of February and I have not read it once since, and I did not not-read it. **Reading it and not reading it are two different things and the difference is about a second and a half and the second and a half is not a decision anybody makes.*** And then: somebody will fill it in one day on a Tuesday in a wet week and it will be wrong, and **there is no column on this sheet that wants to know why a column is empty and I am not going to build one, and I have not built one in eleven weeks and there is no rule.** The round out at ten past eight and in at about four. **The second side now has three blank Wednesdays nobody can correct — the eighth, the fifteenth and the twenty-second of April — and a fourth is coming on the twenty-ninth, and a blank is a day when nobody stood in the weather, and the sheet is right, and on all three about nine people stood in the weather for nothing at all.** The twenty-eight words under a rule undated and uninitialled, read by the man they are about and not acted on, and the man of about sixty they concern was at a shed at Cardew Docks on all three and will be on the twenty-ninth and does not know a piece of paper exists. In a yard at a quarter to ten: **it is the first Monday since the second of January that this district has treated as an ordinary Monday, and the reason is not that nothing happened on it — the reason is that the machinery is still printing and the chair is not**, and **a season ending is not an event; a season ending is a column that stops being printed**, and **a district that cannot produce a document is a district that cannot be instructed, and a district that cannot be instructed has to be in the street in the morning**, and nobody wrote it down — the thirteenth time in fourteen weeks. Field Book: the **twenty-sixth** and the **twenty-seventh**, each one day off the last, and **not one of them a pair**.

### Chapter 112 — Nineteen Calls

**A working day with a person in it, done well by somebody who is not the protagonist, and she is not a symbol and says so on the ramp.** **Nessa Bray, Line 3 conductor, thirty-eight, sixteen years on the run**, out of Crown Heights at seven minutes to eight, **nineteen calls out and nineteen back**, *mind the step, it is the second one.* **I am not a symbol and I am not a survivor and I am not going to be called that. I am a conductor on line three… and there are about nine hundred people a day who get on my car and go somewhere because of a thing I say out loud in a particular order, and that is the whole of what I am and it is a job.** Three asks she made in December — the shed shut, the carbon in the drawer, the names not read in a yard in the rain — all three got, in nine seconds, and **a person who has thanked somebody is a person who has asked**, and she is not going to start. **And the punch roll, which is the object of the chapter: about nine inches of paper in a wooden case, a hole through it for every person that gets on, off a machine at the end of a run and into a tray — the only thing in this city that knows how many people were on that car and it does not know who one of them is, and that is not a defect, and she said so before he could and he would have got it wrong.** And the half of the volume's question she answers by accident: **the instrument a person cannot produce is the thing a person produces about nine hundred times a day, and nobody in this city has ever asked her to make it because they already have it.** The twentieth call is a siding: **there is no form in this city for a place a car goes to be looked at.** And she can give him the doors in the order they come, in the dark, backwards, and cannot tell him why the seventh is before the eighth on a Tuesday and after it on a Friday, and has never needed to. He asked her two questions, neither of them the one anybody in this district would have asked; he did not ask her about the sixteenth of December, or the thirty-one, or the printed sheet; **and he wrote nothing down, and that is the only rule he has made in a book in a coat in seven months.** Field Book: the **twenty-eighth**.

### Chapter 113 — A Column Wants A Person

**The failure, his, the sixth time, the same one, and the person who names the shape is not a clerk and is the person the form is about.** Wednesday evening, a kitchen table, a cold cup of tea, accountancy paper headed in his own hand **OCCUPANCY — RESIDENTS, TEMPORARY ACCOMMODATION**, the six columns in the order of the minute, and a seventh headed **WHY THIS PERSON IS NOT ON THE DISTRICT'S SCHEDULE**. **He got one line and not two, and the one is the best thing he has written in seven months**: *9. Second landing. One resident, woman, sixty, present since the fifteenth of December past… **a department made her rateable by a schedule of persons that this district has refused to supply since the fourth of December, and the refusal is the district's and it is minuted, and the paragraph that stops her is the one the named rateable head drafted herself.*** About a hundred and ten words, all of it true, and **it has no name in it because he does not know her name, and neither does the file, and there is exactly one person in this city who knows it.** Thursday at half past eight, in a doorway: **Lena Marr, about thirty-four, not a clerk and never one, names the whole shape in nine seconds off one sentence — *you have written a seventh column* — and then the other half: *I have twenty-six names and I have not written them down, on purpose, for about four years, and you have spent seven months finding that out about a plate in a field and you have never once applied it to a piece of paper in your own coat.*** And the six resolutions, and the fifth of them went under somebody else's counter and the sixth is in his own hand. **And the finding of the block, in her mouth: a head is the only repository in this city that cannot be produced against anybody** — *you can send for a tin, you can send for a counter, you can send for a coat and a man in it; you cannot send for what I am thinking about at eleven o'clock at night, and that is not because it is hidden, it is because it was never written, and a thing that was never written is the only kind of thing in this city that nobody can take off me, including the twenty-six, including the woman on the second landing, including the one I drafted the paragraph for on the fourth of December past.* And the price, said once: **she has been on paper since the fifteenth of December and not in anybody's head since the day before that.** And the resolution she gives him: **fold it in at the back of your own book, put the date on the fold in your own hand, do not take it out again, and that is the last time anybody in this district is going to be able to say a thing to you about a piece of paper and be right, because I am not going to do it again either.** He did it at about nine that night. **The six resolutions in order: a tin, a coat, a yard, a table in the rain, a counter, and a book** — five places and one sentence said out loud, and the sentence is the worst of the six because it is not a place. Field Book: the **twenty-ninth** and the **thirtieth**.

### Chapter 114 — The Route Is The Doors

**The changed method and the recovery, both somebody else's work, and neither of them a word.** On at ten past seven and off at about twenty past six, **eleven hours, eleven words, all of them to a woman of thirty-eight on a different figure of three, and Corin Mabey said none.** He is fifty-one, twenty-nine years driving line three, he came back with the car on the twenty-second of December and **has not been able to say a sentence since the Friday and is at home when he is not driving and is not hurt.** Nessa's warning from the step: **do not try to make him talk… he is working, and if you go in there looking for a thing to write down you will find it and it will not be his**, and *getting on and saying nothing for eleven hours is a thing a man does when he is about to ask somebody something.* He sat on the off side of the second seat from the front, which is where a person sits who is not going to be asked to move, and the driver looked at him once at the bottom of the ramp and once at the top and on neither occasion appeared to see anything remarkable. **The car was not reading the sheet. The car was being driven, and the driver had it, and the sheet is a print of a man of fifty-one who has had it for twenty-nine years, and a print cannot go out and come back.** And the changed method, one sentence, belonging to two people who have never spoken and whom he is not going to introduce: **the route is the doors, and the sheet is a print of the man who has the route.** And the recovery, which is not a fact and not a document and cost nothing: **a man who cannot talk is the only person in this district I can sit next to for a whole working day without putting anybody in a column.** In the yard at ten past five, the car in at 16.41 and **a man of about sixty-three with a flag who has never looked inside a car in twenty-two years and was not asked to and is not going to**, and his book is right because the times are the times — *if I look inside a car then I know something about a car, and what I know about a car is that it came in… there is no box anywhere in this yard for what I know and I would rather be the man with the flag* — and **there was a day in December that my book has an hour in that has not got anything behind it, and I have not asked about it and I am not going to**, and nobody asked him, and that was the first time in five months that anybody in this district had a reason. And Hettie Brann at ten past one, refusing to name the thing: **you have spent a day not asking him, and six months not asking about four people whose names you do not know… and you have not once written down what it is, and I am not going to give it a name, because the moment I give it a name it is a thing in a book and I have got four returns and one counter.** Field Book: the **first**.

### Chapter 115 — A Boundary Needs Two Ends

**The boundary scene, and the plate stays in the grass and nobody goes over.** Saturday morning, a hundred and forty feet, **one end a corner in a street and the other end a fence**, walked on his own, because one of two pegs put back in on the twenty-eighth was standing in the road surface and the other was about eleven inches out in a gutter and neither was wrong. **A boundary is not a line. It is two men starting at two ends and walking towards each other, and the boundary is where they meet.** Two distances in his own book against the date and nothing else. At about eleven a works gang came on the fortnightly inspection, eleven of them, walked the frontage at the speed of eleven men who are not looking for anything in particular, **and stopped at the fence, because a gang stops at a fence**; a man of about twenty-six in a high-visibility jacket walked past a place in the grass on the far side and did not look at it and was not asked to; nobody said anything and there is no column on a gang sheet for a fence. **And so the two ends of a hundred and forty feet were at the same place for about four minutes for no reason at all, and a boundary needs two ends and does not need two surveyors and does not need anybody to know what a boundary is and certainly does not need a plate.** The plate: **twenty-six days, and the grass has closed over the place where it went down, and that is a fact about a field and not a document** — the third and last sentence about it. And Roy Pryce, unasked, about a flood: **the low end floods about twice in four years and the gutter is the only thing on that length that moves… a form cannot be wrong in a direction** — and then the nine words and the rest: **do not go over the fence, and not because I have told you not to. There is nobody on the far side to stop you and there has not been for six years, and that is exactly why you should not go, and I have not got a better reason than that and I am not going to look for one.** And the finding: **a boundary is a place where two people agreed to have stopped**, a peg is a point and a boundary is not a point, and **there is no column for a peg in a gutter and there is no column for a peg anywhere.** He got a pin, drove it in nine inches from the gutter peg, marked the road peg's true place, left the gutter peg, and wrote both numbers in his own book and nowhere else. Field Book: the **second**.

### Chapter 116 — A Question Put In A Box

**The escalation is a letter, and it is the first time the January line has ever been used for anything.** Friday the first of May, ten past eleven, on a tram authority's own paper: **CAR 214 — LINE THREE. RETURN OF SERVICE PARTICULARS**, four paragraphs. The return for the quarter is not complete at **item 6, the number of persons carried**; **the roll for that working is accounted for and is not in dispute, the roll carries the count, the roll does not carry the count in figures, and the working return calls for figures**; **the district is asked to state in writing the number of persons carried on the working of 07:52 off Crown Heights on Tuesday the sixteenth day of December last, and it is the only party to which this office has been able to address correspondence in respect of that working**; **a reply is requested by the twenty-first day of May, and if no reply is received the item will be returned as not stated and the quarter will close with the item standing open.** He put it face down on his own desk and went out to a fence at ten the next morning. **Item six, four lines deep, built in about 1961 for a conductor to fill in off a roll** — and the finding, and it is the first wrong-shaped form in the volume that is wrong in the direction of a **figure** and not a why: **this city already knows the number and has had it since the sixteenth of December, and what is missing is not a fact but a man with a pen putting a fact that already exists onto a line that somebody will read in about four years.** Monday at half past ten, a corridor in Crown Heights, **Mara Quill, thirty-one, nine words and then six minutes of not saying any more about it: nobody has to answer that, and nobody has to not answer it either, and that is the new part… in January the protection was ours… a letter about a car does not know we exist… this is the first time anybody has used it and I do not know yet whether it is the shield or the gag** — and then *I think it has always been both and that January was the first time it cost anything, and I have never said that to you before.* And then he told her he had not told her about the season, and she said **I am not going to ask you about it… we are two people keeping a number about each other and the number is the same and neither of us has said it out loud, and that is the most intimate thing that has happened between us and it is a document neither of us has mentioned. Do not improve on that in a yard.** And at ten past one in a yard, **Verrick Assal, fifty-eight, with a spade across his knees**, unshown the letter: *you have got a date on it… you are going to write something on about the nineteenth, and I have told you a thing about yourself and I would like you to notice that I got it from nine words on a piece of paper I have not read* — and why he is right: **a man who cannot answer rings the office and says it cannot be answered and writes three words, and the three words are a figure and not a lie, because the return asks for a number of persons carried and that is not a sentence and *not stated* is not the same as *no response*.** And in his own book on the Sunday, before the nineteenth: **I am going to let a rule about one column stop me answering a different one** — the first thing he has written in seven months that is a reason for a future act. Field Book: the **third**.

### Chapter 117 — One Man Short

**A refusal that is correct and a cost that is not curable, and neither is paid by Lena Marr nor by the woman of sixty.** At half past three, at a gate in a high wall, **Pell Harrow, seventeen, said no in about nine seconds and he was right:** *I will not take it… **the moment a courier is on a thing, the courier is the record. That is the whole of it and I have been a courier for two years.**… the boy cannot get out from under a piece of paper about a morning by being forty.* And: **I would say it, and then it would be true, and it would be the first true thing anybody has said about that morning since the Friday in December and it would be about me.** And the third of the three things in the standing rule: **do not send the man from the round… somebody wrote that rule for a reason and the reason is a boy with a satchel, and I am not going to be the reason a rule holds in a different direction in the year I am nineteen.** And the letter's real consequence at a gate in eleven seconds: **there are two reasons and I am not joining them** — twenty-two days up the south on his own money, unasked and going to stay unasked, **that is a refusal of mine, made by a man of forty-one in about four seconds in January, and it has held four months**; and the cost, and it is not curable: **a round is carried by a postman and a postman is on a form and the form wants a depot and a depot is an address, and a district with no rateable unit and no address cannot have a depot, and has not had one since the eleventh of October six years ago when a street name came off an index. So there is a boy of seventeen doing seven stops in eleven streets on a Monday and a Thursday and taking a handcart through a gate in a high wall, and there is nobody to send with him, and there will be nobody to send with him in the fourth year, and it cannot be cured from inside this district, and it is not an injustice and nobody has done anything wrong, and it is a design** — and every word of the standing order is a reason a boy of seventeen is doing two rounds on a cart. At ten past four, Cyril Sale in a doorway: **this is the first time a form has asked a district a question that a person has to be in to answer, and about eleven people in this district are in to answer every day and not one of them can be printed, and the form has got a box and the box is four lines deep and it is going to sit there open until the quarter is closed… and in about four years the item will be a line in a book in another building and the line will be the whole of what this district is on the record as having done in December.** And at ten past five **a man of forty-one with a wet cap looked at a job that was as nearly his as any job has ever been in this district and decided in about four seconds that it was not his: a card under a brick is a thing and not a job… and it is the only thing I am good at and I have had four months to get good at it and I have.** Field Book: the **fourth**.

### Chapter 118 — One Of Four

**The fourth and last comparison, and the business of Tuesday the fifth of May, from outside.** Monday the fourth, in the half hour after four when the counter is shut: four copies out of four places, version and date compared, nothing else read. **Three of the four say version one. One says version two. The fourth has been read and will not be described.** He was not in the room, and the four lines of the reason: **a comparison of four people is not his, and the holder is not M.Q. and is not M.R. and will not be, and the four are four people, and he is one of the four.** **The third of May is a Sunday; the first working day of the month is the fourth, which is a Monday** — and a note in a book in a coat that had said the third since January was wrong on paper for four months and is now right in the only place in this district where anything gets corrected, and he has told nobody and nobody asked. At a quarter past four, nine words: *It is the same… and the number has not moved, and I am not telling you which one, and you were not going to ask me.* **One of four. — 1.iv.**, in pencil, in her hand, the same number since the nineteenth of January and meaning a different piece of paper every month — **and the fact that changed this month is that she did not rewrite it. She has changed it every month since the nineteenth of January and on the fourth of May she did not, and the corner of that envelope is four weeks old and says the first of April on a piece of paper last handled on Monday**, and she is not going to say why, and she has said *I wanted to write something else next to it* three times and is not going to say it a fourth. And the second half of her own rule, out loud for the first time: **the second half is the whole of it, and the second half is the only part of it that is about this envelope, and I have watched you take the first half of it four times and leave the second half four times.** Tuesday, ten past four, a hall emptied into a corridor, **about four hundred people walk past a board every working day, and he stood about forty feet from a door and was spoken to by nobody for about twenty minutes, for the second time in three weeks**; the six items verbatim, the district's matter not on it and not able to be on it, and **the fifth of May did nothing to this district in about six hours, correctly, and it did it on a printed side served by hand to a door.** And the finding: **a list is read by the people who are on it and by the person who made it, and that is the entire readership of a list; a minute is read by nobody — not by the parties, not by the department, not by the chair — until whoever produces it, in about four years, on behalf of somebody who is not in the room. Those are two different readerships of two different objects about forty feet apart in the same building and they do not know about each other and they do not have to… and a district does not get from one to the other, it is in one or the other, and this district is in neither.** And: **the room is not the list and the list is not the room, and about four hundred people a day walk past the difference and nobody has ever made a form of it, and that is not an oversight, that is the design.** Field Book: the **fifth**.

### Chapter 119 — The Same Sentence Twice

**The fortnight's end, and the same sentence said by two women who have never met, and the condition column.** Wednesday the sixth, half past four, he counted the fortnight out loud in a yard **and got it wrong by one day, and corrected it, and about four people heard him do it and nobody said anything.** **Two instruments, one district, and no line drawn between them: a book with a night in it, which is mine, eleven so far this month and twenty-six by the end of it, not one interval; and a sheet with a day in it, which is the district's, five blank Wednesdays — the eighth, fifteenth and twenty-second and twenty-ninth of April and the sixth of May — all five correct, because the only person who stood in the weather on any of them is a man of about sixty at a shed eleven minutes away, and the one on the fifteenth of April correct as well on a day when about nine people in eleven streets stood in the weather for nothing at all, and the five of them are one thing and not five and he has not written that down. There are four things in five months in this district that cannot be shown to anybody and that is the fourth of them, and a blank that is right about a day it cannot see is not a fifth.** And: **a book with a night in it is one night closer to a record and a sheet with a day in it is the only instrument in this district that has never needed a reason, and I have got both of them and I am not going to put them next to each other and I do not know why and I have not written down why.** At ten past six at a counter, and she will not name it: *do not ask me what the number is for… I have written down that I have not paired one of them, eleven times… and I have not got anybody to hold any of it.* And then the batch's deepest beat, in his own book: **a head is the only repository in this city that cannot be produced against anybody** (Lena Marr, thirtieth of April, a doorway) **and a number is the one thing about four people I am allowed to have** (Monday, a counter) — **and they are the same sentence, said by two people in eleven streets who have never met… and the moment two of them are the same sentence there is a third one, and a third one is a pattern, and a pattern is me deciding that two things are the same, and I have written that down four times in this book and I am not going to be the man who decides it a fifth time in a fortnight on a Wednesday** — and: **that is the first time in seven months I have had the whole of a thing in my hands and not put it down, and nobody helped me, and a man of fifty-eight with a spade helped me and he does not know he helped me.** And the fact behind the number: **a version is a fact about a person, because only people make versions; the date is the first of April; she has not written anything on the first of April; so the number on the outside of that envelope is a record of a person deciding not to write something, and it is the only mark in that arrangement which is about a person and cannot be produced against one.** Thursday at half past seven the gang on the length, condition column empty; the sheet into the rack on Friday the eighth at ten past ten and **the second tin now holds twenty-one weeks of gang carbon and the one page that is not a gang sheet.** And at about eleven on the Friday morning, in a corridor, **Cyril Sale says the sixth true sentence anybody in this district has said to him in seven months and does not know it was the sixth: that column is the only one in this building that is allowed to be empty… the other five columns are about what we did, that one is about what it was like, and nobody in this city has ever asked a gang what a length was like** — and then: **nobody has ever asked about line five in nine weeks and a thing that has never been asked about for nine weeks is not protected, it is just not yet asked about, and the only reason it has not been asked about is that a man in another building has been asking about a box on a different form, and that is all that is holding it, and it is not holding it, it is just in the way. And you are going to write something on Monday the nineteenth, and it is going to be the best thing you have ever written, and it is not going to be a number of persons and it is not going to be a why, and I am going to read it in about four years and I am not going to be able to tell whether you refused or ran out of answers.** Field Book: the **sixth** and the **seventh**.

### Chapter 120 — What The District Is Carrying

**The close of the block, and the question standing open, and the run number does not move at all.** Thursday the twenty-first, 07.30, eleven men, **eight walks in April and six in May with an empty condition column, and fourteen since the first of April**; the rack on Friday the fifteenth and **the second tin at twenty-two weeks of gang carbon and one page that is not a gang sheet.** And the letter: **he wrote nothing on the letter. He went into the office on the Friday the fifteenth and on the Tuesday the nineteenth and did not write a figure and did not write a refusal and did not write the answer of the fourth of December, and the envelope went back a day early into the Friday post with the flap down and nothing written on the outside of it, and there is not going to be a date stamped on anything in that building in another city until some time next week, and the item is standing open as of the morning of the twenty-first, and nobody in this district was told.** Verrick Assal was wrong and Milo has written that down and has not written down whether he is glad. He wrote one line, in his own book, not on the letter: ***Twenty-first of May. The item is open. I did not answer it, and I did not answer it for a reason, and the reason is not going in the letter.*** **And a sentence with a why in it is the same shape as a seventh column, and the seventh resolution is the same as the sixth, and there are now seven of them, and the seventh is the first he has written on purpose about a thing that was going to happen.** And **at about half past four on Wednesday the twentieth, on the inside of a gate in a yard, in chalk, in his own hand, Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, eight words: *Whoever holds the pen is not the writer.*** Nobody photographed them, nobody copied them, no date against them, and **he has not told a woman of sixty-one, who has said twice that the moment a thing can be used it is in use, and he has believed her both times, and there are now three people in this city who know there is writing on that gate and he has not told two of them and he does not yet know for how long.** And the list: **thirteen lines**, one a Wednesday, one a Sunday, three of them Mondays and Thursdays, one a Friday, one every day, and no Saturday and no Tuesday in any of it, which he did not know until ten minutes before he wrote it down and is not going to say what it means — the gang and the empty column; the January return with its two queries, its unfound sixth line and **eleven weeks** of an unasked-about nil; the round **one man short and going to be one man short in June and in the fourth year, and it cannot be cured from inside this district because a district with no address cannot have a depot**; the defect form with no box for a length and a boundary with a fence at one end and a flood twice in four years; Fridays, the allocation sheet, the four in the river core, the zero against Mireward, **no second outlook in nineteen years and a man who cannot tell whether the next one does not exist or has not been sent**; Wednesdays and **seven blank Wednesdays, all seven correct, and the fifteenth of April the only one with about nine people standing in the weather for nothing**; the envelope with **four comparisons done and no fifth** and the number unchanged and not rewritten; a Sunday round and nine doors; **three pieces of paper under a counter and a sixth folded in at the back of his own book with the thirtieth of April on the fold**; the page on a bottom shelf; the brick and three things and **Tern still not found and the twenty-two days unasked and unsaid**; the levy and the woman of sixty and **I have added no reference in this fortnight and the count stands where it stood on the twenty-fifth of April**; and the written line home, **blank, and he has stopped writing down that it is there.** And the pairs: **twenty-seven**, unmoved in twenty-six days, and **an interval of one day is not a pair whether or not either of the two days has anything on it, and I have proved that twenty-six times since the twenty-sixth of April… and that is the first thing in seven months that I have wanted to make go up and could not.** And the tally: **nine lines, not looked at since the thirteenth of January, not going to be looked at in June, and there is no crossing in this fortnight.** And the order: **suspended and not discharged, not varied, not withdrawn, the public name not on the street name index, the classification of the eleventh withdrawn and not relied on, the sentence unrestorable, a desk asked twice and not answered twice, the matter standing over and not adjourned and without a date, and it was not on the business of the fifth of May and it is not on anything, and the only instrument in this city that would have put this district in front of anybody is a board about forty feet from a hearing room, and about four hundred people walk past it every day and not one of them stops.** And the three parts of the standing question, unanswered and sharper, with **half an answer to the third of them** in a ramp and a yard about a piece of paper about nine inches long with a hole in it — and **he has not put that next to a head, and has not put that next to a number, and has not put the first of those next to the second of those, and there are three of them and they are the same sentence and he knows it, and the reason he has joined none of them is that a third one is a pattern and a pattern is him deciding two things are the same, and that is the whole of what twenty-six nights in a row has been for.** And the last line: **the writer of the map is not the maintainer of anything, and that is a list of about fourteen other people and not one of them has been asked to be on it, and in twenty-six nights in a row I have not found a way to do anything else.**

### Chapter 121 — Things That Are Still True

**REPAIRED AT CR-043, in three places. (a) The blank Wednesday of the twenty-seventh of May is no longer called the fourth thing in five months: it is the eighth blank, and the fourth thing is the fifteenth of April, which is what the chapter's own Field Book and the brief both say. (b) The two register figures in the closing block now name the day they belong to — 190 and 176 are the first of June's, and they were correct before and are now unambiguous. (c) The plate decision is dated the first of June here, which is the date the volume uses everywhere.**

**The volume's shape, stated in a yard, and the machinery still printing and the district still with no instruction.** Friday the twenty-second of May and Monday the first of June. The weekly sheet for the week of the eighteenth goes into the rack at ten past ten, the duplicate into a tin, and the duplicate into a second tin on the bottom shelf of the plan room under a repair schedule, **and that tin now holds twenty-three weeks of gang carbon and one page that is not a gang sheet.** Eleven men walked the length on the eighteenth and the twenty-first, the reason column empty on both, the condition column empty on both. The allocation sheet came off the machine at nine and a man of about fifty transferred five figures out of it with a pen, the fourth column is empty, the line under *officer responsible* has never been filled in at all, and none of it has moved since the twenty-sixth of January. **Four machines in this city are still running toward documents that are not going to be printed, and none of the four has been told to stop, and being told to stop is a document, and this is the district that cannot produce documents.** The round went out at ten past eight and came in at about four, seven stops, one man short. **The second side had its eighth blank on Wednesday the twenty-seventh of May, and the third of June and the tenth of June are the ninth and the tenth, and the sheet is right about all ten, and on every one of them the only person who stood in the weather in this district was a man of about sixty at a shed at Cardew Docks.** Then the question, in a yard, at about ten past ten, unasked by anybody: *is any of it still true.* **Everything that was true in January is still true and nobody has been asked whether it is, and those two facts are the same fact, and they are why this district is where it is.** Nine things read out: the order suspended and not discharged, the name not on the index, the sentence that cannot be restored and a desk asked twice and not answered twice, the matter with no date and not on the business of the fifth of May, the levy, a woman of about thirty-four who drafted the paragraph that is stopping a woman of sixty out of her own name, a boy one man short, a cover flush since the sixth of April with no form for a fault that stopped. **Not one item has been cured and not one has got worse, and about four people have checked and every one of them checked a machine.** Nell Crale, from a doorway with a hand barrow at about ten past one: *a thing you will not name is a thing you will not go and look at, and that is the whole of what is wrong with you and it is not your handwriting.* At about half past four, at a gate, a man of forty-one on no list at all declined a job in four seconds in nine words — *a card under a brick is a thing and not a job* — and added that there is a rule in this district that a rule holds by nobody testing it and that he has got good at it in about two months and has told nobody and there is nobody to tell. **The three things under the brick are three and have been three since the twenty-first of January, and the third is a boy's blank card, and the run number has not moved since the twenty-fifth of April.** Field Book, the twenty-second and the first: eleven nights and not one interval, thirty-seven nights without a gap, the pairs still twenty-seven; the season's arithmetic; the standing question in three parts, none of them about this district, none moved, one half answered by a punch roll and a flag-man; the plate at fifty-six days; **a decision, not a refusal, not to report the plate, written down and told to nobody**; the woman of sixty-eight on nothing added zero, count at eight; a conductor of thirty in a printed rule book with a hearing that has no date; a foreman of fifty-four two months past the four months and not asked and not going to be asked; ten routes, nine crossings, no crossing, the tally unopened since the thirteenth of January.

### Chapter 122 — Two Lists And No Mark Between Them

**REPAIRED AT CR-043: this chapter's Field Book no longer gives the custody register page to the woman of sixty-one. The page in a drawer with a coat in it is an advocate of thirty-one's and the entry now says so, and adds that a reader in four years is going to take the drawer for hers. The woman of sixty-one's own papers are the three pieces of paper face down under the counter and the envelope.**

**A working day with a person in it, done well by somebody who is not the protagonist, and the volume's method in a third pair of hands.** Tuesday the second of June, the water clinic at the water steps, the third consulting room, no appointment, four times in nine weeks. **Dr Iona Sen reads across**: two departments, two documents, both printed lists, both correct, four months apart, printed by two offices about a mile apart — the water authority's *Premises at which treatment may be obtained*, which lists the clinic on seven days and not on a Sunday, and the hospital's *Sunday and holiday medical cover*, which lists it on a Sunday morning from ten to one. **If one of them were wrong she would have written to somebody in February. The authority prints from a list of premises compiled about 1839 and added to ever since, and a list of premises is not a list of what is open, it is a list of what exists, and there is no form in this city on which a doctor puts *these two printed lists say different things and I have checked both*, and the two printing offices have never heard of each other.** A list is only wrong when somebody has to act on it and the other one is in a different building.
