You have had the conversation** — and the whole of it, that he has got to be able to leave at six and cannot if there is a conversation, and a man who goes out once a year **does not want it to be a day he was talked at.** And then the cost, in his own book, on the Tuesday night, and it is the first price in seven months that is about a wage: **there are two motor vehicles a week in a district of a hundred and forty people and neither of them waits, and one of them belongs to a man who goes out once a year and will not know whether I am standing at a gate or not, and the flatbed is Friday and the lorry is Thursday and both are working days, and about nine people on a floor cover for a man who is out out of a morning they were going to have. A person with an address can be in a place on a Tuesday because being in a place is not a day off. A person without an address cannot, because the being there is a day off and the day off is somebody else's morning. It is not a hardship and it is not an injustice and it is not going to be written about, and it is the reason there are nine hundred cards a month with a nil in the reason column, and it is the reason a forecast can say there is no resident witness in a place where a man has been holding a bar for thirty-one years.** Field Book: **a ratepayer is a person at an address**, and the forecast's own ground, **and a district that is not a place cannot be frightened**; the two halves of the missing record and the refusal to join them; page home 218 and 219, token 204 and 205, the condition column empty a hundred and thirty-seven days, the reason column fifteen weeks, plate 85 days.

### Chapter 140 — No Seventh Column Before The First Of September

**Wednesday the first and Thursday the second of July, and the first block of Volume 03 closes with a thing given up on purpose and not recovered.** An ordinary Wednesday in about four hundred words, and the four hundred words are the machinery of a city that has not been told to stop: the allocation sheet off the machine, five figures transferred with a pen, **the fourth column still empty and filled in three times in nineteen years, the line under *officer responsible* never filled in at all, and the zero in the fen row a zero every week since the twenty-sixth of January and nobody has reduced it and nobody is going to.** The round did not go, because it is a Monday and Thursday round; the thirteenth blank Wednesday, and **a sheet that is right about a day for two reasons is not more right than a sheet that is right about a day for one.** Four things in five months are four, it is two and a half months since the fourth, **and a fifth thing needs somebody to be surprised, and about nine people in this district were not surprised on Wednesday when a man of fifty heard that a district of a hundred and forty people had a card with a sentence on it, and that is the correct condition of a district that has no chair.** Three nils about a place entered as nil are three, he saw all three inside four days for the first time, **and he is not going to write a fourth.** And then, **at about half past four on the Wednesday afternoon, in his own hand, on the facing page above the list, the first thing he has ever put there that is not a measurement:**

> **No seventh column before the first of September.**
>
> *This is not a resolution. The seventh column is the only thing I am good at and I get to be good at it about four times a year and every time somebody else has to hold the paper. I am stopping it for two months and I am not stopping it because I have learned anything. On the thirty-first of May I wrote at the top of an entry that the not-telling was not a rule, and on the fourth of June I chose a thing about myself that can be produced against me on purpose, and neither of those was a resolution either, and I have three of them in this book now and they are not three habits, they are three places where I put something down and did not do anything. I am writing the fourth one down at half past four on the first of July and I am not going to be able to help it and I have written that sentence before and I am writing it again because I have not written it since the fourth of June. Nobody is to be told about this line, because a line that is on a page is a thing somebody can be sent to look at, and I have been in this district seven months and I have learned that and I have not learned the other thing, and the other thing is that not being sent is not the same as not being sent.*

And the cost, nine minutes later, and it is a cost and not a virtue: **what stops on the first of September is me, and nothing else stops on the first of September.** Roy Pryce's condition column will be empty for another eight weeks; **on about the eleventh of July a man of fifty-four is eight weeks past the four months** — corrected on the page in the same entry to **ten weeks, and on the eleventh of July it will be eleven weeks, and that silence is now partly mine and I cannot get it out of my handwriting**; and on the third of July a man of about fifty will transfer a zero, **and there is no column in this city, in that room, in that building or in this book for the man who could have gone, and I am not going, and I am not going because I have put a line on a page about a thing I am good at, and that is the most self-serving sentence I have written in seven months and it is dressed up as a discipline and it is not one, and I know exactly what it is, and the knowing has not stopped me writing the line.** Thursday: the gang at half past seven, the **twenty-sixth** walk, the round out and in, **a Friday had not been and a rack day is a Friday, so the sheet was still in a drawer**. A man of about twenty-six on the length who did not look at the condition column, **which is a thing a man of twenty-six has never once done and has never been asked to say he was not doing.** The plate is eighty-seven days in the grass and is a decision and not a refusal and the asking did not make it into one. And the Field Book: the list reads twenty-eight and has not moved since the twenty-fifth of June, **and the run of fifty-two consecutive nights is over and there is no run now, there is a habit, and a habit is not a discipline and a habit is not a method and it is the only thing I have built on purpose in seven months**; ten routes, ten crossings, eleven marked, one unexplained; the page home the two hundred and twenty-first day, blank; the token cold, the two hundred and seventh day, done nothing since the seventh of December; **the whole of the standing conditions, thirty of them, on one page of a book in a coat, none of them moved, including a foreman who is eleven weeks past four months on the eleventh of July and is not going to be asked, and that is now partly mine**; the standing question in full, all three parts, with the first part carrying a hundred and forty people, nineteen children, a man of forty-four, a man of fifty-eight, a woman of sixty-one, a boy of seventeen and a foreman, **and one of them has stationery**; and the working timetable at Alder Quay, bounded in eight words again, **and the thirty-one are at home, and I have not told them, and there is no form on which I could.** And the last line of the book, of the fortnight, and of the first block of the volume: ***the writer of the map is not the maintainer of anything, and that is a list of about fourteen other people and not one of them has been asked to be on it.***

---

## Volume 03, Batch 0002 — Chapters 141–150 — *The Order Is Not A Weather Report*

### Chapter 141 — Four Grounds And None Of Them Is The Work

**Friday the third of July, in eleven streets, and the escalation of the volume arrives in a bundle with the week's papers at about half past eleven and nobody had to ask anybody for it.** **CONTINUANCE OF SURFACE AND DRAINAGE WORKS — DISTRICT SCHEDULE, SEASON 3**, both sides, issued under a section number and not a name, with the season's reference printed at the top in the size of the title. *To be completed by the person responsible for maintenance in the district and returned within seven days of issue.* Six columns: **district; stability of district; works of record; units benefited; ground of discontinuance; signature of the person responsible.** **Column five is the reason this is not a weather report** — a column about whether a district's work is to go on. A key of **four grounds** on the second side, all defensible: **(a) no maintained seam of record; (b) no resident witness; (c) nil returns; (d) works not reimbursable at the present rating.** At the foot: **a district that has not returned within the period stated will be completed by this office.** And under a rule, printed, **empty**, and not a signature: **OFFICE OF THE FIRST SURVEYOR** — **a signature block is not a man and it is not a department, it is the place on a form where a man would put his name if the form were the kind that needed one, and this form does not need one, and there is no line anywhere for the office to ask for anything, or to receive anything, or to answer anything, and there is no person in this city who can be produced behind it.** He looked at it for longer than the other five columns together, **and he has not asked anybody, and asking is the one move that would turn a desk into a man, and asking that question is the ninth thing of that shape he has done in eight months.** About nine hundred rows, read by name and not counted. **Three rows are not words.** MIREWARD: a hand-transferred nil in column two, four empty boxes, **the column is not typed and has never been typed, and the row is as wide as the others, and this is the same zero off the same sheet and not a fourth nil.** The eleven streets: **a printed name and five empty boxes, because the district has never sent one of these, it sends a works sheet instead — and that is not the same as the row being wrong, and the row is empty, and empty is the only thing this form can read.** The man of about forty-one on no list at all, from the doorway, about nine seconds: **it is empty because the eleven streets sends a works sheet and not this, and I have been in this office four times a day since the twenty-eighth of December and I have never once seen one of these go out and I never once saw one come in.** Rack day: **the second tin went to twenty-nine.** Condition column **a hundred and forty-one days** empty; reason column empty; the plate **eighty-eight days** in the grass. And the sentence, at about eleven at night, written down and not acted on: **it is not a weather report, it is a land instrument wearing a weather coat, and the whole of what the weather does on it is to be the occasion** — and then the finding, which is the volume's engine in two sentences: **a district entered as stable is a district that cannot be frightened, and a district that cannot be frightened is a district where there is nothing to insure against, and nothing to insure against is ground (d), and the key is correct, and I have got nothing against the key and I want to be careful about that, because a man who wants to be careful about a key is a man who has already decided what is on the other side of it.** Page home 222, token 208, return due **Friday the tenth of July**.

### Chapter 142 — The Best Answer Anybody Is Going To Hear

**Monday the sixth and Tuesday the seventh of July, in eleven streets. One working day with a person in it, done well by somebody who is not the protagonist.** The gang at half past seven on the Monday — the **twenty-seventh walk since the first of April**, both columns empty and both correct — and **Cyril Sale reads the district schedule out on the length, all of it, including the key and the line at the foot, in the voice he reads a sheet in.** The round out at ten past eight and in at about four, one man short since the twenty-eighth of December. **On Tuesday at about ten the foreman came in, put his hat on the counter, which nobody does, and said he was the person responsible for maintenance in the district, and asked which of the four grounds is the one that is true, and he took three of them apart himself in about forty seconds and he was right about all three.** (a) is not true: **there is a level of record, in a file, with a date on it, under my signature, and it has not moved since the fourth of December, and if the department does not know that is my business and not mine to say.** (b) is not a lie: **there are two hundred and fifty-two people in a building with no number and there is not one of them on a list; (b) does not mean nobody is here.** (c) is not true: the sheet comes Monday to Sunday, goes into the rack on the Friday, the duplicate into a tin, **and there is not one Monday in nine months that has not had a sheet in it.** (d) is a question about money and is not his. **And then the best answer anybody is going to hear in this fortnight, in about four seconds, unasked: he went into the corridor and came back with the weekly sheet for the week ending Sunday the fifth of July and put it on the counter squared. That is the state of the works.** Six columns named left to right, the way a man names things in his own office. And: **and what is the state of the length. There is nothing wrong with it.** **And there is no box on that form, or on the sheet, or in this building, or in this city, for *there is nothing wrong with it*.** The wrong shape, in eleven minutes at a counter: the form's column three is *works of record*, and the works of record for eleven streets is a sheet in a rack, a duplicate in a tin, and a carbon in a plan room in another building on a bottom shelf under a repair schedule — **and there is no column anywhere that takes a piece of paper and two copies, and the reason there is no column is that nobody has ever needed one, because the piece of paper was never supposed to be the works of record; it was supposed to be a note that a foreman had walked.** And his own shape of it: **you have printed four ways for a district to lose its works and you have not printed one way for a district to keep them.** **I have. Look at the foot of it. There is one way to keep them and it is on the Friday, and it is not printed as a way to keep them, it is printed as a way to complete them, and both of those are the same sentence with a different Friday in it.** The condition column, twice, and unasked: **I have not put a word in it since the twelfth of February… and I am not going to, and you are not going to ask me to, and if you put a question in that column then the sheet stops being a record of a length and starts being a record of a man, and a man can be asked what he wrote, and I have got eleven men and I have got a contract to the thirty-first and I am not spending any of it on that. The column is for a hole.** The return is due Friday the tenth, and he says **I will have something for Friday**, and **I know you do not need me to have something for Friday. I am going to have something for Friday.** Field Book: **the form asks a correct question and offers four wrong answers, and I have now met that twice in eight months and did not write it down the first time either**; **he read me the four grounds and took three of them apart himself, standing up, in about forty seconds, and I have written down that he was right about all three, and he has still got to fill in a box, and I have still got a fortnight in me in which I could fill in column two myself in about ninety seconds, and there is a reason on the facing page of this book and the reason is not about him, and a stop that a man wrote about himself is not a stop.** And the foreman's silence, dated forward: **on Saturday the eleventh of July a man of fifty-four will be eleven weeks and three days past four months, and a Saturday is the day a foreman of thirty-one years is on a gate and not in a building.** Page home 226, token 212, condition column 145 days, plate 92 days.

### Chapter 143 — As Far As The Third Post

**Friday the tenth of July, in Mireward, and the travel logic is the block's cost.** **The flatbed from a yard at Cobb Lock comes in on a Friday morning at about a quarter to seven and goes back on the Friday afternoon and it does not wait either way; there are two motor vehicles a week and a lorry on a Thursday morning and one tractor and no bus since about 1961.** He was at the gate at ten to seven with a bag; the driver asked him nothing; **he did not go out on the tenth in order to be in Mireward, and he has written that the second reason is the one that was true and the first one is the one he would have written in** — because the last day for the district's answer was the tenth and the only vehicle that would have carried an envelope back went back at about four without it. **The rack day happened eleven miles away and the second tin went to thirty and neither needed him and nobody telephoned.** At about half past ten he was on the far side of the bridge, **the side the file says is EXT**, and the boundary there is **the third kind in eight months and it is a column and not a place anybody walked**: every entry in the *side* column says CITY except two, the key says *external — see the drainage board*, the drainage board was abolished in about 1968, **and there is no fen commission on any letterhead in this city and has not been one since 1968** — and **the half that is maintained is the half a lorry can stand on and the half that is not maintained is the half that people walk on.** All three boundaries written down and none joined. **Three iron posts, not in the file, about ninety yards apart, set out with a chain and not a wheelbarrow; two sets of marks about ninety feet apart near the second one, two hands, the same water, neither in a file; and nineteen cuts in a post at a ford four miles away — three sets of marks and nothing in them that is one thing.** **And beside the third post, in about four feet of ground, a culvert head with a cast number in the collar, and the number is in column three of the schedule under *works of record*, and it is the only piece of paper anybody has ever made about this district except a card with a hand on it.** He held the schedule against the collar for about four minutes and **did not write it down, because a number on a collar is a thing a person can be asked for and it has just gone from a thing in the ground to a thing on a form in a bag in a coat that goes into rooms.** And: **the post is not on the schedule and it is the reason a man of fifty-eight and a woman of thirty-seven and a man of forty-four can all say where the water is going, and I am not going to stand here and decide which of them the boundary is.** He went as far as the third post and no further, and stood there about eleven minutes, and there is no reason — **and the true reason, which I am writing down because I have written the other one down twice and it is a smaller reason: a bar is about nine hundred yards that way and a bank is what a bar is for, and the man who holds it is forty-four and he is not here and he has not been asked and he is not going to be told that I stood at a post for eleven minutes because of him.** At about four the flatbed went back and the form was still in the bag. Page home 229, token 215, condition column 148 days, plate 95 days.

### Chapter 144 — A Seventh Column That Is The Same Mistake For The Ninth Time

**Saturday the eleventh of July, about four in the afternoon, in a room above a kitchen in Cobb Lock, and the failure.** Two pieces of paper on a table: **a Mireward district returns card, with two inches of ruled space at the bottom for the district's own use that has been there about forty years and that almost nobody fills in; and the four-sided schedule, whose last day for the district's answer was yesterday.** He writes the **ninth seventh column** — a heading and about a hundred and ten words, **why this district's works are not of record**, every word true — and reads it back, and names it himself in about four seconds, standing up, to nobody: **a column that wants a name and has nobody to be given one is the same thing as a column that wants a name and is refused one, and both of them are a form that wants a person.** Then he looks at the facing page, which he has not brought into Mireward and does not need to: **No seventh column before the first of September**, four lines, in his own hand, first of July, with its own cost under it. **And a stop that a man wrote about himself is not a stop.** He says that out loud in a village, on a Saturday, in July, to nobody, **and that is the truest thing he has said in eight months and it is not a resolution**, and he folds the sheet once and puts it face down in the bag, **and there are now two face-down seventh columns in one bag and a bag is not a custody.** And then **the thing he is good at, in ninety seconds, in pencil, on the back of their own schedule, eleven miles from the district it is about, on the day after the day it was due** — a true sentence for column two of the eleven streets' row, naming the seam, the level, the fourth of December, the withdrawn classification, the twice-weekly works, and the empty condition column. **And if that had gone in on the tenth, the check would not have found nil, the office would not have had a key to write down, and there would not have been a sentence in a file about a morning in December that a person in a room a mile away could ask for.** He does not send it, and the reason is on the facing page and is about him, **and a fifth column filled in by a man who is not responsible for maintenance in any district in this city means the only row on nine hundred that has a man from another district's building in it has one, and the man is me, and about four years from now a room can produce it. So there is no version of this that is not him deciding.** **And Sabra Trewin, thirty-four, nine years teaching, comes in for something ordinary and says it is still ordinary when she gets there** — she will not keep the form for a week, in one long sentence: **if the district has an address then the first thing that arrives at the address is a demand, and if a schoolroom has a form in the drawer then the schoolroom is a place, and I have got nineteen children in it and not one line of them is on any paper in this city, and the pencils are in that drawer** — and **I would rather say it once to somebody who has come from four miles away** than say no to four people. Then, on her way to the door, the cost, in one sentence: **your foreman sent something on Friday. I do not know where and he did not tell me and I did not ask… and he is a man who has said nine words to me three times in eight months and he said that one twice.** And he works it out standing up, in about nine seconds, to nobody: **a district with no rateable unit has no stationery, and a district that was never on anything has nothing to put anything in** — and the whole of the rest of it, which is the block: **I could have gone and got them out. There is a drawer in Crown Heights with a sheet in it from 1911 and a bus exists and I did not go, and I did not go on the Saturday and I did not go on the Sunday.** Page home 230, token 216, plate 96 days, and **a foreman of fifty-four was on a gate in eleven streets this morning, eleven weeks and three days past four months, and nobody asked him and he is not going to be asked.**

### Chapter 145 — Five Men And A Bar And Not One Of Them On Paper

**Tuesday the fourteenth of July, on the top track in Mireward, at a sluice belonging to a body that was abolished in about 1968, and the changed method and the recovery, both somebody else's work and neither of them a word.** **A man of forty-four holds a bar on the Tuesdays and the Fridays and is paid nothing for it out of a farm wage by the hour for ditching, and the farm pays him for the Tuesday and the Tuesday is not a ditch.** There are **five of them** on ninety feet of bank and he did not introduce the other four, **and when asked how long they have been doing it he said *longer than you have been asking*** — nine years. One of them lives at the north end; **one of them is about nineteen and cannot read the water at all and can lift.** Milo Rook watches the whole thing twice and then gets the first half of the answer in eleven words: **there are five of you.** **Then you asked it wrong.** And the changed method, without stopping work and without a word: **he walked the nineteen-year-old up and down ninety feet four times, by the arm, and then put him on the other end and went back to the bar** — and that is what it was about, **what a man does on a Tuesday in a wet week when the man who put him on that end is on a farm an hour away. He is the Tuesday.** And then one sentence, the only one that was not about water or a bar, in about four seconds: **I have been getting them ready for nine years and I have never once told them I was getting them ready, and I have never once written it down, and there is going to be a day when I am not able to come and there is going to be five of us here and not one of us will be on a piece of paper about it, and that is not a fault and it is the only thing I have got.** And then, before Milo Rook can say anything: **you are not going to thank me for that.** No. **Good. Do not thank me for that. If you thank me for that then in about four years somebody is going to ask you what I said, and then I am a man who said something, and then I am on a piece of paper, and there have been four of us named in this district in thirty-one years and three of them left inside two years and the one who is still here is the one who is not.** And the recovery, worked out on the walk back in about eleven minutes, **and it is the third best hour he has had since the first of April and it cost nothing**: **this district has got five men and a bar on it and not one of them is on any paper in this city, and there is a printed instrument about a mile away with a ground on it called *no resident witness*, and this is what a no resident witness looks like from the inside: it looks like five men who cannot be named, doing a thing on a Tuesday, for nothing, in a district that is not a place, and if it stops there is no column for the stopping and no column for the doing and no form for a chain coming off the second time. And the instrument wants a sentence about this district. The district is made of things that are not sentences.** Page home 233, token 219, condition column 152 days, plate 99 days, the walk on the Monday the thirteenth the **twenty-ninth** since the first of April with him not on it and nobody telephoning.

### Chapter 146 — Side Four, And A Row With A Sentence In It That Is Not Anybody's

**Friday the seventeenth of July, and the escalation's consequence, in a room and then in a yard.** The flatbed from Cobb Lock goes back on the Friday afternoon and does not wait; he got on at about ten to four. **The bundle was on the counter of the quay section office when he came in**, and the third side was in it and the fourth side was under it and **the fourth side was not the same paper as the third side**:

> **THE ELEVENTH STREET DISTRICT, one line, typed.**
> Stability of district: — Works of record: — Units benefited: —
> **Ground of discontinuance: (a) no maintained seam of record**
> **NOT TO BE MAINTAINED AFTER 31 AUGUST**

A reference in the same series as the number on the face-up card, and a date: **the sixteenth, a Thursday.** **The office completed it on Thursday and it is a Thursday thing and it has been a Thursday thing for about eleven years and it is not a punishment and it is not a meeting. The check is done by reading the district's own file.** And then, in about four seconds, standing up in a corridor, the whole mechanism: **the entry that would put the sixteenth of December in that file rests on a classification; the classification of the eleventh is withdrawn and is not to be relied on by any party; a record that rests on a withdrawn classification is not to be relied on by any party; so the sixteenth of December is not in the file, so the check finds nil, so the key gets written down. Nobody lied. A department that cannot record a loss it has no form for is doing the correct thing with the forms it has.** **The refusal that is correct and changed nothing, in four seconds, in a corridor:** Cyril Sale puts the pen down and does not pick it up again. **I am not signing column six, and you cannot make me… because if I sign that, I have put a sentence about the sixteenth of December into a file, and there is not one sentence about the sixteenth of December that is both true and mine. There is a true one and it is not mine. There is one that is mine and it is a form about a length of road and it is not about the sixteenth of December.** And: **I am a works clerk of forty-six with three sentences on the backs of three forms in the correspondence tray and not in a drawer, and I have stopped putting them there, and this is the same thing a different size.** And: **side four has been round twice** — it went out on the third, the office kept it, it came back out on the seventeenth with a row completed on it, **and nobody in this district was told that it had been kept.** The man of about forty-one's **eleventh** nine words, to a request: **standing in a room is standing in a file.** And the cost, itemised, uncurable, **paid by neither Lena Marr nor the woman of sixty on the second landing:** the contract runs to the thirty-first of August, **and the contract is not the city's and the men are not the city's and the foreman is not the city's**; after that date no gang, no sheet, no rack, no duplicate, no carbon in a tin, **no carbon in the second tin on the bottom shelf of the plan room under a repair schedule, and a repair schedule is a schedule and nobody has ever asked when it ends**; the condition column is a hundred and fifty-five days empty **and on the thirty-first of August it will not be empty, it will not be there, and those are two different sentences and this city has no word for the second one**; **the form says not to be maintained after the thirty-first of August, which is a MONDAY and is itself a gang day; the last walk of the season is on Monday the thirty-first of August, which is the last day of the contract; the last Thursday is the twenty-seventh; a rack day is a Friday and the last one is Friday the twenty-eighth; the last sheet covers Monday the twenty-fourth to Sunday the thirtieth; there is no sheet for the week of the thirty-first, so the last walk of the season is on no paper at all; the second tin stops at thirty-seven; and the rack is still in the corridor on Friday the fourth of September with nothing in it in it.** And: **I cannot do one single thing about it, and there is no form for that, and the reason there is no form for it is that nothing in this district is a party to anything.** At about half past six, a yard, about nine people, no coffee, thirty seconds: **side four came into this office in the bundle this afternoon… I have been two days trying to find the person who did it wrong and there is not one, and that is the worst thing that has happened in this district in eight months.** Then Roy Pryce: **I had something ready for Friday… and there is no post comes to eleven streets, because the post goes to a rateable unit and we have not got one, and the satchel is the only thing that comes in here, so it went out in the satchel on the Friday morning and it came back in the satchel on the Friday afternoon and it has been in the satchel every day since… and I would like it noted that I did not stand there on the Friday morning with it in my hand and ask anybody, because I did not think there was anywhere to ask.** And then, after about eleven seconds of nobody saying anything: **that condition column has been empty for a hundred and fifty-five days, and a man in a building a mile away has written a sentence about it, and it is the first sentence anybody has ever written about that column, and it is not mine, and I am not going to pretend it is.** And Milo Rook said nothing, and could not have, and **he thinks I did not fill in the column because of him.** Field Book: the mechanism in full; the refusal; the machinery; and the guard against the one join this block invites — **there is a card with a hand on it and six words and a reference number, and this side four has a reference number out of the same series, and both are about a district with no rateable unit, and a district is printed on both of them and the district is the whole of the connection, and the two sentences have nothing to do with each other and I do not know whose hand the first one is in.** Page home 236, token 222, condition column 155 days, plate 102 days, second tin 31.

### Chapter 147 — The Same Print Run, About A Mile Away, On A Saturday Morning

**Saturday the eighteenth of July, at a records counter open to the public from about ten, with two people waiting, and Mara Quill's half of the block.** She finds the second use **in about nine seconds and she does not go into a room**: **column two, stability of district, is the column a land schedule wants.** Because **a land schedule does not want a condition, it wants to know who is on the ground** — and column two of about nine hundred rows is a hand-transferred column that says, in the handwriting of a man with a machine, that there is nobody there, **and a thing on the ground in a district that column two says has nobody in it is a thing on the ground that nobody is on.** And the print run: **the schedule was printed in the middle of May and issued in the first week of July, and the same press run went to a land desk about a mile from where the form was drafted, which has had a schedule that says a place is unoccupied for about eight weeks while eleven streets got its copy nine days ago, and nobody in eleven streets and nobody in Mireward has been told that there are two of these.** She reads the rest out loud, standing up, in the order the print run went: **MIREWARD — 41-2, 4 to 12 inclusive — works of record — unoccupied, no rateable unit. Nine plots, held on shares, and there is a book of shares, and the book of shares is in a different building and it is the only book about this district that anybody in this city has ever opened.** And the line in the return: **no** — and then four facts and no sentence: **the last Thursday of every month I reconcile a return in that room, the last Thursday was the sixteenth, I have done it for about two years before that, and it has never come up in a conversation with anybody. That is four facts and none of them is the line, and it is deliberate and it is the last of it.** And the disagreement, **correct on both sides, set up here, not repaired here, and not going to be repaired in this volume**: *you should put the district's answer on the record* — **it is the only place in this city where an objection is a thing that exists** — against *it is the only place in this city where an objection is a thing a man with a box can ask for in about four years*, and she answers with the same sentence he used. **And he says out loud that the sentence will be produced against a woman of thirty-four who knows twenty-six names and has not written them down for four years on purpose, and against a foreman of fifty-four who is not going to be asked about December and will be asked about a file** — and that **he said that same sentence to a works clerk of forty-six on a Friday afternoon and the clerk said it back to him in four seconds and he has not improved it.** The season: **twelve. You have stopped counting. I stopped counting in May and I started dating it and dating it is a column, and a column is worse than a count.** The drawer: **not going to say anything about it, this week or in July, and that is not me being cold, and you have known that since May.** And the close: **so we are two people who have both said that and neither of us has moved and neither of us is going to… and I am not going to improve it in a records room on a Saturday with two people waiting.** **Nothing romantic happened and nothing is going to; the custody register page is untouched for the eighth consecutive block and was not mentioned in the words either of them used.** And the last exchange is the one that matters and it is work: **you did not write it down, and it is on a printed side since the middle of May, and it was on a printed side before you put a hand on it on Friday, and the fact that you put a hand on it on Friday has changed nothing at all and I want that written down somewhere, because I have watched a man put a hand on something and I have watched what it did last time.** Page home 237, token 223, plate 103 days.

### Chapter 148 — Two Inches Of Ruled Space At The Bottom Of A Card

**Sunday the nineteenth of July, an ordinary Sunday, in eleven streets, and something given up on purpose.** The round did not go; there is no gang on a Sunday; **the second side of the sheet is right about today for the same reason it is right about every other day since the eighth of April.** He walks the **hundred and forty feet and eleven inches** at the low end, from the corner end, alone, for the fourth time since the twenty-eighth of April: **the gutter peg has not moved a tenth of an inch in fourteen weeks and could not have, the figure is the figure and there is no box in this city for it, and a kettle moved it in the direction of a fence in May, correctly, and nobody is going to be told.** **The plate is a hundred and four days in the grass and nobody has reported it and four people who are not told about it.** And at about twenty past four in a front room, a Mireward returns card on a table, and the schedule **left in a bag in a room in eleven streets at about one o'clock on purpose** — and here is the whole of it, four seconds to do and forty minutes to be able to write down: **a district with no rateable unit has no correspondence address, and therefore no envelope, and therefore nobody to complete a form for it; and a man of thirty is a person, and he is not a ratepayer and is not a resident and is not responsible for maintenance in any district in this city, and none of the three facts stops him writing his own name in a space that is there for the district to use.** He writes it at about twenty-five past four: *Received 17 July. The district has no rateable unit and no correspondence address and no person responsible for maintenance. The works of record in this district are a number cast into the collar of a culvert and about nine feet of drain that a man of fifty-eight writes four lines about once a year into a pad in a drawer in Crown Heights. — M. Rook, surveyor.* And four things about it: **One. It is not the six words, and he does not know whose hand they are in, and there is no connection between six words on the seventeenth of June and a paragraph on the nineteenth of July, and he has written that down for the first time because a reader is going to join them, and joining them would be him deciding they are the same, and he has done that three times in eight months about three other things and each of the three was wrong.** **Two. It is not an instrument and not a form and not a return and not a record; it is a paragraph in two inches of ruled space that has been there about forty years and nobody has asked for it and nobody is going to.** **Three. It is the fifth of his own five and the first of the five he has paid for** — the plate (about a person, cost him not saying a thing to a woman of sixty-three), the seventh column on a table in his own office (about a person, cost a boy of seventeen nothing and a man of forty-one four seconds of not-asking), the not-telling (about a person, eleven weeks and whatever comes after), **the rule on the facing page (a habit, and it cost Roy Pryce a silence that is now partly in my handwriting)**, and this one — about a person, and the person is me. **Four. He wrote it face down and did not put it in the machine, and the reason is the only thing in the chapter that is any use:** a face-up card is re-run once a week on *re-run — face up only* and comes back with a reference number, **and a sentence with a reference on it is a thing a person can be asked for. So the hand stays on the paper. The machine does not take the hand over. And that is the first time in eight months that I have wanted a machine not to take a hand over, and I have not written down what that is, and I am not going to, and I have written down that I am not going to, which is the same shape of thing and about nine of them and every one correct.** And the close: **a district of a hundred and forty people with no rateable unit and no street number since 1911 and no envelope and no correspondence address had a man's name on a piece of paper in it for the first time in about sixty years. And the nil is still a nil. The column is still hand-transferred and still not typed and there is still a row for it and the row is as wide as the others. Nobody has been frightened. Nobody has been asked for anything. A district that is not a place cannot be frightened, and I have given a place a name, and it is the only one of the five I cannot get back. Because a card with a thing struck through it is a card with a mark on it, and the re-run is on a Friday at about four, and about four hundred of those go out of that room a week.** Page home 238, token 224, plate 104 days, condition column 157 days.

### Chapter 149 — No Defect Observed, In Pencil, By A Man Of About Twenty-Six

**Monday the twentieth of July, in eleven streets, and the fortnight's end, and a working day with a person in it, done well.** The gang on the back length at half past seven, eleven men and a foreman with a clipboard, the **thirty-first walk since the first of April**. **The condition column has four words and a figure in it for the second time in a hundred and fifty-eight days.** **Sixty yards, nothing** — written by the foreman himself, in pencil, in the hand he has used on that sheet for thirty-one years: **Mon 20/7. 60 yds. No defect observed.** The man of about twenty-six, unprompted, about four feet away: **I have walked this length for about four years and I have never once had anything to write in it and I did not know that was unusual.** **It is not unusual. It is what it is for.** And then the shape of it: **a column that wants to know what a length was like has been used for a hundred and fifty-eight days to record a hole, and a hole is an event, and an event is something that happened to a gang, and a gang can be asked whether an event happened. A state is not an event. A state is a thing that was true on a Monday morning, and a man who has written *no defect observed* on a sheet has written something that is true at four o'clock on Monday and is not thereby a fault and not thereby a clearance and not thereby anything anybody can be asked about, and the whole of the difference between the two is the four words.** Cyril Sale, at about ten past eight, in about nine words and not twice: **that column is the only one in this building that is allowed to be empty… and it has now been asked about twice in eight months by two men who are not me, and it is answered twice, and it is on a sheet for the first time in five months and a half, and it is seventeen weeks late for a district that is not going to be maintained after the thirty-first of August.** Nobody said anything to that. The round out at ten past eight and in at about four, one man short. **And the work, done well, not asked for, and not thanked for:** at about half past eight the man of about twenty-six sat down on the low wall at the north end of the length with the duplicate carbon and a hard pencil, **because a foreman's pencil comes through onto the copy underneath as a grey smear, and it has been a grey smear on every sheet in this district since the second of January, and nobody has ever minded, because there has never been anything in the condition column to mind about** — and copied the four words and the figure onto the carbon, letter for letter, in about four minutes, so that the sentence would be legible on the copy. **Nobody asked him to. Nobody thanked him. Milo Rook did not thank him either, because a person who has thanked somebody is a person who has asked, and a conductor of thirty-eight said that in April about a card, and it is the best sentence anybody in this city has said about paperwork and it is a conductor's and it is not a surveyor's and it is not improved here.** In the afternoon he goes nowhere; **a rack day is a Friday and there is no rack day in this week until tomorrow.** **Atlas residue, once in ten chapters, six words, in his own book at about eleven at night, given to nobody and to be given to nobody: *A printed ground holds nobody up.*** And it is not handed to a woman of sixty-one at a counter, and not said in a yard, and there are about nine people in this district and none of them has it, **and there is a woman of thirty-four in a village who would understand it and he is not going to tell her either, and there is a man of fifty-four on a gate who would argue with it.** Page home 239, token 225, condition column 158 days, **and the plate is a hundred and five days in the grass today and it is also the page home on the sixteenth of June, and those are two different registers and a reader is going to try to make them one.**

### Chapter 150 — The Standing Question In Three Parts, With A Printed Instrument In The First Of Them

**Monday the twentieth of July, 23.50, and the block ends on a week and not on a cliff.** The pairs: **the list reads twenty-nine, it has moved once since the twenty-fifth of June, the interval is seven days from the ninth to the sixteenth, and the last time it moved the interval was also seven days — and two sevens in about a fortnight is a coincidence and I have not written anything about it.** There is no entry for the tenth, the eleventh, the twelfth, the thirteenth, the fourteenth or the fifteenth, and there are six nights in that: **the tenth of July was a decision — I had the form in the bag and I took it out and read it and put it back and did not open the book, and I have written that down three times in three days and I cannot now remember whether I decided not to or simply did not, and a decision and not doing are the same size when you have to write them down afterwards** — **and the other five were not a decision at all.** The list is **two sixes, seven threes, one nine, four twos, three fives, one eight, one twenty-five, four sevens, six fours**, and **must not be read as twenty-one, twenty-six, twenty-eight, thirty or thirty-one; there is no thirtieth pair, no eleventh pair, no thirteenth pair, no twenty-first pair, no twenty-second pair and no twenty-eight pair; the four threes that were here in December are not collapsed; there is one eight and not two; and the run of fifty-two consecutive nights is over, there is a habit, and two sevens in a fortnight has not turned the habit into a run and I have checked twice.** Then **the standing question in three parts, written out whole because the first part has a printed instrument in it for the first time in eight months and pretending otherwise would be the ninth thing of that shape.** **(a)** the law of the place, in a working man's mouth, and then the whole engine: **a district entered as stable is a district that cannot be frightened, a district that cannot be frightened is a district where there is nothing to insure against, a district where there is nothing to insure against is a district whose works are not reimbursable at the present rating, and that is one of the four grounds, and the key is correct, and I have got nothing against the key and I want to be careful about wanting to**; the eleven streets has five empty boxes; **the sixteenth of December is not in the file because the classification of the eleventh is withdrawn and is not to be relied on by any party; so the check finds nil; so a gang of eleven men and a foreman stop on the last morning of a contract that ends on the thirty-first, and the thirty-first is a MONDAY, and the gang is on a Monday and a Thursday, and nobody in the office that printed the form looked at a calendar, because A PRINTED DATE DOES NOT KNOW WHAT DAY OF THE WEEK IT IS, and that is the whole of the discovery, and the discovery is worse and not better than the one he was expecting: the last morning of the gang is the last day of the contract, and there is no sheet for the week of the thirty-first, and so the last walk of the season is not on anything, and nobody in this district had done that sum until Friday afternoon. And nobody has done anything wrong anywhere in this, and that is the sentence he has been trying to get round for two days and cannot get round.** **(b)** **there is no second outlook** — unmoved, twenty-six days, **the only part of anything in this book that has not moved at all** — **and I am not going to find out in the last week of a month by asking a man a question.** **(c)** **six instruments that are not one** — a punch roll, a flag and a book of times, a field book, a pad in a drawer nobody has rung up about since 1969, a roll of nineteen children in a woman's head, **and a smell in a dry week, which is the strongest of the six and the one thing a person produces and cannot produce on demand, and there is no form for it and I am not going to build one, and a route that cannot be produced cannot be asked after** — **half an answer is not an answer, and there is a fourth part under the third that I have never once been down to, and it is the fifth time I have written that sentence and improved it none of the five.** Then the **five of his own, not resolutions**: the plate, the table, the not-telling, **the rule on the facing page, which is the only one of the five that is about a habit and the only one that cost somebody else something, and a stop like that is not a stop**, and the fifth, **a paragraph and a signature in two inches of ruled space, about a person, and the person is me, and it cost four seconds, and nobody asked me to — and the first four cost somebody else something and the fifth cost me a name on a piece of paper and I cannot get it off.** Then **the conditions, thirty-one of them and about fourteen people and not one of them has been asked to be on it**, including the new one and the fact that **nothing that has happened this month has given the matter a chair**; the ledger; **a sound and a taste, no face, no place, no person, no name, and it did not come in nineteen days and I have not written down whether that is a thing that has stopped or a thing that has not come**; **no crossing, and Chapter 136 says he could have and wrote down that he could have**; and **the ninth's failure to cost one is still unexplained and he is holding to the promise he made on the twenty-fifth of June, and a promise about the end of a month is the only date in this book he has written down twice.** Then **the lines that are not joined**, itemised, with the new ones named: the two sets of marks near the second post, the nineteen cuts in a post at a ford, the cast number on a culvert collar, **and the nine words in a hand on a card** — **a fourth is not permitted to become a fourth pattern**; and the line in Mara Quill's return recorded as a fact and not a sentence; and the timetables hold; and the ford unresolved and the Rainway not named and no Weather remnant identified and the office of the First Surveyor a printed box with a rule and nothing on the rule. And the close, which is a week: **the walk on Thursday the twenty-third, the rack day on Friday the twenty-fourth, the second tin to thirty-two, and the last walk of the season on Monday the thirty-first of August, which is the last day of the contract and is itself a gang day, and the last rack day Friday the twenty-eighth, and the last sheet covers Monday the twenty-fourth to Sunday the thirtieth, and there is no sheet at all for the week of the thirty-first, and the second tin stops at thirty-seven, and nobody empties a rack** — and the four things in this entry that are not going to be fixed, and **the fourth is me.** Page home 239, token 225.

### Chapter 151 — A Season, Counted Down In A Building Where Nobody Says The Number

**Tuesday the twenty-first of July to Monday the twenty-seventh, and the only thing this district has for August is a count.** The second tin on the bottom shelf of the plan room went to **thirty-two on Friday the twenty-fourth of July** and nobody telephoned about it, and the man of thirty did not ask the man of about fifty whether the number was right, because the number is not a thing anybody in this city has ever asked about; he heard it Monday the twenty-seventh from **the man of about forty-one on no list at all**, in a corridor, with a handcart, who was not sent. In four minutes, standing up, on the back of a sheet of his own paper: **from the twenty-first of July to the thirty-first of August there are twelve Mondays and Thursdays, the first is Thursday the twenty-third, the last is Monday the thirty-first of August, which is the last day of the contract and is itself a gang day; and there are six Fridays between them, and on the seventh Friday after that there is a rack with nothing in it in it.** He wrote down **twelve** and **six** and looked at them for a minute and a half, and the finding is the block's: **there is no column anywhere in this district that wants either of those numbers, and a district is entered as stable or not stable and a week is walked or not walked, and a form about a district with six columns and four of them about money cannot be made to want a week.** Wednesday the twenty-second: the second side of the sheet is right about the day, and **the only person who stood in the weather in eleven streets on Wednesday the twenty-second was a man of about sixty at a shed at Cardew Docks, and a district entered as nil is the second reason, and nobody is counting them.** The plate is a hundred and seven days old. **Thursday the twenty-third, half past eight, a bell went once from past the end of the back length, and he wrote the time and the word *bell* and did not write down what it was, and he told nobody, and that is the ninth thing of that shape in eight months and the first one that has cost nothing.** At half past four on the low wall he wanted a seventh column that would take a sound, **had it in his hand in about eleven seconds, and did not draw it**, and wrote in the margin of the page that carries the rule: *I want one. That is the whole of it… **I am not going to wait for the first of September either and I want that written down in the same hand as the rule, because the rule is about a month and what I am actually doing is about me.*** Field Book, Thursday the twenty-third, 23.20: the pairs are twenty-nine and have not moved and the reason is that he has entries on most nights and an interval of one day is not a pair; **the condition column has two entries in a hundred and sixty-one days and both are four words and a figure; the second tin is at thirty-one and does not go to thirty-two until tomorrow.** Page home 242, token 228, plate 108.

### Chapter 152 — Four Copies Of A Bell

**Friday the twenty-fourth of July, Mireward, and the first time anybody has told Milo Rook what four bells are.** The flatbed comes in from the city at about a quarter to seven, is in Mireward at about half past ten with the week's post and the sacks, and goes back on the Friday afternoon and is in the yard at about half past four; **two motor vehicles a week, one tractor, and no bus since about 1961, and he got off where the driver was going and got back on at ten to four.** **Barnaby Pike, sixty-one, has dug in this district for about thirty-one years, is not on a rate roll because a ratepayer is a person at an address and there is no address, has no employer because the families pay him in coin out of a jar on a table, and no wage, no record, no holiday and no name on anything in this city.** He rang a handbell on a nail inside a house with no number, **twice before he started and once when he stopped, and he dug with his right hand while he rang it with his left and did not stop digging to ring it.** Milo Rook stood forty feet away for two hours and a quarter and said nothing, because there was nothing to say to a man who is working. **At about half past one, for about four seconds, the sound went, the way it has gone twice in eight months, and a taste came with it that has no face, no place, no person and no name, and it acquired nothing, and it did not become the bell.** Then the account, four hundred words, told while he dug, and none of it a document: **a death here is registered in a town eleven miles away — somebody signs it, there is a man with a book and a date in it and he is not wrong and Pike has never once said he was wrong — and what there is not, in any book in this city, is a row, because there is no district to have a row in, so the paper goes on a wall and about two years later the damp gets it and what is left of that person is that about four people were in the room.** And the practice: **the bell goes the day somebody dies, and again the following year on the same date, and the year after that, and it has gone every year since, and nobody arranged it and there is no meeting and there was no first one anybody can point at; Pike opens the hole up, puts it right, does the grass, takes the weed off it, and goes; he will not say for whom, because if he says a name then in about four years somebody can ask him which date goes with it, and the date is the whole of it, and there are about nineteen years of dates in his head and no piece of paper and he has never been asked, and those two things are not a coincidence.** The number, refused and then given in four words: **I do not know the number either. I know the people I have seen. I have got an idea. An idea is not a number.** And the sentence the whole block turns on, unasked: **whatever you write down will be the first time anybody has written down any of it, and that is not a service, that is a door.** Field Book: **four bells in four places and that is four things and not one thing, and there is no word in this city for what the four of them have in common, and the word is not *district* and it is not *route*; if somebody in this district starts writing down the four then the four stop being four people doing a thing and become a row, and a row is a document, and a document is the door he has just told me about in about four seconds without being asked**; and **a lost sound and a bell that rings are two different things and I am not going to write them in the same column**; and **nineteen years in a man's head and nineteen cuts in a post at a ford are two different numbers and I have written them down next to each other on purpose so that a reader of this book can see me not joining them.** Page home 243, token 229, plate 109, condition 162, second tin 32.

### Chapter 153 — Seventeen On A Friday, And A Bell With A Rope On It

**Friday the thirty-first of July, Cobb Lock, about four miles walked from the flatbed.** The schoolroom has a bell on a bracket over the door, **hand bell, screwed to a frame from about 1911, the year the village was renumbered for a drain, and the numbers went on a sheet in a drawer in Crown Heights and nobody has ever asked for the sheet — a school is a rateable thing, a rateable thing needs a street, and this schoolroom has never had a street and has had a bell for the whole of that time, and the bell is the only piece of apparatus in this district that somebody required to exist and did not pay for.** **Seventeen children, and Sabra Trewin, thirty-four, has taught in that room for nine years, and the roll is nineteen on a Tuesday and twenty-one on a Monday and seventeen on a Friday, and she has never worked out why and said in June that she is not going to.** He stood at the back for an hour and a quarter and was sent out at ten past twelve. **For about nine years, when somebody in that room has had somebody die, she has rung it. Once. Not the next year. There is no other rule, it has never been written down, there is nowhere to write it down, and two people have asked her this year whether she would put it in a book and she said no both times.** The count is nothing: **three numbers in four miles and not one practice, and a practice with a number at the end of it is a rule, and a rule can be broken by a person.** And the count in her room: **four children take turns on the rope, the rule is not written anywhere, she has watched them give it nine and seven and eleven in one term, she has never once corrected one of them, she could not tell you what the right one is, and she will not flatten out four old women who are dead because they do not know that she is entitled to.** And then she gave it because she had decided to, not because he asked: **there is a child in this room whose mother's mother died at the end of May, she has the date, she told me on the Thursday and does not know what it is for, and I have not written it down** — **I know it. I have got it since the Thursday. That is the point and you have not got there yet. I have got a date in my head the same as the man with the spade, and the only difference is that I have also got seventeen names and twenty-one names and a Monday number, and if anybody ever asks me for any of it I can produce all of it, in order, today, from here. And that is why I am not going to let you have the date. You are a man who writes things down.** At half past two the bell went once with a pull from a child and nobody counted it aloud; **he had the number in about two seconds, it was eight, he did not write it down, and he has written that he did not write it down, and a man who writes down that he did not write a number down has still had the number.** Field Book: **the category, named on the page and worth nothing — Weather with Cadence leaking. Weather is collective feeling and season and there is a season in that room. Cadence is sequence and timing and a bell with a rope on it is the plainest timing instrument in this district and it is doing a job it was not made for: it means *the day is starting* and *the day is finishing*, and for four years it has been meaning *a year ago today, and there is no row for it anywhere, and I cannot tell you how many times to pull it*.** And: **I have got three bells in four miles and I have been asked by a woman of thirty-four not to put them in the same column, and she is right, and the reason she is right is that a number at the end of a practice turns it into a rule, and a rule can be broken by a person. Four people doing a thing is four people doing a thing. It is not a form. The last thing that has to happen in this district is somebody writing down the four.** Page home 250, token 236, plate 116, condition 169, second tin 33.

### Chapter 154 — Eleven Yards Of Grass, And A Man With A Mower On The Wrong Side

**Friday the seventh of August, the city side, then across.** The second tin went to **thirty-four**; the page home is 257, the token 243, the plate 123, the condition column 176; a foreman of fifty-four is fifteen weeks and two days past four months. **The man of about fifty with the mower has cut the city half for nine years and has never been over, and in June said without being asked that there is a line in the middle of the deck that is not painted, it is just where the mowing stops, and there is a different grass on the far half because there was a man used to cut that side and he stopped.** On the seventh he stopped the machine **about four feet short of the line, and shut it off, and stood there for half a minute.** His reason, unasked for: **about three hundred yards of path on the far side that eleven men and a foreman walk twice a week, and a machine of about two hundred and forty pounds, and he is not putting it over a bridge with a rail on one side and a slope on the other and a load calculation in a drawer from about 1974 with the low end not re-checked.** And the cost of telling, which is the point: **he has cut the far half with a sickle, nine years, about ten minutes a morning before he starts on this side, it is not on his time sheet, nobody pays him for the ten minutes, he has never told anybody, and he is telling you because you have been standing on this deck since the quarter past eleven and you are the first person in nine years to stand on the far side of it, and if I do not say it now then in about four years somebody will find out that I have been over here and think I did it for a reason.** Milo Rook crossed in about four seconds, stood on the far half, **and did not go along the path and did not go past the second post.** The three posts, iron, four feet out, set out with a chain; **the two sets of marks about ninety feet apart near the second one, two hands, the same water on the same days, none of them in a file** — **he was about eleven feet from them and could have walked ten yards and did not, and not going was not a decision, and that is the fourth time in two months he has not gone and looked at a thing he could have looked at.** Beside the third post, **a culvert head with a number cast into the collar, and he read it out loud, standing up, in about four seconds, to nobody, and did not write it down** — the first of the ten things of that shape in eight months that was neither producing it nor writing it down, and **a thing said out loud to nobody in a fenland is not a record, and the only reason that is true is that fenlands are large and there is nobody in them.** He stood at the third post for about four minutes and did not go on. **Nobody has ever gone past the third post. Nobody is going to. That is not a rule and it is not a resolution and it is not on the facing page, and the line is the line because two people agreed in about 1974 to stop maintaining one half of a bridge, and there have not been two people out here since about 1968.** He crossed back at twenty past twelve. Neither of them said anything and he did not thank him and the man did not expect it. The note at the foot: **the pairs are twenty-nine, the tally is ten lines and I have not looked at it, the ninth is unexplained, the end of July was three days ago, and I am going to write on the thirty-first of this month that I am not going to explain it, which is worse than breaking it and is a third kind of keeping it.**

### Chapter 155 — The Fifth Column, And A Machine In A Room About A Mile Away

**Wednesday the twelfth of August, and the block's failure, and it is not the seventh column.** The allocation sheet comes off the machine on a Wednesday at about nine: five rows, four columns, a line under *officer responsible* that has never been filled in, a fourth column filled three times in nineteen years, and a zero in the fen row that is a zero and has been since the twenty-sixth of January. Page home 262, token 248, plate 128, condition 181, second tin 34, **a foreman of fifty-four sixteen weeks past four months, exactly.** He stood in that room for two hours and a quarter with a **fifth column** in a book in a coat and did not ask for it. A fifth column across the top would take a name; the hand would go in the way it went in the reason column on the seventeenth of June. **They do not set a machine for one district, which he did not know until the man of about fifty said it, and did not ask, and was told anyway in about nine words while transferring the fifth figure: a column is set once and it stays, it is set for nine hundred and not for one, and it is not set again in August, because in about four years somebody will be going to want to know the day a card was issued and the day is a column and you cannot take a column out without taking the day out.** And: **I do not know what you would want to put in it, and I have not asked, and I have had a machine that prints a card in this room for nineteen years and I have never once seen a hand in the reason column before the seventeenth of June and I have not asked about that either.** Milo Rook said *thank you* and was told, in effect, not to: **you have said that to about four people and every one of them is a person who had told you something for nothing, and I am the fifth, and I have not told you anything.** Then the one question of the morning, **a lie built in about half a second**: *are you the one that wants a column.* **No.** **I did not think you were. I have been in this room nineteen years and I have never been the one anybody wanted, and I have never been able to work out whether that is because nobody wants anything from me or because there is nothing here anybody could want.** The cost, written out that night: **there is a fifth column in this city that would take a name for a district with no rateable unit, and there is a machine that would print it, and the machine does not care which district it is for, and I did not ask. And I did not ask because a man with a spade said four words to me on a Friday: whatever you write down will be the first time anybody has written down any of it, and that is not a service, that is a door. A man of sixty-one has been paid in coin for thirty-one years and has no employer and no wage and no name on anything, and if a family wanted this week to give him something for thirty-one years there is no form in this city on which to enter it and no employer to enter it against. He cannot be asked, so he cannot be claimed. He cannot be claimed, so he cannot be paid, and he cannot be paid, so nothing has ever been wrong, and the reason nothing has ever been wrong is that nothing has ever been about him.** And then the tenth thing of that shape, six words, given to nobody: **NOTHING WRITTEN DOWN CAN BE GOT WRONG** — *there are nine people in this district and none of them has it, and I am not going to say it in a yard, or to a man of about fifty who has been in a room for nineteen years and who put a pen down for me, or to a woman of thirty-four who would tell me the reason it is a lie in about four seconds and who would be right.* And the facing page: **the rule is twenty days off and I wrote on Thursday that I was not going to wait for the first of September, and the reason I meant it is that the rule is about a month and the thing is about me, and a rule that is about a month can be waited out and a thing that is about me cannot.** Wednesday the twelfth is right about the second side of the sheet for the same reason it is right about every one since the eighth of April, **and he is not counting them.**

### Chapter 156 — A Bell At A Landing, And A Man Who Is Not Going To Be There Tomorrow

**Friday the fourteenth of August, the second flatbed of the block, and the block's recovery, which takes about two seconds and is not his.** The second tin went to **thirty-five**; page home 264, token 250, plate 130, condition 183, **and none of those five numbers is on any paper he can reach and none of them is about anything that is happening today.** **Garn Ollerton, thirty-seven, twenty-six years, four landings, no piece of paper on any of them**, sat on the low step at the top landing and counted his Fridays out loud: **the Tuesday lorry goes to a yard at the north end and does not go to a landing, the Friday one comes in at half past ten and goes back at four, and you have been out on four of them since the end of June, and there is no bus and there has not been one since about 1961.** He said the three he had — the top track, the schoolroom, the nine he could not put a ring on — **and he had it ready before he got to the step, and has written down that a man who has a sentence ready before he arrives somewhere has already decided that somewhere is where the sentence goes.** And then, in about two seconds, unasked and not thanked: **Then that is four. I am not going to tell you where the other two are and I am not going to tell you which of the three you have got wrong, and I have said four and that is one more than I have said to anybody in twenty-six years and I said it because you said the nine and I have never once in my life wanted somebody else to know how many times something was rung.** Then the bell on the wall: **about four inches across the mouth and it came off a boat, and it is for the first one of the day and the last one off and a boat coming in the dark, and it has been all three of those since before I was doing this, and I have never once written any of it down, and there is no form for it and there is not going to be one. Every day. At ten past seven and at four in the afternoon, and there are days when I am not on this water at either of those and I do it anyway, and on Sundays, and I have done it every day since about 1979 and I have not missed one. If I do not do it, it does not get done. And if it gets done, it is because somebody is here. And I would rather it was because a bell went off on a wall. You are going to tell me that is a mistake. You are going to tell me a thing that happens every day at the same hour is a timetable, and you are right about it and you can have it, and I have got twenty-six years and about nine people who use this landing and a route that cannot be produced cannot be asked after, and I have decided which of those three things I would rather have, and it is not the one you would pick.** He said nothing, twice now, and she noticed. And the finding he wrote down and did not tell her and is not going to: **a thing that happens every day at the same hour is a timetable, and a timetable in this city is a document with a column on it, and the moment a bell at a landing is rung every day at ten past seven it stops being a thing four people do and becomes a service, and a service is a thing somebody is responsible for, and the moment anybody in this district is responsible for it there is a form with a signature on it, and the four people stop.** He got back on the flatbed at ten to four, **and there are four bells, and he is the only person in this city who knows there are four, and he has not written the four down, and he has not told anybody, and there are four people who have not been asked whether it is four.**

### Chapter 157 — A Name Off A Contract, In A Box, Typed, On A Thursday

**Thursday the twentieth of August, and the block's escalation, and it is a document, and nobody lied.** An office about a mile away returned side four of a district schedule with something in column six and **nobody in eleven streets was told until the Monday.** He read it in a corridor four rooms above a fitter's shop in about four seconds and went and did the round. The block: > *Ground of discontinuance: (a) no maintained seam of record* / > *NOT TO BE MAINTAINED AFTER 31 AUGUST* / > *Signature of the person responsible: R. PRYCE*. **The date under it is the twentieth of August, which was a Thursday, and they complete them on the Thursday, and it has been a Thursday thing for about eleven years and it is not a punishment and it is not a meeting.** The mechanism, in about nine minutes at a counter, **with nobody lying and for the second time in eight months no one to be angry at, and it is worse than the first: a district with no rateable unit has no correspondence address, so it cannot be sent anything and cannot be asked anything, and it has been exactly that since 1911, so the office did the only thing available to it and went to the document it could reach — and a contract for surface and drainage works names the supervisor, because a contract has to be able to say who is in charge of a gang on a Tuesday.** So the office took the name off the contract and typed it into a box, **and in about four years a room can produce that sheet, and the room will produce a page with a foreman's name in the column that says he signed it, and he did not sign it, and there is nothing in this city that can make a room give a typed name back to the man it belongs to.** Monday the twenty-fourth of August, half past seven, eleven men and a foreman, **the forty-first walk since the first of April**, both columns empty and both correct. Page home 274, token 260, plate 140, condition 193, **second tin thirty-six and the last rack day was Friday the twenty-one**; a foreman of fifty-four is seventeen weeks and five days past four months. At half past six, about nine people in a yard, no coffee: **it is a notice that the works on the back length are discontinued after the thirty-first of August and it has got my name in a box on the last line and I did not sign it and I have not seen it and nobody asked me. And I am not going to stand here and be the man who complains about it. Because if I say I did not agree to it then it is a disagreement. And a disagreement wants two. And there is one. And there is no second one, and there is not going to be a second one, because the district has got no address and the office could not write to us and it went to the contract instead, and I am on the contract, and that is the whole of how I got into a box on a Thursday. And if I stand in this yard and say it is wrong, then in about four years there is a piece of paper in a file with a man in a yard on it saying the eleventh street district disputes the discontinuance, and there is no district, and I will have built the first piece of paper there has ever been about this place, and it will be about me, and it will be wrong, because the works are going to stop on the thirty-first whatever I say.** And then: **I would like it noticed that I have written two sentences in a condition column since February and one of them is four words and one of them is four words and a figure, and I have done that because a length is a length and a hole is a hole, and I have not put one word in that column about a thing that is not a length, and I am not going to start on Monday.** One question, in about four seconds: **did you put my name on it.** No. **Right.** And Milo Rook stood in a yard and could not say one word and could not have said one word and the sentence he did not say is the whole of the block and he has written it out in his own book and it is seventy-eight words long and it is not *no*.** At ten past seven, at a gate in a high wall, **the man of about forty-one on no list at all**, nine words, and: **I have never been able to work out why it is that a row of a district is a document and one man in one box is a different kind of thing, and I have been at this gate four times a day for two hundred days and I have not been sent, and I would rather somebody in a good coat who writes things down worked that out than me.** And the note at the foot: **a name got into a piece of paper about this district in July and it was his, and a name got into a piece of paper about this district in August and it is not his, and he did not do it, and he cannot get it off either, and the only difference between the two is that he can see his own.** And: **the end of July was four days ago and I did not do it and I am going to break the promise about the ninth crossing on the last day of this month, in writing, which is the only tool I have and the worst possible one.**

### Chapter 158 — The Last Thursday Of A Month, And A Cell With Nothing To Go In It

**POV: Mara Quill, thirty-one.
